CV 97-0322 DAE 


29a 


Appeals from the United States District Court 
for the District of Hawaii 
David A. Ezra, District Judge, Presiding 


Argued and Submitted 
January 16, 1998 - San Francisco, California 


Filed March 27, 1998 


Before: Charles Wiggins, John T. Noonan, and 
A. Wallace Tashima, Circuit Judges. 


OPINION 
TASHIMA, Circuit Judge: 


This case presents a constitutional challenge to a vote 
put to the citizens of Hawaii on November 5, 1996. Plain- 
tiffs ask us to invalidate the vote on the ground that the 
Hawaii Supreme Court’s interpretation of blank ballots 
was so unforeseeable that the voters’ substantive due 
process and free speech rights were violated. We reject 
this constitutional challenge and uphold the Hawaii vote. 


3 


The vote at issue was on a question asking Hawaii 
voters whether they wished to hold a convention that 
would propose amendments to the Hawaii state constitu- 
tion. The constitutional convention question was one of 
several unrelated items on the general ballot, including 
ratification votes on three proposed state constitutional 
amendments. 


By way of background, amendments to the Hawaii 
constitution can be proposed either by the legislature or 
by a convention. Haw. Const. art. XVII, § 1. The Hawaii 
Constitution states: 


30a 


The legislature may submit to the electorate at 
any general or special election the question, 
“Shall there be a convention to propose a revi- 
sion of or amendments to the constitution?” If 
any nine-year period shall elapse during which 
the question shall not have been submitted, the 
lieutenant governor shall certify the question, to 
be voted on at the first general election follow- 
ing the expiration of such period. 


Haw. Const. art. XVII, § 2. Amendments proposed by the 
convention must then be ratified by the people. Id. Pur- 
suant to Haw. Const. art. XVII, § 2, voters were asked in 
1966, 1976, 1986 and 1996 whether they wished to hold a 
constitutional convention. 


Prior to 1996, machine-counted ballots that left the 
convention question blank were simply ignored in com- 
puting whether the convention question had passed. 
“Yes” votes were counted against “no” votes, and the 
majority ruled. : 


In preparation for the 1996 vote, the Office of Elec- 
tions distributed an “information sheet” that was present 
at every polling place. The sheet explained the signifi- 
cance of a “yes” vote and a “no” vote, but was silent on 
what happened to blank ballots. Additionally, a few days 
before the election, the Office of Elections printed a “fact 
sheet” that said that a majority on the convention ques- 
tion would be determined without considering blank bal- 
lots. This fact sheet was available at the Office of 
Elections to be picked up, and some voters did so. The 
fact sheet was also available on the internet, although 
only a small number of “hits” occurred at this internet 
site. 


3la 


At some point before the 1996 vote — it is unclear 
exactly when — the Office of Elections told Citizens for a 
Coiistitutional Convention (“Citizens”) that blank ballots 
would not be counted on the convention question. Citi- 
zens is an organization that lobbied Hawaii voters in 
favor of the convention question, and it claims to have 
changed its campaign strategy in reliance on the Office of 
Elections’ instruction. 


At the November 5 election, the electorate voted (or 
failed to vote) on the constitutional convention question, 
as follows: 163,869 voters marked “yes”; 160,153 marked 
“no”; 45,245 left the question blank; and 90 marked both 
“yes” and “no.” Thus, if blank ballots are counted, they 
control the outcome of the vote. 


Shortly after the election, the Hawaii State AFL-CIO! 
sued Dwayne D. Yoshina, the Chief Elections Officer of 
Hawaii, in an original action in the Hawaii Supreme 
Court. The AFL-CIO sought a declaratory judgmer:t that 
the constitutional convention question had failed and an 
order directing Yoshina to certify that it had failed. The 
Hawaii Supreme Court ruled in favor of the AFL-CIO. 
Hawai'i State AFL-CIO v. Yoshina, 935 P.2d 89, 98 (Haw. 
1997) (“Yoshina I”). 


The court noted that Haw. Const. art. XVIL, § 2, 
required a constitutional convention only if the question 


' The Hawaii State AFL-CIO was joined by 51 individually 
named plaintiffs in the state suit, but for convenience we refer 
only to the AFL-CIO. 


* As in this case, several other defendants were also named. 
For convenience, we refer to them collectively as “Yoshina.” 


32a 


received a majority of the “ballots cast upon such a aues- 
tion.” (Emphasis added.) Once a convention is convened, 
however, and amendments are proposed to the voters, 
the amendments pass if they receive “a majority of the 
votes tallied upon the question.” Haw. Const. art. XVII, § 2 
(emphasis added). The court noted the difference in lan- 
guage and pointed to a committee report from the 1950 
constitutional convention that indicated that the differ- 
ence in language had been intentional. Yoshina I, 935 P.2d 
at 95. Therefore, the court concluded that “ballots cast” 
included all submitted ballots that contained the ques- 
tion, while “votes tallied” meant only the “yes” or “no” 
votes (i.e., blank ballots operated against the convention 
question). Id. at 96. Because in the 1996 convention vote, 
the “yes” votes did not constitute a majority of the total 
“ballots cast” (as the Hawaii Supreme Court understood 
that term), the court ordered Yoshina to certify that the 
question had failed. Id. at 98. 


Three weeks after the Hawaii Supreme Court's rul- 
ing, Mark J. Bennett, Citizens for a Constitutional Con- 
vention, Let the People Decide, and several individual 
plaintiffs commenced this action. Some of the individuals 
alleged that they had abstained on the convention ques- 
tion, but that if they had known that not voting had the 
same effect as voting “no,” they would have voted differ- 
ently. The other individuals voted “yes,” and the institu- 
tional plaintiffs alleged that their members mostly voted 
“yes” on the convention question. Plaintiffs (collectively, 
“Bennett”) argued that the Hawaii Supreme Court's deci- 
sion in Yoshina I was an unforeseeable departure from 
past election practices and was unconstitutional, primar- 
ily for lack of notice. 


33a 


The Hawaii State AFL-CIO and 49 individually 
named plaintiffs (collectively “applicants” or “AFL-CIO”) 
filed a motion to intervene in the case. The applicants — 
all parties to Yoshina I - claimed to have an ideological 
interest in the outcome of the case, as well as an interest 
in protecting their victory in state court. The motion to 
intervene was denied. 


On cross-motions for summary judgment, the district 
court ruled for plaintiffs. It found that plaintiffs had 
standing, and granted their motion for class certification, 
rulings that are not contested on appeal. The court con- 
cluded that federal jurisdiction existed in this case, reject- 
ing the Rooker/Feldman doctrine (see District of Columbia 
Court of Appeals v. Feldman, 460 U.S. 462 (1983); Rooker v. 
Fidelity Trust Co., 263 U.S. 413 (1923)) and the Eleventh 
Amendment as jurisdictional bars. Finally, the district 
court concluded that the constitutional convention vote 
had been fundamentally unfair because the Hawaii 
Supreme Court’s opinion marked a departure from prior 
election practices, was unforeseeable to the average voter 
because it contradicted the previously understood elec- 
tion procedures, and contradicted information that the 
State itself had disseminated before the election. The 
court then ordered that a new election take place. 


Everyone appeals. In No. 97-16408, Yoshina appeals 
the ruling that the Eleventh Amendment does not bar this 
suit, the conclusion that the convention vote was funda- 
mentally unfair, and the order to conduct a new election. 
In No. 97-16543, Bennett cross-appeals the district court's 
decision to order a new election, instead of requiring 


34a 


Hawaii to certify tht the constitutional convention ques- 
tion passed. In Nos. $/-16540 and 97-16596, the AFL-CIO 
appeals the denial of its motion to intervene. 


II. 


Before we can reach the merits, we must overcome 
two jurisdictional hurdles. The first is the Rooker/Feldman 
doctrine, which holds that “a losing party in state court is 
barred from seeking what in substance would be appel- 
late review of the state judgment in a United States 
District Court, based on the losing party’s claim that the 
state judgment itself violates the loser’s federal rights.” 
Johnson v. De Grandy, 512 U.S. 997, 1005-06 (1994). The 
rationale behind this doctrine is that the only federal 
court with the power to hear appeals from state courts is 
the United States Supreme Court. Dubinka v. Judges of Sup. 
Ct., 23 F.3d 218, 221 (9th Cir. 1994). 


We agree with the district court that the doctrine is 
not implicated in this case because it applies only when 
the federal plaintiff was a party to the state suit. Johnson, 
512 U.S. at 1006. Here, the federal plaintiffs were not 
parties to the state suit. That case was between the AFL- 
CIO and Yoshina. Yoshina lost, and the referendum was 
decreed to have failed. Then a coalition of entirely new 
people sued Yoshina in federal court, and Yoshina is now 
in the position of having to defend his defeat in state 
court. However, since the new plaintiffs were not parties 
to the state suit, their suit is not barred by the Rooker/ 
Feldman doctrine. 


: 
en eee eC Tee 


Sl aac ne 


35a 


The only twist is that two of the federal plaintiffs, 
Citizens for a Constitutional Convention and Let the Peo- 
ple Decide, participated in the state case as amici curiae. 
Amici, however, are not actually parties to a case, cannot 
conduct discovery, and do not have the right to appeal an 
adverse decision. Since the Rooker/Feldman doctrine 
applies only when the federal plaintiff was a party to the 
state case and is challenging an adverse decision by the 
state court, Johnson, 512 U.S. at 1005-06, it follows that 
mere participation in the state case as amici does not 
invoke the Rooker/Feldman bar. Exxon Corp. v. Bd. of Ed. of 
Lamar County, 849 F. Supp. 479, 487 (S.D. Miss. 1994), 
aff'd, 68 F.3d 472 (Sth Cir. 1995) (per curiam). Accordingly, 
we conclude that the Rooker/Feldman doctrine does not 
apply. 

We are likewise unpersuaded by Yoshina’s argument 
that we lack jurisdiction because of the Eleventh Amend- 
ment. The Amendment is understood to bar suits by a 
person against a state on questions of federal law. Semi- 
nole Tribe v. Florida, 116 S. Ct. 1114, 1122 (1996). However, 
an exception exists for a suit that seeks prospective relief 
from a state’s ongoing constitutional violation, provided 
it names a state official as the defendant. Edelman v. 
Jordan, 415 U.S. 651, 064-66 (1974); Ex parte Young, 209 
U.S. 123, 159-60 (1908). 


Yoshina argues that no ongoing constitutional viola- 
tion exists, reasoning that the November 5, 1996, election 
has already taken place and whatever problems may have 
occurred are unlikely to be repeated. In effect, Yoshina 
argues that completed state election mishaps can never be 
reviewed in federal court because once the election has 
occurred, any relief would be retrospective. The flaw in 


36a 


this argument is Yoshina’s unduly narrow conception of 
the alleged constitutional injury. If, after ruling that blank 
ballots counted against the convention question, the 
Hawaii Supreme Court had also decided that it was 
unfair to surprise voters with this new rule and ordered a 
new election, we do not suppose that Bennett would have 
troubled himself with a federal lawsuit. What Bennett 
objects to is not the mere occurrence of an allegedly 
unfair vote, but the state’s decision to give effect to that 
vote. And obviously, the iegal effectiveness of the 1996 
vote — 1.e., whether a constitutional convention will take 
place — is something that will, or will not, happen in the 
future. Thus, we conclude that Bennett seeks prospective, 
not retrospective, relief. Cf. Goodisman v. Lytle, 724 F.2d 
818, 820 (9th Cir. 1984) (holding that Eleventh Amend- 
ment does not bar a suit by a public employee seeking 
reinstatement, despite the fact that the unconstitutional 
termination was already completed). 


Further, a decision by this court that the Eleventh 
Amendment bars this suit would be contrary to the deci- 
sions of several other circuits which, while not explicitly 
addressing the Eleventh Amendment, decided the consti- 
tutionality of completed election errors. E.g., Roe v Ala- 
bama, 43 F.3d 574 (11th Cir. 1995); Curry v. Baker, 802 F.2d 
1302 (11th Cir. 1986); Griffin v. Burns, 570 F.2d 1065 (1st 
Cir. 1978). While these cases did not directly confront the 
Eleventh Amendment question we decide today, we 
decline to suggest that those other courts acted in the 
absence of federal jurisdiction. Accordingly, we reject 
Yoshina’s Eleventh Amendment argument. 


Having surmounted the jurisdictional barriers, we 
now turn to the merits. 


37a 


Il. 


Bennett makes two broad constitutional attacks on 
the 1996 convention vote. First, he argues that Yoshina | 
was a totally unforeseeable interpretation of the state’s 
election laws and, therefore, amounted to a denial of 
substantive due process. Conceding that the Hawaii 
Supreme Court is the ultimate arbiter of Hawaii law and 
that the court had never before ruled on whether blank 
ballots counted in a constitutional convention vote, Ben- 
nett nonetheless argues that the past election practice was 
sufficiently clear that voters were unfairly surprised by 
Yoshina I. He also points to the misinformation distrib- 
uted by the state as evidence of the voters’ surprise. 


Second, Bennett argues that counting blank ballots as 
votes against the constitutional convention question vio- 
lates the First Amendment. He contends that the Hawaii 
Supreme Court effectively re-wrote voters’ abstentions 
into “no” votes and that this rewriting amounted to 
unconstitutionally coerced speech. 


We first postulate two assumptions that must guide 
the constitutional inquiry before us. First, states have 
considerable freedom to design their own election laws. 
Burdick v. Takushi, 504 U.S. 428, 433 (1992). The Supreme 
Court has long held that the structure of a state’s internal 
democratic processes is a “political question” beyond the 
ken of judicial review. Baker v. Carr, 369 U.S. 186, 218-226 
(1961) (citing cases). Although federal courts may invali- 
date state election laws that deny some voters equal 
protection of the law, Reynolds v. Sims, 377 U.S. 523, 568 
(1964), unduly burden free speech, Anderson v. Celebrezze, 
460 U.S. 780, 788-90 (1983), or violate a federal statute, 


38a 


Foster v. Love, 118 S. Ct. 464, 467 (1997), we must normally 
defer to the states’ regulatory interests when these fed- 
eral rights are not implicated. Cf. Timmons v. Twin Cities 
Area New Party, 117 S. Ct. 1364, 1369 (1997) (recognizing 
states’ regulatory interests). 


Second, the Hawaii Supreme Court is by definition 
the final arbiter of Hawaii law. Johnson v. Fankell, 117 S. 
Ct. 1800, 1804 (1997). We are powerless to review that 
court’s decision in Yoshina I, even if we thought it was 
wrong or contrary to general principles of election laws. 
Insofar as Bennett’s suit is a backhanded critique of the 
merits of Yoshina I, we should be clear that we are unwill- 
ing and unable to indulge this. 


With these general principles in mind, we now turn 
to Bennett’s constitutional claims. 


A. 


Bennett's first claim is that Hawaii’s unforeseeable 
departure from past election practices was a denial of 
substantive due process. 


In Bates v. Jones, 131 F.3d 843 (9th Cir. 1997) (en banc), 
we recently considered what restraints substantive due 
process places on a state’s election laws. In Bates, we 
upheld a California term limits initiative against the 
claim that voters did mot understand that the initiative 
imposed a lifetime limit on officeholders. Id. at 846. We 
assumed, “without deciding, that a federal court may 
determine whether a state has given adequate notice to 
its voters in connection with a statewide initiative ballot 


39a 


measure. ...” Id. We then concluded that adequate notice 
existed, assuming any was needed. Id. 


We agree with Bennett that Bates does not resolve the 
substantive due process claim in this case. First, Bates did 
not actually decide whether voters must receive notice of 
what they are voting on - it merely assumed so arguendo. 
Second, the notice at issue in Bates is of a completely 
different kind from the notice at issue in this case. In 
Bates, the court dealt with whether voters understood the 
substantive content of what they were voting on. The 
lack-of-notice argument was a radical challenge to the 
entire initiative process, and indeed the entire legislative 
process, because it effectively claimed that federal courts 
could strike down legislation whenever the legislators 
did not understand what they were doing. We deal here 
with a more modest substantive due process claim. Ben- 
nett challenges only Hawaii's election procedures, and he 
argues only that voters need to understand that they are 
voting, not the substance of what they are voting on, or 
anything else. Thus, unlike Bates, this case concerns a 
procedural challenge to the administration of an election, 
and we can resolve this constitutional claim without 
inquiring into the content of the proposal to be voted on. 
Accordingly, we put Bates to one side ard turn to a 
different strand of Ninth Circuit precedent. 


Several appellate courts, including our own, have 
held that an election is a denial of substantive due pro- 
cess if it is conducted in a manner that is furdamertally 
unfair. E.g., Soules v. Kauaians for Nukolii Campaign Comm., 
849 F.2d 1176, 1183-84 (9th Cir. 1988). We have drawn a 


40a 


distinction between “garden variety” election irregu- 
larities and a pervasive error that undermines the integ- 
rity of the vote. Id. In general, garden variety election 
irregularities do not violate the Due Process Clause, even 
if they control the outcome of the vote or election. Gold v. 
Feinberg, 101 F.3d 796, 801 (2d Cir. 1996) (human error 
resulting in miscounting of votes, presence of ineligible 
candidates on ballot, and delay in arrival of voting 
machines); Curry v. Baker, 802 F.2d 1302, 1316 (11th Cir. 
1986) (allegedly inadequate state response to illegal cross- 
over voting); Bodine v. Elkhart County Elec. Bd., 788 F.2d 
1270, 1272 (7th Cir. 1986) (mechanical and human error in 
counting votes); Hendon v. North Carolina State Bd. of 
Elections, 710 F.2d 177, 182 (4th Cir. 1983) (technical defi- 
ciencies in printing ballots); Gamza v. Aguirre, 619 F.2d 
449, 454 (5th Cir. 1980) (negligent vote counting); Hen- 
nings v. Grafton, 523 F.2d 861, 864-65 (7th Cir. 1975) (mal- 
functioning of voting machines); Pettengill v. Putnam 
County R-1 School Dist., 472 F.2d 121, 122 (8th Cir. 1973) 
(counting some votes that were illegally cast); Powell v. 
Power 436 F.2d 84 (2d Cir. 1970) (non-party members 
mistakenly allowed to vote in congressional primary); 
Johnson v. Hood, 430 F.2d 610, 613 (5th Cir. 1970) (arbitrary 
rejection of 10 ballots). 


However, when election irregularities transcended 
garden variety problems, the election is invalid. In Griffin 
v. Burns, 570 F.2d 1065, the First Circuit dealt with Rhode 
Island’s practice of counting absentee ballots in state 
primary elections. The established practice in the state 
was to count such ballots, and state election officials 
advertised to voters that absentee ballots would be coun- 
te. in the primaries. Id. at 1067. However, after the vote, 


4la 


the Rhode Island Supreme Court invalidated the absentee 
ballots, finding them improper in primary elections. Id. at 
1068. This ruling meant that 10 percent of the total votes 
cast would not count. Id. at 1067. 


The First Circuit ruled the primary unconstitutional. 
The court focused on the massive ex post disenfranchise- 
ment that the Rhode Island Supreme Court's ruling cre- 
ated, and it noted that the ruling would have been a total 
surprise to the typical voter. Id. at 1078-79. 


Roe v. Alabama, 43 F.3d 574, also dealt with surprise to 
the voters and disenfranchisement. In that case, the prior 
Alabama practice had been not to count absentee ballots 
unless they were notarized and witnessed; but after the 
1994 election, an Alabama court ordered the improperly 
submitted absentee ballots to be counted. The Eleventh 
Circuit ruled the election fundamentally unfair, citing 
two theories: first, that Alabama was diluting the votes of 
those who had submitted proper ballots; and second, that 
Alabama had disenfranchised those people who would 
have voted absentee, but were deterred from doing so 
because they did not want to expend the effort to obtain 
notarization and witness validation of their ballots. 


The limits of Griffin and Roe are demonstrated by 
Partido Nuevo Progresista v. Barreto Perez, 639 F.2d 825 (1st 
Cir. 1980). In Barreto Perez, the court upheld the Supreme 
Court of Puerto Rico’s decision to count mismarked bal- 
lots, notwithstanding the Surprise this created for the 
voters. The court rejected that vote “dilution” could be a 
constitutional violation, reasoning that counting more 
votes than had previously been permitted did not disen- 
franchise anyone. Id. at 828. The court also noted that, on 


42a 


the facts of Barreto Perez, there was no reliance by anyone 
on the previously understood election practice because 
no one could really have “relied” on the assumed 
invalidity of ballots in which the mark was drawn 
slightly Outside of the printed rectangle. Id. 


A generai pattern emerges from all of these cases 
taken together. Mere fraud or mistake will not render an 
election invalid. However, a court will strike down an 
election on substantive due process grounds if two ele- 
ments are present: (1) likely reliance by voters on an 
established election procedure and/or official pronounce- 
ments about what the procedure will be in the coming 
election; and (2) significant disenfranchisement that 
results from a change in the election procedures.* 


It is immediately apparent that neither element is 
present in this case. As in Barreto Perez, one requires a 
vivid imagination to see how voters could have “relied” 
on an understanding that blank ballots would not be 
counted. Citizens who wished to vote “yes” or “no” were 
presented with a clear way to do so. Voters who didn’t 
care simply left the question blank. It is beyond belief to 
suggest that thousands of voters who left the convention 
question blank were secretly relying on the hope that 
their votes would not be counted, and that they would 


3 This is not an exhaustive description of electoral 
problems that might be fundamentally unfair. See, e.¢g., Duncan v. 
Poythress, 657 F.2d 691, 703 (5th Cir. Unit B) (entire electorate 
disenfranchised when legally required election did not occur); 
Griffin, 570 F.2d at 1080 (“outrageous racial discrimination” 
requires invalidating an election “simply for its lack of 
integrity”). 


A en Oo Pe PE ee Nae ORE Ee Sy LD a eT Oe Tee S, 


43a 


have voted “yes” had they foreseen the decision in 
Yoshina I. They did not vote because they did not care or 
did not care enough to do so. 


Further, there was no disenfranchisement or mean- 
ingful vote dilution in this case. Every ballot submitted 
was counted, and no one was deterred from going to the 
polls. The massive disenfranchisement of absentee voters 
present in Griffin and the deterrence of people who 
would have voted in Roe are simply not present here. 


In the end, the Hawaii Supreme Court’s decision in 
Yoshina I was merely a clarification of the State’s election 
laws: Hawaii used to interpret voter indifference as a 
reason neither for nor against amending the state consti- 
tution, but now interprets voter indifference as a reason 
not to convene a constitutional convention.’ This ex post 
clarification of the state’s election laws possibly amounts, 
at worst, to a garden variety election irregularity, but is 
hardly a pervasive error that undermines the integrity of 
the vote. Soules, 849 F.2d at 1183-84. Accordingly, we 
reject Bennett’s substantive due process challenge. 


B. 


Bennett's second, separate constitutional challenge is 
based on the First Amendment. He argues that Hawaii's 


* Bennett and Yoshina disagree on whether this issue has 
been outcome determinative in any past election on whether to 
convene a constitutional convention. The record does not 
solidly support either side. 


44a 


policy of putting all questions on the same physical bal- 
lot,5 when combined with Yoshina I’s method for calculat- 
ing a majority, “coerces” votes in violation of the First 
Amendment. Bennett correctly observes that the combi- 
nation of these rules effectively prevents Hawaii voters 
from abstaining solely on the constitutional convention 
question: Provided the voter turns in a ballot, that ballot 
will have a legal effect on the constitutional convention 
question, regardless of whether the voter answered “yes,” 
“no,” or not at all. In Bennett’s view this coerces the 
citizens to vote; and, because blank ballots have the same 
effect as “no” votes under the rule announced in Yoshina 
I, this is content-based speech discrimination. 


The central problem with Bennett’s First Amendment 
claim is his inability to distinguish between a state’s 
forcing someone to vote and a state’s deciding not to 
bother with a constitutional convention unless most of 
the citizens express an interest in one. It is perfectly 
constitutional for a state to demand that a proposition 
win not only a majority of the votes cast, but a majority of 
all the votes that could have been cast. Gordon v. Lance, 
403 U.S. 1, 7 (1971); Gray v. Town of Darien, 927 F.2d 69, 70 
(2d Cir. 1991); Hall v. Thornton, 445 F.2d 834, 835 (4th Cir. 
1971). That is not very different from requiring that a 
proposition win a majority of all the votes that could 
have been cast by everyone who actually went to the 


5 It is unclear if Hawaii election officials are required to do 
this. Bennett states that Haw. Rev. Stat. § 11-137 mandates that 
all questions appear on the same physical ballot, but that statute 
merely prohibits the exhibition or removal of ballots from the 
polling place. 


45a 


polls - which is what Hawaii does. In short, Hawaii is not 
forcing anybody to vote. It is just deciding not to hold a 
constitutional convention if the majority of voters either 
don’t want one or don’t care. 


Nor does Hawaii impermissibly convert blank ballots 
into “no” votes. See Burdick, 504 U.S. at 438 (upholding 
the reasonable regulation of speech on election ballots, 
but concluding that states may not “require voters to 
espouse positions they do ..ot support”). Under Hawaii's 
procedure, blank votes are counted as blank and commu- 
nicated to the world as blank, so the expressive content of 
the voter’s blank ballot is not altered. What Bennett is 
really complaining about is that Yoshina I's definition of a 
majority of “ballots cast” makes it difficult for “yes” votes 
to prevail. But that has nothing to do with speech. 


We reject Bennett’s free speech claim. 


IV. 


Having concluded that the Hawaii Supreme Court’s 
decision in Yoshina I did not violate any voter’s free 
speech or substantive due process rights, we reverse the 
district court’s grant of summary judgment for Bennett in 
No. 97-16408, and remand with instructions to enter sum- 
mary judgment in favor of Yoshina. We vacate the district 
court’s injunction ordering a new election on the constitu- 
tional convention question and the stay of the injunction 
pending appeal. 


Our disposition of the merits also resolves the 
remaining issues before us. In No. 97-16543, Bennett 
cross-appeals the district court’s order for a new vote, 


46a 


seeking an order requiring Hawaii to certify that the 
constitutional convention question passed. In Nos. 
97-16540 and 97-16596, the AFL-CIO appeals the district 
court’s denial of its motion to intervene. We dismiss these 
three appeals as moot. Yoshina shall recover his costs on 
appeal from Bennett. Bennett and the AFL-CIO shall bear 
their own costs on appeal. 


REVERSED and REMANDED in part, VACATED in 
part, and DISMISSED in part.

ORDER GRANTING PLAINTIFFS’ MOTION 
FOR CLASS CERTIFICATION AND GRANTING 
PLAINTIFFS’ MOTION FOR SUMMARY 
JUDGMENT AND DENYING DEFENDANTS’ CROSS-

The court heard the parties’ Motions on July 7, 1997 
Mark J. Bennett, Esq., appeared at the hearing on behalf 
of Plaintiffs; Deputy Attorney General John P. Dellera, 
appeared at the hearing on behalf of Defendants. After 
reviewing the motions and the supporting and opposing 
memoranda, the court GRANTS Plaintiffs’ Motion for 
Class Certification, GRANTS Plaintiffs’ Motion for Sum- 
mary Judgment, DENIES Defendants’ Cross-Motion for 
Summary Judgment, and ORDERS a Special Election on 
the issue of a Constitutional Convention for the State of 
Hawaii. 


BACKGROUND 


This case revolves around a requirement contained 
within the Hawaii Constitution mandating that every ten 
years the question of whether a constitutional convention 
shall be held must be placed before the voters. 


The 1996 Hawaii general election ballot contained a 
question as to whether there shall “be a convention to 
propose a revision of or amendments to the Constitu- 
ticn.” Hawaii’s Constitution provides that “if a majority 
of the ballots cast upon such a question be in the affirma- 
tive,” delegates to the convention shall be chosen and 


MESS capes wat eg 3 
OSS OS OES OER ea ae ee eee ee a tae a 


49a 


elected.’ Historically, and without exception, the past 
practice in Hawaii had been not to count blank ballots as 
ballots cast upon the constitutional convention (“con- 
con”) question. 


The Elections Office distributed to the voters, both in 
written form, and on the Internet, information that speci- 
fically told voters, that in calculating a majority on the 
con-con question, if the “yes” votes exceeded the “no” 
votes, the con-con question would pass, and that blank 
ballots would not be counted for any purpose. 


One of the named Plaintiffs, Citizens for a Constitu- 
tional Convention (“Citizens”), had been informing 
voters that blank ballots would count as “no” votes. 
Citizens was contacted by the Elections Office shortly 
before the election, told that blank ballots would not 
count, and told that they were absolutely prohibited from 
telling voters that blank ballots would count. Citizens is 
an organization of several hundred people who were 
actively campaigning for the con-con, and changed its 
course as a result of what it was told. 


The con-con question was approved. The elections 
officials found that the “yes” votes exceeded the “no” 
votes. The blank ballots were not counted. Following the 
election, the Hawaii Attorney General issued a formal 
opinion that according to the Hawaii Constitution, the 
blank ballots did not count, and that the con-con was 
approved. A local labor union - the UPW - filed an 
election challenge, and the Hawaii Supreme Court ruled 
for the first time that blank ballots should have been 


' Haw. Const. Art. XVIII § 2. 


50a 


counted, and that the con-con question did not pass. The 
Court made a distinction between those voters who did 
not turn in their ballots at all and those who turned in 
ballots which were blank on the con-con question. Those 
ballots which were not turned in were not counted; those 
which were turned in blank were counted as “no” votes. 


In Hawaii, a constitutional convention is the only 
way citizens have to directly amend their State Constitu- 
tion. There is no right to an initiative. The right to vote on 
a constitutional convention only comes to citizens of 
Hawaii once every ten years. 


Plaintiffs claim that they have First and Fourteenth 
Amendment rights to a fundamentally fair election. They 
claim that it is fundamentally unfair if voters are given no 
notice of what their votes will mean. Consequently, Plain- 
tiffs argue, it is a priori fundamentally unfair if voters are 
actually given the wrong information by the State as to 
what their votes will mean. 


DISCUSSION 


I. Class Certification 


The decision to grant or deny class certification rests 
within the sound discretion of the trial court. Yamamoto v. 
Omiya, 564 F.2d 1319, 1325 (9th Cir. 1977). In order for a 
class action to be certified, plaintiffs must establish all of 
the requirements of Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 23(a) 
and at least one of the alternative requirements of Rule 
23(b). Blake v. Arnett, 633 F.2d 906, 912 (9th Cir. 1981), 
Rule 23(a) provides as follows: 


¥ 4 
x 
& 


5la 


One or more members of a class may sue or be 
sued as representative parties on behalf of all 
only if (1) the class is so numerous that joinder 
of all members is impracticable, (2) there are 
questions of law or fact common to the class, (3) 
the claims or defenses of the representative par- 
ties are typical of the claims or defenses of the 
class, and (4) the representative parties will 
fairly and adequately protect the interests of the 
class. 


Fed. R. Civ. P. 23(a). These tour requirements have been 
more conveniently termed: (1) “numerosity”; (2) “com- 
monality”; (3) “typicality”; and (4) “adequacy.” See Paxton 
v. Union Nat'l Bank, 688 F.2d 552, 559 (8th Cir. 1922), cert. 
denied, 460 U.S. 1083, 103 S. Ct. 1772, 76 L. Ed. 2d 345 
(1983); Yslava v. Hughes Aircraft Co., 845 F. Supp. 705, 712 
(D. Ariz. 1993). A district court’s denial of class certifica- 
tion will not be overturned absent an abuse of the district 
court’s discretion. Siles v. ILGWU National Retirement 
Fund, 783 F.2d 923, 926 (9th Cir. 1986). 


The court will first note that Defendants apparently 
concede the fact that the first two requirements of numer- 
osity and commonality have been met in this case. See 
Defendants’ Memo in Opp. at 3. The court likewise finds 
little room for debate over the first two requirements. 


A. Numerosity 


In order to satisfy the numerosity element, as with all 
of the Rule 23 requirements, the Ferson seeking -lass 
certification bears the burden of proving impracticability. 
Siles, 783 F.2d at 930 (class certification denied because 


52a 


plaintiff failed to provide sufficient evidence of numer- 
osity).2 “Joinder is considered more practicable when all 
members of the same class are from the same geographic 
area. Where class members may be easily identified, join- 
der is also more practicable.” Hum v. Dericks, 162 F.R.D. 
628, 634 (D. Haw. 1995) Although the court is entitled to 
draw reasonable inferences of the class size from facts 
before it, Sherman v. Grienpentrog, 775 F. Supp. 1383, 1389 
(D. Nev. 1991), a party seeking certification may not rely 
on conclusory allegations that joinder would be impracti- 
cable, or on mere speculation regarding the size of the 
class. Siles, 783 F.2d at 930. 


There are 163,869 citizens who voted “yes” on the 
con-con question and 45,245 citizens who did not mark 
their ballots, and who turned those ballots over to pre- 
cinct officials for deposit in the ballot box. Plaintiffs pro- 
pose to certify a class of well over 100,000 individuals. 
Specifically, Plaintiffs seek certification of a class of 
“(tlhose citizens of the State of Hawaii who voted in the 
1996 General Election, and who, on the question on the 
ballot as to whether there shall be a convention to pro- 
pose a revision of or amendments to the Constitution, 
either voted in the affirmative, or did not mark their 
ballots either “yes” or “no,” and who turned those ballots 


2 In Siles, plaintiffs’ only evidence of numerosity was that 
31,000 employees covered by the plan, lost their jobs in 1974 and 
1975. However, the plaintiffs presented no evidence regarding 
how many employees worked 10 years in covered employment, 
how many did not receive a pension, or how many did not work 
a year in covered employment after 1976. According to Siles, 
sheer numbers, without more, is insufficient to satisfy 
numerosity. 


53a 


over to precinct officials for deposit in the ballot box.” 
Clearly joinder of all members of this proposed class 
would not only be impracticable, but virtually impossi- 
ble. The court deems this requirement met. 


B. Common Questions of Law and Fact 


Rule 23 provides for certification of a class action 
only if “there are questions of law or fact common to the 
class.” Fed. R. Civ. P. 23(a)(2) Therefore, “to justify class 
action treatment, there must be some issue involved 
‘common to the class as a whole,’ and relief must ‘turn on 
questions of law applicable in the same manner to each 
member of the class.’ ” Haley v. Medtronic, Inc., 169 F.R.D. 
643, 648 (C.D. Cal. 1996) (citing Ceneral Tel, Co. v. Falcon, 
457 U.S. 147, 155 (1982)). 


Plaintiffs claim that common questions of law and 
fact exist between the Plaintiffs in the proposed class 
because they concern actions that were applicable to the 
class as a whole. The class members either voted affirma- 
tively for the con-con or they did not vote one way or the 
other. Because the proposed class members allege that the 
post-election interpretation of the election standards vio- 
lates their constitutional right to a fair election, they 
sufficiently allege common questions of law and fact. In 
addition, they all seek the same declaratory and injunc- 
tive relief: either certification that the con-con passed — or 
a new election. As Defendants raise no arguments to 
contest this, the court finds that the commonality require- 
ment is met. 


54a 


C. Typicality of Claims 


Rule 23 requires that “the claims or defenses of the 
representative parties [be] typical of the claims or 
defenses of the class.” Fed. R. Civ. P. 23(a)(3). Typicality is 
met if other members of the class have similar injury 
resulting from the same course of conduct and as a class 
advanced similar legal theories. Hanon v. Dataprods., Inc., 
976 F.2d 497, 508 (9th Cir. 1992). 


Defendants argue that typicality cannot be met where 
plaintiffs are subject to defenses that other plaintiffs are 
not subject to. Specifically, Defendants maintain that 
some plaintiffs may be subject to a defense of standing 
that is based upon lack of competent proof as to how they 
voted, while that standing defense may not apply to 
other class members who could show, through competent 
and admissible evidence, that they either supported or 
abstained from voting on the con-con question. Thus, 
according to Defendants, the position of the named repre- 
sentatives, and presumably other plaintiffs, is not typical 
of all members of the class. The court finds this argument 
to be without merit.* 


While it is true that typicality may be lacking where 
the purported class representative is subject to a unique 
defense that could not be asserted against other members 
of the class, Koos v. First National Bank of Peoria, 496 F.2d 
1162, 1165 (7th Cir. 1974), such is not the case here. 


3 See Griffin v. Burns, 570 F.2d 1065 (1st Cir. 1978) (upon the 
state’s invalidation of absentee and shut-in votes, district court 
properly certified class comprised of all who voted by absentee 
or shut-in ballots in the primary, without regard to how each 
voted). 


"s _ 


55a 


Voters, by their mere status as voters, have standing to 
sue for violation of their voting rights. Gray v. Sanders, 
372 U.S. 368, 374 (1963) (“[A]ny person whose right to 
vote is impaired has standing to sue.”). Although a voter 
cannot be allowed to impeach a certified election by 
stating the content of his vote, National Federation of Rail- 
way Workers v. National Mediation Board, 110 F.2d 529, 536 
(D.C. Cir.), cert. denied, 310 U:S. 628 (1940), nothing pro- 
hibits an individual from stating how he voted in order to 
establish standing. Even if the defense of standing were 
not applicable to all class members, minor differences in 
defenses may be overlooked if they are not likely to affect 
fair representation of the class. Hochschuler v. G.D. Searle 
& Co., 82 FR.D. 339, 345 (N.D. Ill. 1978). 


Defendants refer to differences in the factual allega- 
tions of the representatives in support of their argument 
that typicality is not met. Specifically, Defendants argue 
that because two representatives voted “yes”, they have 
no claim for infringement of a “right to abstain” or for 
unequal treatment, and likewise, those representatives 
who turned in blank ballots have no claim for unlawful 
dilution of their vote. Thus, Defendants contend that the 
named representatives cannot represent the interests of 
all-class members, because their claims do not rest upon 
the same legal theories. See Defendants’ Memo in Opp. at 
4-5. 


Defendants’ argument fails for the following reasons. 
As noted in Defendants’ memorandum, if a court finds 
that the representative’s claims are not typical of the 
class, it may divide the class into subclasses and appoint 
a different representative for each, rather than dismissing 
the class action. Evans v. Chicago, 689 F.2d 1286 (7th Cir. 


56a 


1982); FRCP 23(c)(4). Plaintiffs have already assigned 
named representatives to represent each subclass within 
the class: (1) Citizens campaigned for passage of the con- 
con; (2) two of the class representatives voted for can-con; 
and (3) three of the class representatives specifically did 
not vote on the con-con but turned in ballots.4 


In the Ninth Circuit, a named plaintiff's claim will be 
typical of the class “where there is a nexus between the 
injury suffered by the plaintiff and the injury suffered by 
the class.” Jordan v. County of Los Angeles, 669 F.2d 1311, 
1321 (9th Cir.) vacated, 459 U.S. 810 (1982). Therefore, “a 
named plaintiff’s claim is typical if it stems from the same 
event, practice, or course of conduct that forms the basis 
of the class claims and is based upon the same legal or 
remedial theory.” Id. 


The entire class seeks relief from the same course of 
conduct based upon the same remedial theory. As the 
named representatives’ claims are identical to that 
asserted for their respective subclasses, the court finds 
the typicality requirement to be satisfied. 


Notwithstanding this, if only one representative is 
adequate, that satisfies the requirements of Rule 23. 
Grasty v, Amalgamated Clothing & Textile Workers Union, 
828 F.2d 123, 128 (3rd Cir. 1987), cert. denied, 484 U.S. 1042 
(1988). Therefore, even if the court declined to subdivide 
the class, either Frumin or Bennett would be an adequate 


4 Dividing the class into subclasses would solve any 
potential problems of overlapping issues. The class sought to be 
certified has severa! subparts: those who voted “yes,” and those 
who voted, but did not vote on the con-con question and turned 
in their ballots, and those who campaigned for the con-con. 


57a 


representative so that the typicality requirement would 
be satisfied in this case. 


D. Adequacy of Representation 


Class action is proper if “the representative parties 
will fairly and adequately protect the interests of the 
class.” Fed. R. Civ. P. 23(a)(4). In order to show that 
representation is adequate, plaintiffs must demonstrate: 


(a) The class representative must not have 
interests antagonistic to the class regarding the 
substance of the litigation; 


(b) The representative’s attorney must be qual- 
ified, experienced and generally able to conduct 
the proposed litigation; and 


(c) The representative and his/her counsel will 
vigorously prosecute the action in a manner 
consistent with the best interests of the absent 
class members. 


Honolulu Fed. Sav. & Loan Ass‘n v. Verex Assurance, Inc., 
116 F.R.D. 474, 476 (D. Haw. 1987) (citations omitted). In 
general, the representation is adequate if: (1) the attorney 
representing the class is qualified and competent; and (2) 
the class representatives are not disqualified by interests 
antagonistic to the remainder of the class. Lerwill v. 
Inflight Motion Pictures, Inc., 582 F.2d 507, 512 (9th Cir. 
1978). The differences between class members must be 
substantial and relate to potentially antagonistic objec- 
tives or perspectives to defeat adequacy of representa- 
tion. Gill v. Monroe Co., 95 ER.D. 518, 520-21 (W.D. N.Y. 
1982) 


58a 


Counsel for Plaintiffs, is adequate to represent the 
class. Plaintiff’s counsel has practiced before this court in 
extensive and complicated cases, including those which 
have raised major constitutiona! issues. This court is not 
aware of any reason why counsel for Plaintiffs would be 
inadequate or incompetent to handle the present case. 
Further, Defendants do not contest the competency of 
Plaintiff’s counsel. 


Defendants do claim, however, that because Plain- 
tiffs’ counsel is a named representative as well as counsel 
for the class, there is a potential conflict of interest. Again 
Defendants’ argument fails, as the rationale for denying 
representative status to class members serving as counsel 
is only pertinent when monetary damages are sought, 
and when attorneys fees provide an incentive to reach an 
unfavorable settlement. In re Hotel Tel. Charges, 500 F.2d 
86, 91 (9th Cir. 1974). None of the above rationale are 
applicable here. 


E. Rule 23(b)(2) 


Defendants also oppose class certification on the 
ground that such certification is unnecessary because any 
relief accorded to Plaintiffs will benefit the entire pro- 
posed class. This “lack of need” approach has been both 
embraced and rejected by a number of courts;5 under this 


> Newberg, the leading commentator on class actions, came 
to this conclusion regarding this issue: 


Like Newton’s Law of Thermodynamics, for every 
class denial on the basis of lack of need, one is able to 
find a decision, or several decisions, often in the same 


sR a a SS pe Rca a ea cen ren eran 


59a 


rationale, district courts may deny class certification 
under Rule 23(b)(2) if certification is not needed for the 
potential class members to obtain the desired class-wide 
relief. See Aacen v. San Juan County Sheriff's Dep't, 944 F.2d 
691, 700 (10th Cir. 1991); Berger v. Heckler, 771 F.2d 1556, 
1566 (2nd Cir. 1985) 


The Ninth Circuit, however, has not taken a clear 
position an this question. In James v. Ball, 613 F.2d 180 (9th 
Cir. 1979), rev'd on other grounds, 451 U.S. 355 (1981), the 
Ninth Circuit concluded that denial of certification was 
appropriate and not an abuse of discretion where “the 
relief sought will, as a practical matter, produce the same 
result as formal class-wide relief.” Id. at 186. However, 
two district courts have interpreted a later Ninth Circuit 
case, Zepeda v. INS, 753 F.2d 719, 729-30 n.1 (9th Cir. 1972), 
as narrowing this holding to the situation where class- 
wide relief would inevitably follow if the requested indi- 
vidual relief were granted. See Calderon v. Madigan, 831 F. 
Supp. 1484, 1489 (D. Idaho 1992); Nehmer v. United States 
Veterans’ Administration, 118 E.R.D. 113, 118 (N.D. Cal. 
1987). 


In Nehmer, the district court noted that the Ninth 
Circuit in James gave only a brief discussion to the issue 


circuit, where other courts have certified Rule 23(b)(2) 
classes under virtually the same circumstances . . . [A] 
need requirement finds no support in Rule 23 and, if 
applied, would entirely negate any proper class 
certifications under Rule 23 (b), a result hardly 
intended by the Rules Advisory Committee. 


1 Herbert B. Newberg & Alba Conte, Newberg or Class Actions 
§ 4.19, at 4-62, 4-70 (3rd ed. 1992). 


60a 


and thus could not be considered as having adopted this 
requirement. Nehmer. 118 F.R.D. at 118. After reasoning 
that the precedential value of James was limited by its 
reversal and by Zepeda, the court then noted the “strong 
policy reasons against rigidly employing a need require- 
ment”: 


Litigating complex claims by class actions is a 
favored procedure, not only because class 
actions efficiently resolve the disputes of 
numerous similarly situated individuals, but 
because they have other advantages. For exam- 
ple, the use of class actions allows a large 
number of plaintiffs to pool resources to hire 
attorneys capable of conducting complex litiga- 
tion. Class actions require court approval of set- 
tlements, so that courts can ensure that the 
interests of a wide group of people have been 
protected and advanced. Class actions enable 
unidentified class members to enforce court 
orders with contempt proceedings, rather than 
relying on the res judicata in a subsequent law- 
suit. Finally, class actions enable plaintiffs to 
avoid the mooting to important claims. 


Id. at 119. Given these advantages, and the fact that the 
need requirement does not appear in the text of Rule 23, 
the district court “question[ed] whether it should impose 
the need requirement on the plaintiffs in this case in the 
absence of clearer guidance by the Ninth Circuit.” Id. 
Regardless, however, the district court concluded that the 
named plaintiffs demonstrated that class certification was 
necessary because part of the requested relief was to void 
prior benefits denials and it was uncertain at what point 


61la 


defendant would recalculate benefits for non-class mem- 
bers, the date of the initial denied application or the date 
of the court’s decision. ld. 


While it is apparent that Plaintiffs are individually 
requesting relief identical to that sought by the proposed 
class, Plaintiffs maintain that class certification is neces- 
sary because of the expense of mounting a federal chal- 
lenge to Defendants’ electoral decision. This court agrees 
that class action is an appropriate mechanism for the 
instant case as courts have traditionally viewed electoral 
cases seeking declaratory or injunctive relief under 42 
U.S.C. § 1983 as well-suited for class action treatment. See 
Baker v. Carr, 369 U.S. 186 (1962) (class action suit by 
voters of various counties regarding the constitutionality 
of state apportionment law was Properly maintained 
under § 1983). 


Therefore, because this court has found that certifica- 
tion is appropriate based upon the requirements of Rule 
23, the nature of the allegations, and the relief sought, the 
court need not resolve whether the Ninth Circuit would 
require an additional element for class certification that is 
neither contained within the express language of Rule 23 
nor previously expressly adopted by the Ninth Circuit.6 


Rule 23(b)(2) states that a class may be certified if the 
“party opposing the class has acted or refused to act on 
grounds generally applicable to the class, thereby making 


® The court also notes at this point that the courts of appeals 
which have adopted the “lack of need” approach have done so 
when affirming the denials of class certification, as opposed to 
overruling a district court’s decision to certify a class. 


62a 


appropriate final injunctive relief or corresponding 
declaratory relief with respect to the class as a whole.” 
Fed. R. Civ. P. 23(b)(2). 


The thrust of Plaintiffs’ action is that the court issue 
declaratory and injunctive relief either enjoining Defen- 
dants from counting the blank ballots as “no” votes, thus 
requiring Defendants to certify that the con-con passed, 
or by ordering a new election. Consequently, for the 
above stated reasons and because the requirements of 
Rule 23 are satisfied, the court GRANTS Plaintiffs’ 
Motion for Class Certification. 


Il. Plaintiffs’ Motion for Summary Jucgrnent 


Rule 56(c) of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure 
provides that summary judgment shall be entered when: 


... the pleadings, depositions, answers to inter- 
rogatories, and admissions on file, together with 
the affidavits, if any, show that there is no genu- 
ine issue as to any material fact and that the 
moving party is entitled to a judgment as a 
matter of law. 


The moving party has the initial burden of “identify- 
ing for the court those portions of the materials on file 
that it believes demonstrate the absence of any genuine 
issue of material fact.” T.W. Elec. Serv., Inc. v. Pacific Elec. 
Contractors Ass’n, 809 F.2d 626, 630 (9th Cir. 1987) (citing 
Celotex Corp. v. Catrett, 477 U.S. 317, 323, 106 S. Ct. 2548, 
2553 (1986)). The movant must be able to show “the 
absence of a material and triable issue of fact,” Richards v. 
Neilsen Freight Lines, 810 F.2d 898, 902 (9th Cir. 1987), 
although it need not necessarily advance affidavits or 


63a 


similar materials to negate the existence of an issue on 
which the nonmoving party will bear the burden of proof 
at trial. Celotex, 477 U.S. at 323. But cf., id., at 328 (White, 
J., concurring). 


If the moving party meets its burden, then the oppo- 
sing party may not defeat a motion for summary judg- 
ment in the absence of any significant probative evidence 
tending to support his legal theory. Commodity futures 
Trading Comm'n v. Savage, 611 F.2d 270, 282 (9th Cir. 1979). 
The opposing party cannot stand on his pleadings, nor 
can he simply assert that he will be able to discredit the 
movant’s evidence at trial. See T.W. Elec., 809 F.2d at 630. 
Similarly, legal memoranda and oral argument are not 
evidence and do not create issues of fact capable of 
defeating an otherwise valid motion for summary judg- 
ment. British Airways Bd. v. Boeing Co., 585 F.2d 946, 952 
(9th Cir. 1978), cert. denied, 440 U.S. 981 (1979). Moreover, 
“if the factual context makes the nonmoving party’s claim 
implausible, that party must come forward with more 
persuasive evidence than would otherwise be necessary 
to show that there is a genuine issue for trial.” Franciscan 
Ceramics, 818 F.2d 1466, 1468 (9th Cir. 1987), (citing Mat- 
sushita Elec. Indus. Co., Ltd. v. Zenith Radio Corp., 475 U.S. 
574, 587 (1986)) (original emphasis). 


The standard for a grant of summary judgment 
reflects the standard governing the grant of a directed 
verdict. See Eisenberg v. Insurance Co. of North America, 815 
F.2d 1285, 1289 (9th Cir. 1987) (citing Anderson v. Liberty 
Lobby, Inc., 477 U.S. 242, 250 (1986)). Thus, the question is 
whether “reasonable minds could differ as to the import 
of the evidence.” Eisenberg, 815 F.2d at 1289. 


64a 


However, when “direct evidence” produced by the 
moving party conflicts with “direct evidence” produced 
by the party opposing summary judgment, “the judge 
must assume the truth of the evidence set forth by the 
nonmoving party with respect to that fact.” T.W. Elec., 809 
F.2d at 631. Also, inferences from the facts must be drawn 
in the light most favorable to the nonmoving party. Id. 
These inferences may be drawn both from underlying 
facts that are not in dispute, as well as from disputed 
facts which the judge is required to resolve in favor of the 
nonmoving party. Id. 


Plaintiffs claims can be summarized into one primary 
argument: that the Hawaii Supreme Court’s interpreta- 
tion of the election results was unforeseeable, and hence 
fundamentally unfair, According to Plaintiffs, this funda- 
mental unfairness is of constitutional magnitude and 
mandates either: (1) an order by this court directing 
Defendants to issue a certificate certifying that the con- 
con question on the 1996 general election ballot was 
approved by the voters of the State of Hawaii; or (2) an 
order for a new special election on the con-con question 
within sixty days pursuant to § 11-174.5 of the Hawaii 
Revised Statutes. 


A. Rooker-Feldman Doctrine 


The Rooker-Feldman doctrine embodies the 
principles set forth by the Supreme Court in 
District of Columbia Court of Appeals v. Feldman, 
460 U.S. 462 (1983), and Rooker v. Fidelity Trust 
Co., 263 U.S. 413 (1923): “lower federal courts 
lack subject matter jurisdiction to engage in 
appellate review of state court determinations 


65a 


or to evaluate constitutional claims that are 
inextricably intertwined with the state court's 
[decision] in a judicial proceeding.” 


Marks v. Stinson, 19 F.3d 873, 885 n.11 (3rd Cir. 1994) cert. 
denied, 513 U.S. 1111 (1995). However, the Rooker-Feldman 
doctrine does not apply where the plaintiffs are not par- 
ties to the state court action. Id. in the instant case, the 
named Plaintiffs were not parties to the original state 
court action, and therefore, they had no opportunity to 
raise their constitutional claims in the state circuit court. 
Furthermore, rather than seeking review of the merits of 
the State’s decision, Plaintiffs seek a determination of 
whether the decision violated their constitutional rights. 
Therefore, the Rooker-Feldman doctrine does not preclude 
judicial review in the instant case.” 


B. Fundamentally Unfair Election 


Plaintiffs argue that there is a constitutional right to 
vote in a fundamentally fair election. Defendants concede 
that Plaintiffs must prevail if the election was fundamen- 
tally unfair. Defendants’ Memo. In Opp. at 14-19. Plain- 
tiffs assert that based upon the reasoning of Roe v. State of 
Alabama, 43 F.3d 574 (11th Cir. 1995), cert. denied, __ US. 
—~ 116 S. Ct. 276 (1995), an election is fundamentally 
unfair if the State’s interpretation of the ultimate result of 
an election was unforeseeable to the average voter. 
Defendants argue that Roe, is not analogous to the instant 
case. 


? Defendants do not dispute that the Rooker-Feldman 
doctrine is inapplicable in this case. 


66a 


The Roe case involved Alabama’s procedures for 
counting absentee ballots in elections. According to the 
Alabama Code, a person voting by absentee ballot was 
required to execute an “affidavit” in the presence of a 
“notary public or other officer authorized to acknowledge 
oaths or two witnesses 18 years of age or older.” Id. at 
577. Once the proper procedures were followed, the bal- 
lots were to be counted as if the absentee voters were 
voting in person. On November 8, 1994, Alabama held a 
general election for several statewide offices including 
Chief Justice of the Supreme Court of Alabama and Trea- 
surer of the State of Alabama. Between 1000 and 2000 
absentee voter failed to properly complete their affi- 
davits, either by failing to have their signatures notarized 
or by failing to have them witnessed by two people. 
Because the affidavits were improperly completed, they 
were not counted in the election. Following the election, 
two absentee voters filed suit in the Circuit Court for 
Montgomery County, Alabama, seeking an order that the 
contested absentee ballots be counted. The circuit court 
granted the temporary restraining order stating that 
“{a]bsentee ballots may not be excluded from being counted 
because of lack of notarization or a lack of witnesses.” Id. 
(emphasis in original). The circuit court required certi- 
fication of the results of the election after the contested 
absentee ballots were included in the revised totals. 


Subsequently, a voter suing on behalf of himself and 
others similarly situated brought suit in federal court 
under 42 U.S.C. § 1983 seeking an injunction against the 
election officials to preclude them from complying with 
the circuit court’s order. The district court granted the 
injunction, finding that the past practice of the Alabama 


67a 


election officials to the general election had been to 
retrain from counting any absentee ballot that did not 
include notarization or the signatures of two witnesses. 
The district court concluded that the defendant election 
officials were violating the Fourteenth Amendment. The 
court ordered that the officials refrain from certifying any 
election results based on a vote count that included the 
contested absentee ballots. The election officials appealed 
to the Eleventh Circuit Court of Appeals. 


The Eleventh Circuit affirmed the district court’s 
decision. The court determined that although “federal 
courts do not involve themselves in ‘garden variety’ elec- 
tion disputes,” they should intervene where “the election 
process itself reaches the point of patent and fundamental 
unfairness.” Id. at 580. The court stated that “[s]uch a 
situation must go well beyond the ordinary dispute over 
the counting and marking of ballots.” Id. 


The court found that failing to exclude the contested 
absentee ballots constituted a post-election departure 
from previous practice in Alabama. The court based its 
holding that the post-election decision rendered the elec- 
tion fundamentally unfair upon the fact that the results 
were unforeseeable to the average voter. Specifically, the 
court stated, “[w]le consider it unreasonable to expect 
average voters and candidates to question the Secretary’s, 
the Attorney General’s, and the election officials’ inter- 
pretation and application of the statue, especially in light 
of its plain language,” Id. at 581. 


In the instant case, Plaintiffs argue that the Hawaii 
Supreme Court’s post-election determination that blank 


68a 


votes should be counted as “no” votes constitutes a retro- 
active validation of a controlling number of votes. They 
claim that based upon long-standing practice, the Attor- 
ney General's interpretation, the election officials’ inter- 
pretation and the election materials, it was completely 
unforeseeable for the public to know that blank votes 
would be counted as “no” votes. The court finds this case 
closely analogous to the Roe case, and finds the reasoning 
of the Roe court to be persuasive. If the voters casting a 
controlling number of blank ballots in the November 1996 
election could not reasonably have known the meaning of 
their ballots, the election was fundamentally unfair.* 


1. Foreseeabilty 


Plaintiffs assert that reasonable voters could not have 
been expected to know that their blank ballots on the con- 
con question would have been later interpreted as “no” 
votes by the Hawaii Supreme Court. In support of this 
claim, Plaintiffs state that the public was led to believe 
that blank votes would not be counted for any purpose 
by: (1) the past practice of elections officials never to 
count blank votes for any purpose; (2) the information

In opposition, Defendants argue that the decision of 
the Hawaii Supreme Court was foreseeable because: (1) it

linguistics at the University of Hawaii, believes that the 
Hawaii Supreme Court's interpretation was accurate; and 
(4) the fact that Plaintiffs misunderstood the law before 
the election does not make the election process unfair. 


As a threshold matter, the court notes that both Plain- 
tiffs and Defendants? spend significant amounts of time

decision, and indeed, would be violative of the Rooker-Feldman 
doctrine. Moreover, such a determination is wholly irrelevant 
and unnecessary for this court to reach a conclusion concerning 
foreseeability in this case. Indeed, the question here is not 
whether the Hawaii Supreme Court was correct or incorrect in 
its interpretation of the Hawaii Constitution. The Hawaii 
Supreme Court is in fact the ultimate authority on the 
interpretation of the state constitution, and this court is bound 
by and respects its view. This case is premised not upon the 
Hawaii Constitution, but upon the United States Constitution 


70a 


discussing the merits of the Hawaii Supreme Court's 
decision. The court does not have the authority to con- 
duct such review. This inquiry is limited to whether the 
constitutional rights of the citizens of Hawaii have been 
violated by a post-election decision regarding whether 
blank votes should be counted as “no” votes. 


Defendants have not come forth with evidence to 
refute Plaintiffs claims regarding the information dissem- 
inated prior to the election which informed voters that 
blank votes would not be counted. Instead, Defendants 
state that “there is no evidence that a significant number 
of voters saw a copy [of the Fact Sheets]” which were 
distributed to the public stating that blank votes would 
not count. They argue that as for information posted on 
the Internet, “there is no evidence that an appreciable 
number of voters saw that either.” Defendants’ Memo in 
Opp. at 16-17. 


However, it is undisputed that the Fact Sheets were 
distributed to numerous members of the voting public at 
the polling places and otherwise. The question is one of 
fundamental fairness, not whether Plaintiffs can prove 
conclusively that if all voters had been clearly informed 
the result would have been different. More important is 
the fact that the elections office prepared the “Fact Sheet” 


and centers upon the question of whether the election was 
fundamentally unfair as judged by federal constitutional 
standards. Therefore even if the State Supreme Court’s 
interpretation of how to count ballots should have always been 
as indicated in its recent decision, the question still remains 
whether the election was fundamentally unfair because voters 
were misled by elections officials and past practice that a 
different procedure would apply.

for distribution to all voters who requested information 
on voting, and for distribution to all poll workers who 
worked at the polls. Complaint at 8. The Purpose of the 
Fact Sheets was to answer voters’ questions regarding the 
constitutional convention. Id. The State elections office 
informed all voters and anyone who inquired, that in 
determining whether the con-con question would pass, 
blank ballots would not count for any purpose. Id. at 7. 
The elections office accomplished this orally, in writing 
and on the Internet. Based upon the elections office’s 
belief that blank votes would not count, it affirmatively 
prohibited Citizens for a Constitutional Convention from 
telling voters that blank ballots would count. 


Moreover, in the State of Hawaii, it has been the 
long-standing practice of the elections office not to count 
blank votes for any purpose. The Chief Elections Officer,

'© Mr. Yoshina is the Chief Elections Officer at the Elections

The relevant Hawaii Revised Statutes provides in its 
entirety as follows: 


Each contest or question on a ballot shall be 
counted independently as follows: 


(1) If the votes in a contest or question are 
equal to or less than the number to be 
elected or chosen for that contest or ques- 
tion, the votes for that contest or question 
shall be counted.

Though the elections officials interpreted this lan- 
Suage to mean that only ballots which were marked 
“yes” or “no” would be counted, the Hawaii Supreme 
Court later interpreted it differently. In its Opinion, the 


74a 


Hawaii Supreme Court interpreted the meaning of the 
language “ballots cast” and “upon such a question.” It 
also considered the intent of the framers of the Hawaii 
Constitution in reaching its decision and concluded that 
the phrase “ballots cast upon such a question” required 
counting all voting ballots which were turned over to 
elections officials without regard to whether they voted 
affirmatively, negatively or not at all on any particular 
question on the ballot.'* This court has no reason to 
determine the efficacy of the Hawaii Supreme Court's 
decision, and therefore will not address whether its deci- 
sion was reasonable.'* Rather, the issue is whether based 
upon the available information prior to the election, the 
average voter could have foreseen such an interpretation. 
This court concludes that the average voter could not.'4 


'2 Plaintiffs also contend that the Hawaii Supreme court

in a particular case depends on the particular wording of the

Rather than countering Plaintiffs’ evidence, Defen-

'S For this same reason, Defendants’ argument concerning
