In addition, Defendants argue at great length that 
state law was not well-settled, therefore, there could not 
have been a clear understanding among voters that blank 
ballots would not count. However, as mentioned previ- 
ously, it is much less likely that average voters could seek 
out, find, understand and consider statutes and constitu- 
tional provisions, including legislative history that quali- 
fied attorneys have taken years to learn to interpret. 
Moreover, the court agrees with the Eleventh Circuit that, 
it would be “unreasonable to expect average voters and 
candidates to question the [Chief Elections Officer’s], the 
Attorney General’s and the election officials’ interpreta- 
tion-and application of the [constitution and] statute” 
especially in light of the historical practice of the elec- 
tions office and the information which was disseminated 
to the public.’ This court finds, that based upon the 


Constitution, as interpreted by Byron W. Bender, Professor of 
Linguistics at the University of Hawaii, also fails. If determining 
the common usage of the phrase “ballots cast upon such a 
question” requires the interpretation of a professor of 
linguistics, that is strong evidence that the same is not readily 
knowable by an average voter. In fact, if a linguist’s 
interpretation varies markedly from that of the average voter, 
than there is a strong indication that Plaintiffs’ previous 
“misunderstanding of the law,” was unforeseeable. 


16 Defendants also argue that because one newspaper 
article appearing in the Honolulu Star Bulletin in 1966, reported 
that blank ballots would be counted as “no” votes on the con- 
con question, Plaintiffs cannot show that they were harmed by 
misinformation by election officials. However, the court finds a 
3-paragraph article written over 30 years ago by a lay reporter 
cannot counter the substantial inference to the contrary 
provided to voters by elections officials in this past election. 
Notwithstanding the article, the elections officials, in fact, did 


ET ye ee 


77a 


information distributed to voters prior to the election, 
and based upon the fact that blank votes have never been 
counted for any purpose, there would be no reason for 
voters to assume that their blank ballots would be coun- 
ted as “no” votes any more than they should assume that 
their blank votes would be counted as “yes” votes. There- 
fore, the court finds that the Hawaii Supreme Court's 
post-election decision rendered the election an the con- 
con question fundamentally unfair in violation of Plain- 
tiffs’ Fourteenth Amendment rights to due process of law. 


A state’s election laws which impairs First or Four- 
teenth Amendment rights of its citizens can survive con- 
stitutional scrutiny only by a showing that it addresses a 
compelling state interest and is narrowly tailored to serve 
that interest. Eu v. San Francisco County Democratic Cent. 
Comm., 489 U.S. 214, 222 (1989). Defendants have not 
come forth with any compelling interest advanced by the 
State. Their only argument is that the State’s interest in 
requiring an extraordinary majority to call a constitu- 
tional convention in order to initiate changes to our 
“basic law” is rationally related to a legitimate public 
purpose. While there is no doubt that Hawaii is “free to 
place reasonable time, place, and manner restrictions on 
voting” such as requiring a supermajority on a con-con 
question, Roe, 43 F.3d at 580, there is no compelling 
interest supporting its retroactive application in this case. 


not count blank votes as “no” votes in the 1966 election. Thus, 
even 30 years ago, a person reading the article would be led to 
believe that the newspaper was wrong, and indeed, blank votes 
would not be counted for any purpose. 


78a 


The action is insufficient to pass strict scrutiny. The only 
remaining question is one of remedy.'” 


Ill. Defendants’ Cross-Motion for Summary Judgment 


Defendants’ filed a cross-motion for summary judg- 
ment on the grounds that: (1) Plaintiffs lack standing; (2) 
this suit is barred by the Eleventh Amendment; and (3) 
the election was not fundamentally unfair.'* 


A. Standing 


While this court addressed this question in ruling on 
Plaintiffs’ motion for summary judgment, it will be 
briefly addressed again here as Defendants raise slightly 
different arguments on standing in support of their cross- 
motion for summary judgment. Defendants argue that 
Plaintiffs cannot demonstrate that they have sustained 
injury for purposes of standing because to do so, they 
would have to rely upon how they voted. Defendants 
contend that because this could not be proven by admis- 
sible, competent evidence, Plaintiffs do not have stand- 
ing. This argument lacks merit. 


The Supreme Court of the United States has stated, 
“The right of suffrage can be denied by a debasement or 


17 Because the court finds a constitutional deprivation, the 
court need not address Plaintiffs other arguments that the 
decision violated their substantive due process rights and equal 
protection rights. 


‘8 The court will not address this claim as it was previously 
addressed in the section regarding Plaintiffs’ Motion for 
Summary Judgment. 


79a 


dilution of the weight of the citizen’s vote just as effec- 
tively as by wholly prohibiting the free exercise at the 
franchise.” Reynolds v. Simms, 377 U.S. 533, 555 (1964). A 
voter's constitutional injury may arise not only by denial 
of the right to vote, but by the denial of a fundamentally 
fair election. Roe, 43 F.3d at 580. 


As indicated above when addressing this issue in 
Plaintiffs’ motion for summary judgment, Plaintiffs lost 
the right to a fundamentally fair election — a constitu- 
tional deprivation constituting an injury in and of itself. 
However, because the Hawaii Supreme Court's inter- 
pretation of the Hawaii Constitution resulted in a reversal 
of the election results, Plaintiffs also lost their right to a 
constitutional convention. Clearly, this is both a direct 
and significant injury as the opportunity for an election 
to call a constitutional convention in Hawaii only arises 
once every ten years. Once Plaintiffs are unconstitu- 
tionally deprived of the right to a fundamentally fair 
election on whether to have a con-con, they will not have 
another opportunity to exercise this right until the year 
2006. This is not a harm which is contingent upon future 
events. The harm followed immediately upon the 
recounting of the votes, and the certification that the con- 
con did not pass. Moreover, Plaintiffs have shown causa- 
tion because their injury was directly caused by specific 
acts of Defendants: (1) the reversal of Defendants’ prior 
certification that the con-con passed; (2) the counting of 
blank votes as “no” votes; and (3) the certification that 
the con-con passed. 


As a final effort, Defendants also make a slippery 
slope argument. They contend that this court should find 
that Plaintiffs lack standing because to do otherwise 


80a 


would be to expand judicial oversight to any election 
dispute which is brought into federal court. This argu- 
ment also lacks merit. 


This court recognizes that elections should be over- 
turned only in rare circumstances of clear constitutional 
deprivations. “Not every state election dispute, however, 
implicates the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth 
Amendment.” Roe, 43 F.3d at 580. The case presently 
before the court is not an ordinary election dispute. The 
post-election interpretation of the [sic] how blank votes 
should be counted on the con-con question was the first 
time in the history of Hawaii that a retroactive decision 
reversed the results of an election. Federal courts have a 
duty to uphold the United States Constitution, which is 
paramount to the Constitution of the State of Hawaii. 
This court would be derelict in its duties to the citizens of 
Hawaii, if it disregarded the Constitution when the citi- 
zens’ voting rights have been so severely impaired. The 
court finds that Plaintiffs have sufficiently demonstrated 
their injury in this case. Notwithstanding this, while the 
members of a class must have suffered injury, the injury 
requirement is applied more flexibly in the class action 
context. LaDuke v. Nelson, 762 F.2d 1318, 1325-26 (9th Cir. 
1985). Thus, this court finds that Plaintiffs have standing 
to bring the instant suit. 


B. Eleventh Amendment Immunity 


Plaintiffs have sued Defendants for constitutional 
violations as well as for violation of 42 U.S.C. § 1983. 
Defendants have asserted that the Eleventh Amendment 


8la 


doctrine of sovereign immunity provides a complete 
defense. The Eleventh Amendment reads: 


The Judicial power of the United States shall not 
be construed to extend to any suit in law or 
equity, commenced or prosecuted against one of 
the United States by Citizens of another State, or 
by Citizens or Subjects of any Foreiga State.19 


The Supreme Court has interpreted this Amendment to 
signify that “in the absence of consent, a suit in which the 
State or one of its agencies or departments is named as 
the defendant is proscribed[.]” Pennhurst State School & 
Hosp. v. Halderman, 465 U.S. 89, 100 (1984). Where state 
officials are the named defendants, the Eleventh Amend- 
ment bars the suit if “ ‘the state is the real, substantial 
party in interest.’ ” Pennhurst, 465 U.S. at 101, (citing Ford 
Motor Co. v. Dep't of Treasury of Indiana, 323 U.S. 459, 464 
(1945)). “Thus, ‘[t]he general rule is that relief sought 
nominally against an officer is in fact against the sover- 
eign if the decree would operate against the latter.’ ” Id. 
(citing Hawaii v. Gordon, 373 U.S. 57, 58 (1963)) 


Moreover, a plaintiff may not sue a state in either law 
or equity. Id. at 100-01 (citing Missouri v. Fiske, 290 U.S. 18, 
27 (1933) (“the Amendment necessarily embraces 
demands for the enforcement of equitable rights and the 
prosecution of equitable remedies when those are 
asserted and prosecuted by an individual against a 
State”)). Reading these two principles together, a suit 
against a state official that is in fact a suit against a State is 


'° The Supreme Court has held that this provision bars 
Suits against a state brought by its own citizens, as well as by 
foreign citizens. Papasan v. Allain, 478 U.S. 265, 276 (1986). 


82a 


barred regardless of whether the plaintiff seeks monetary 
or injunctive relief. [d. at 101-02 (citing Cory v. White, 457 
U.S. 85, 91 (1982)) (emphasis added). 


The Supreme Court has recognized an exception to 
this general rule: a plaintiff may challenge the constitu- 
tionality of a state official's action by seeking to enjoin 
the official from continuing to enforce allegedly uncon- 
stitutional state laws. Id. at 102 (citing Ex Parte Young, 209 
U.S. 123 (1908)). If the plaintiff asks the federal court to 
enjoin the official's future unconstitutional conduct, the 
Eleventh Amendment presents no bar, because uncon- 
stitutional actions by state officials cannot be authorized 
by a state. Edelman v. Jordan, 415 U.S. 651 (1974). How- 
ever, a plaintiff may not seek retroactive monetary relief 
against the state official for past allegedly unconstitu- 
tional behavior. Id. at 666-67. 


Simply asking for injunctive relief and not damages 
will not automatically allow the plaintiff. to overcome the 
dictates of the Eleventh Amendment. The Supreme Court 
has recognized that the difference between retrospective 
and prospective relief “will not in many instances be that 
between day and night.” Papasan, 478 U.S. at 278 (citing 
Edelman, 415 U.S. at 667). Generally, “[rjelief that in 
essence serves to compensate a party injured in the past by 
an action of a state official in his official capacity that was 
illegal under federal law is barred [by the Eleventh 
Amendment].” Papasan, 478 U.S. at 278 (emphasis added). 


It is clear that in the case at bar, Plaintiffs do not seek 
retroactive monetary relief against state officials for past 
conduct. Nor do they seek compensation for past injuries. 
Plaintiffs seek prospective injunctive and declaratory 


83a 


relief against Defendants with claims virtually identical 
to those of the plaintiffs in Roe and Griffin, 570 F.2d at 
1065 (affirming district court's reversal of state’s retroac- 
tive invalidation of absentee and shut-in ballots). More- 
over, there is no merit to Defendants’ argument that 
holding a new election would be an undue expense for 
the State.2° Plaintiffs seek to enjoin Defendants’ retroac- 
tive application of the Hawaii Supreme Court's ruling. 
Following this court's order, the Hawaii Supreme Court's 
interpretation of how blank votes should be counted on 
the con-con question can only be implemented prospec- 
tively — beginning with the next election. Thus, the court 
concludes that the Eleventh Amendment does not shield 
Defendants from relief in this case. 


a. Remedy 


In the Roe decision, the Eleventh Circuit determined 
that the state judicial decision resulted in a retroactive 
validation of a controlling number of votes. For that 
reason, a new election was not ordered, but rather the 
court enjoined the counting of those votes. The court in 
Griffin found that because the state court’s post-election 
decision was retroactive, it ordered a new primary elec- 
tion, 570 F.2d at 1080. 


In a similar vein, Plaintiffs seek reversal of the elec- 
tion results on the con-con question as the appropriate 


*° “The Eleventh Amendment does not bar the imposition 
of costs to secure compliance with prospective relief or costs 
that are incidental or ancillary to prospective relief.” Hook v. 
Arizona Dep't of Corrections, 107 F.3d 1397, 1403 (9th Cir. 1997). 


84a 


remedy for the constitutional violation. However, Plain- 
tiffs acknowledge that for due process violations, a new 
election may also be an appropriate remedy. Soules v. 
Kauaians for Nukolii Campaign Comm., 849 F.2d 1176, 1180 
(9th Cir. 1988) (“Although we have the power to invali- 
date constitutionally infirm elections, we take into 
account equitable considerations in fashioning the appro- 
priate remedy in each case.”). 


Defendants advance no equitable considerations in 
support of requiring a new election as opposed to a 
reversal of the election results. While this court may have 
the authority to order elections officials to certify the con- 
con question as having passed, to do so wou.d result in 
imposing the same unfairness upon the election that this 
suit has sought to correct. The Hawaii Supreme Court has 
now determined that the Hawaii Constitution requires 
that blank ballots cast are to be counted as “no” votes on 
the question. If this court orders elections officials to 
certify the question as having passed, the result will be 
the calling of a state Constitutional Convention based 
upon an election held in violation of the Hawaii Supreme 
Court’s interpretation of the State Constitution. While 
Hawaii voters could not have foreseen the Hawaii 
Supreme Court's recent ruling prior to the election, it is 
nonetheless the law of the State of Hawaii and must be 
respected. 


It is also true, for the reasons stated in this order, that 
to allow the election to remain certified as having failed 
passage would violate the United States Constitution’s 
guarantee of fundamental fairness in elections. This court 
concludes that the only appropriate, proper, and equita- 
ble remedy is to order a new election, and by that election 


85a 


give the people of Hawaii a fair Opportunity to vote upon 
the question of whether there should be a Constitutional 
Convention. In this new election, voters will be fully 
informed of the consequences of their vote. Those in 
favor of a Constitutional Convention, as well as those 
opposed to it, will have an equal and fair Opportunity to 
address voters prior to the election. Most importantly, the 
requirements of both the Hawaii State Constitution and 
the United States Constitution will be met. 


Therefore, Plaintiffs’ motion for Summary Judgment 
is hereby GRANTED, the results of the Constitutional 
Convention election held in 1996 are declared void, and 
Defendants are ordered to hold a new speciai election on 
the question of whether a Constitutional Convention 
Should be held. That election is ordered to take place 
within 60 days pursuant to HRS § 11-174.5. 


CONCLUSION 


For the reasons stated above, the court GRANTS 
Plaintiffs’ Motion for Class Certification, GRANTS Plain- 
tiffs’ Motion for Summary Judgment, DENIES Defen- 
dants’ Cross-Motion for Summary Judgment, and 
ORDERS a Special Election. 


IT IS SO ORDERED. 


DATED: Honolulu, Hawaii, July 10, 1997.

(Filed July 25, 1997) 
ORDER GRANTING IN PART AND DENYING IN PART 
DEFENDANTS’ MOTION TO STAY PENDING APPEAL 


The court heard Defendants’ Motion on July 25, 1997. 
Mark J. Bennett, Esq., appeared at the hearing on behalf 
of Plaintiffs; Deputy Attorney General John P. Dellers, 


87a 


appeared at the hearing on behalf of Defendants. After 
reviewing the motion and the supporting and opposing 
memoranda, the court GRANTS in part and DENIES in 
part Defendants’ Motion to Stay Pending Appeal. 


BACKGROUND 


The facts of this case have been fully set forth in this 
court’s Order Granting Plaintiffs’ Motion for Class Certi- 
fication and Granting Plaintiffs’ Motion for Summary 
Judgment and Denying Defendants’ Cross-Motion for 
Summary Judgment and Ordering Special Election filed 
on July 10, 1997. Those facts will not be repeated here. 


STANDARD OF REVIEW 


The Federal Rules of Civil Procedure provide the 
procedure for a court to order a stay of proceedings to 
enforce a judgment as follows: 


When an appeal is taken from an interlocutory 
or final judgment granting, dissolving, or deny- 
ing an injunction, the court in its discretion may 
suspend, modify, restore, or grant an injunction 
during the pendency of the appeal upon such 
terms as to bond or otherwise as it considers 
proper for the security of the rights of the 
adverse party. 


Fed. R. Civ. P. 62(c). The filing of a notice of appeal 
divests a district court of jurisdiction only over those 
aspects of the case involved in the appeal. Griggs v. 
Provident Consumer Discount Co., 459 U.S. 56, 58 (1982). A 
court has power to stay an injunction pending appeal, 


88a 


however, “the powers of the district court over an injunc- 
tion pending appeal should be limited to maintaining the 
status quo... . ” Coastal Corp. v. Texas E. Corp., 869 F.2d 
817, 820 (5th Cir. 1989). In order for a party to prevail in 
his motion for a stay or modification pending appeal, he 
must demonstrate: (1) the likelihood of prevailing on the 
merits on appeal; (2) the likelihood of suffering irrepar- 
able injury if the stay is denied; (3) the other parties 
involved will not be substantially harmed by the granting 
of the stay; and (4) the granting of the stay will serve the 
public interest. Stop H-3 Association v. Volpe, 353 F. Supp. 
14, 16 (D. Haw. 1972). 


A second standard requires that the moving party 
demonstrates either a combination of probable success on 
the merits and the possibility of irreparable injury or that 
serious questions are raised and the balance of hardships 
weighs sharply in his favor. Estate of Daily v. Title Guar. 
Escrow Service, Inc., 178 B.R. 837, 847 (D. Haw. 1995). 
Under the second analysis, the critical inquiry is weigh- 
ing the relative hardship to the parties if the stay is 
granted or denied. Lopez v. Heckler, 713 F.2d 1432, 1435 
(9th Cir. 1983). 


DISCUSSION 


I. Likelihood of Success on the Merits 


Defendants contend that they have a likelihood of 
success on the merits because: (1) the Eleventh Amend- 
ment bars this suit; and (2) this court erred in its deter- 
mination of facts essential to the judgment. 


89a 


A. Eleventh Amendment Immunity 


Once again, Defendants raise the argument that this 
suit is barred by the Eleventh Amendment. Defendants 
argue that this court erred in concluding that Plaintiffs’ 
claims were prospective, and by concluding that this case 
is not about past events. 


Though the Eleventh Amendment prevents a plaintiff 
from suing a state in law or equity, Pennhurst State School 
& Hosp. v. Halderman, 465 U.S. 89, 100 (1984), a plaintiff 
may challenge the constitutionality of a state official's 
action by seeking to enjoin the official from enforcing 
allegedly unconstitutional state laws. Id. at 102. The 
Supreme Court held that “the Amendment grants the 
States an immunity from retroactive monetary relief, [yet] 
it reaffirmed the principle that state officers are not 
immune from prospective injunctive relief.” Hutto v. Fin- 
ney, 437 U.S. 678, 690 (1978); Pennhurst, 465 U.S. at 103 
(“the federal court may award an injunction that governs 
the official’s future conduct, but not one that awards 
retroactive monetary relief.”). The Eleventh Amendment 
does not prevent a court from granting injunctive relief 
even though compliance with the injunction will cost the 
state large sums of money in the future. Quern v. Jordan, 
440 U.S. 332 (1979). 


The Supreme Court has determined that prospective 
or ancillary relief is not barred by the Eleventh Amend- 
ment. Graham v. Richardson, 403 U.S. 365 (1971) (ordering 
injunction compelling state officers to grant welfare bene- 
fits to otherwise qualified recipients who were aliens 
even though it cost the state treasuries large sums of 


90a 


money). The Pennhurst Court explained that the distinc- 
tion between prospective and retroactive relief “will not 
in many instances be that between day and night.” Penn- 
hurst, 465 U.S. at 105. However, the Eleventh Amendment 
is concerned with retroactive relief that will be paid by 
the state, while prospective relief is simply an order to 
comply with the law, where the costs are incidental to the 
state’s obligation to act in accordance with federal law. 
Erwin Chemerinsky, Federal Jurisdiction 397 (2d Ed. 
1994).} 


The Hawaii Supreme Court rendered its decision on 
how blank votes should be counted, months after the 
November election had passed. Once the Hawail 
Supreme Court retroactively applied its interpretation of 
how blank votes should be counted on the con-con ques- 
tion, the citizens of the State of Hawaii were immediately 
deprived of their right to a fundamentally fair election on 
the con-con question. This harm is ongoing. The harm 
continues until the voters of Hawaii are granted a funda- 
mentally fair election on the con-con question. The next 
election in which the con-con question will be placed on 
the ballot is in the year 2006. Surely this court is not 


! For instance in Milliken v. Bradley, 433 U.S. 267 (1977), the 
Supreme Court upheld a school desegregation order, including 
several remedial and compensatory education programs which 
required the expenditure of state funds. The court held that the 
educational components were ordered into effect prospectively, 
and were designed to extinguish continuing conditions of 
inequality produced by the unequal school system maintained 
in Detroit. The court found that the plan “operate[d] 
prospectively to bring about the delayed benefits of a unitary 
school system,” Id. at 289-290 (emphasis in original). 


Se 


9la 


without authority to remedy a constitutional harm which 
is presently occurring and scheduled to continue for the 
next ter years. 


Taking Defendants’ argument to its logical conclu- 
sion, states would, for instance, be free to retroactively 
enact and apply criminal laws to individuals who had no 
prior notice that their acts would later be considered 
crimes. Even under such circumstances of egregious con- 
stitutional violations, federal courts would be without 
power to enjoin retroactive aplication of the statute 
because it is considered a past event. Plaintiffs in this 
case have sought to enjoin this present, and potentially 
future harm of deprivation of a fundamentally fair elec- 
tion. The Eleventh Amendment does not present a bar.2 


B. Facts Essential to Judgment on the Merits 


Defendants argue that this court erred in concluding 
that “Fact Sheets” were distributed to numerous mem- 
bers of the voting public at the polling places and other- 
wise, and that the elections officials informed the public 
that blank votes would not count on the con-con ques- 
tion. Rather, Defendants contend that “precinct informa- 
tion sheets,” which were not included in the record, were 
distributed to voters. 


* Moreover, the Eleventh Amendment does not bar suits for 
declaratory relief where the plaintiff claims a continuing 
Violation of federal law or a threat of future violation. Green v. 
Mansour, 474 U.S. 64, 72-73 (1985). Thus, the Eleventh 
Amendment does not preclude declaratory relief as an 
appropriate remedy in this case. 


92a 


Defendants argue semantics. Both the fact sheets and 
the precinct information sheets? contained very similar 
information regarding how their votes would be counted. 
The Chief Elections Officer testified at deposition that the 
purpose of that information was to inform the public of 
how the votes would be counted on the con-con question. 
Yoshina Depo. at 42. In addition, he testified that the 
polling and elections personnel were given the informa- 
tion to inform all members of the public who inquired, 
that blank votes would not be counted for the con-con 
question. Id. at 11-13. In any event, Defendants seek to 
dissect these facts from the whole. Given the widespread 
and uniform elections procedures including: (1) distribut- 
ing the fact sheets, (2) distributing the precinct informa- 
tion sheets, (3) posting information on a web page on the 
Internet, (4) state-wide distribution to all print and elec- 
tronic media, (5) prohibiting Citizens for a Constitutional 
Convention from telling voters that blank votes would, in 
fact, count, and (.) informing all inquiring voters that 
blank votes would not count, a reasonable voter would 
have no reason to disbelieve the affirmative misrepresen- 
tations of the elections office. 


Defendants also dispute this court’s determination 
that it has been the long-standing practice of the elections 
office not to count blank votes for any purpose. This 
argument fails as the evidence was supported by the 
deposition testimony of the Chief Elections Officer, 
Dwayne Yoshina, who is well seasoned and qualified to 


3 A copy of the precinct information sheet was attached to 
Plaintiffs’ Motion for Summary Judgment and Permanent 
Injunction as Exhibit 8. 


93a 


testify as to the past practice of the elections office. More- 
over, at the hearing, Defendants’ counsel specifically 
admitted that the Attorney General took the position 
before the Hawaii Supreme Court that it was clear that 
under the Hawaii Constitution, blank votes are not to be 
counted on the con-con question. Because the evidence 
concerning the fundamental unfairness of the election 
was overwhelming, this court found a constitutional vio- 
lation to exist. Under the facts and circumstances of this 
case, the court finds that Defendants have a marginal 
likelihood of success on the merits. 


Il. Injury or Hardship to Defendants if Stay is Not 
Granted 


Defendants only argument regarding potential harm 
if the stay is not granted is as follows: 


In the absence of a Stay, the State will be 
required to hold a new con-con vote before the 
Court of Appeals can consider its appeal, since 
once the vote is conducted, prosecution of the 
appeal would be pointless. If the voters rejected 
the call for a convention, the controversy would 
be at an end. If they approved the call, it would 
be impractical for Defendants to prosecute their 


appeal. 


Motion at 10. Defendants have not demonstrated how 
any of the aforementioned results would thwart their 
ability to appeal. If the election is conducted prior to the 
Court of Appeals’ ruling on the issue, and the con-con is 
approved, Defendants would still be eniitied to appeal. In 
fact, Defendants would have ample time to have their 
appeal heard prior to the convening and resolution of the 


94a 


actual constitutional convention. In addition, if the con- 
con was rejected, an appeal would be unnecessary. While 
the election itself may cause inconvenience and some 
expense, it in no way rises to the level of irreparable 
harm alleged by Defendants. For example, the State legis- 
lature has provided for special elections to be held in the 
event of a death of a Congressman. See H.R.S. § 17-2. 
While the expense associated with such an event may be 
inconvenient? and costly, it in no way constitutes irrepar- 
able injury. The expenditure of funds, or the difficulties 
that the Elections Office would encounter in conducting 
the election do not constitute irreparable injury. 


III. Harm to Plaintiffs if Stay is Granted 


Defendants claim that the harm to Plaintiffs is mini- 
mal if a stay is granted because the earliest that delegates 
could be elected is November 1998. Because the conven- 
tion must convene “not less than five months prior to the 
next regularly scheduled election,” the proposed amend- 
ments could be submitted to the voters no sooner than 
November of 2000. Thus, Defendants argue, a stay of the 
judgment pending appeal would not cause substantial 
harm to Plaintiffs. This argument is without merit. 


If a special election is held in the fall of 1997, the 
legislature will have enough to [sic] time to allow for the 


4 The court notes that Mr. Yoshina testified at the hearing 
that conducting the special election is not an inconvenience, as 
it is his job to conduct such elections.

election of delegates in May 1998, allow for the conven-

On the other hand, Plaintiffs would be subjected to 
substantial hardship if the stay is granted. This court 
agrees that denying the citizens of Hawaii their oppor- 
tunity to vote on this important issue in a fundamentally 
fair election constitutes a hardship far in excess of the 
consequent administrative and cost burden on the State. 
The citizens’ fundamental voting rights are paramount to 
the State’s interest in saving time and money. 


Delay of the election until the appeal is complete and 
decided could potentially take anywhere from one to 
three years. The entire process of approving a con-con, 
electing the delegates convening a convention, and 
approving or disapproving amendments is time-consum- 
ing in and of itself. This burden is substantial as even 


° Amicus Curiae Republican Party of Hawaii submitted a

special election were held in 1997, a Constitutional Convention

Attached to the memorandum as Exhibit A is a Declaration of

postponing the con-con vote until the 1998 general elec-

these reasons, the court finds that Defendants’ request to 
stay the proceedings pending the decision on appeal or 
even until the 1998 general election, is entirely unreason- 
able. This court rejects that recommendation. 


IV. Public Interest 


Defendants argue that the public interest would be 
served by staying the election pending appeal. Speci- 
fically, Defendants point to the declaration of Mr. Yoshina 
who testifies that the 60-day time-table for special elec- 
tions established by the state Legislature in H.R.S. 
§ 11-174.5(b) is unreasonable in this case because it would 
require a great expenditure of time and resources.® Fur- 
thermore, it would require curtailing of elections activ- 
ities including installation of an updated version of its 
present ballot counting system, adoption of new rules 


® At the hearing, Defendants suggested that substantial 
funds and resources would be spent on voter education and 
information. This argument is without support however, as the 
public has been well-informed of the only question that will be 
placed on the ballot - whether a convention should be 
convened. Yoshina testified that much of the work for a special 
election has already been completed. For instance, 70% of the 
polling places have been committed for the election, and 
revising fact -heets and modifying web sites take about one 
week. Most of the preparation for a special election would take 
from 2 to 4 months. It appears that bulk of the burden is not the 
time or expense but the taxing of resources on Yoshina’s staff of 
18 persons. However, Yoshina added that the elections office 
works closely with the county clerks who help put on elections. 


97a 


and regulations, and installation of a new election infor- 
mation management system. However, other than making 
ballot counting more efficient, Yoshina’s testimony did 
not explain how these activities benefit the public at 
large, nor why these activities could not continue after 
the special election. 


Yoshina testified at the hearing that while he did not 
think a special election could not be conducted properly 
by September 9, 1997, he believes that one could be 
conducted within 120 days of this court's grant of the 
injunction, or November of 1997. This court has previ- 
ously determined that the voters of Hawaii were denied 
the right to participate in a fundamentally fair election, in 
which the only equitable remedy is to hold a new elec- 
tion. As such, the public interest favors enjoining that 
constitutional violation as soon as possible. Moreover, if 
the citizens of Hawaii do approve a Constitutional Con- 
vention, then the public interest also favors holding that 
convention as soon as possible. The public interest in 
having an election which comports with the United States 
Constitution outweighs the public interest in for instance, 
installation of an updated version of the present ballot 
counting system. 


Under either standard for granting a stay, the balance 
of the equities tips sharply in favor of the public interest 
in having a fundamentally fair election on whether a 
Constitutional Convention should be convened. 


This court was indeed required to look to state law in 
determining when to hold a special election. Title 42 of 
the United States Code § 1988 provides in relevant part: 


98a 


The jurisdiction in civil and criminal matters 
conferred on the district courts by the provi- 
sions of this Title, and of the Title “CIVIL 
RIGHTS,” and of Title “CRIMES,” for the pro- 
tection of all persons in the United States in 
their civil rights, . . . shall be exercised and 
enforced in conformity with the laws of the United 
States, so far as such laws are suitable to carry 
the same into effect. . . . but in all cases where they 
are not adapted to the object, or are deficient in the 
provisions necessary to furnish suitable 
remedies .. . the common law, . . . and statutes of 
the State... shall be extended to and govern the said 
courts in the trial and disposition of the cause... . 


42 U.S.C. § 1988. In this case, federal law did not provide 
an explicit remedy, therefore, the court appropriately 
looked to state law. However, the court has equitable 
latitude under federal law to modify or suspend an 
injunction pending appeal where appropriate. See Fed. R. 
Civ. P. 62(c). The court finds that the public interest is not 
substantially burdened by a limited suspension of the 
injunction, therefore the special election will be held no 
later than December 2, 1997.7 Thus, the court GRANTS in 
part and DENIES in part, Defendants’ Motion for Stay 
Pending Appeal. 


? The court notes that on the one hand the Defendants take 
issire with this court’s ruling arguing that this court has 
somehow interfered with “states rights” and ignored state law. 
On the other hand, Defendants argue that this court should 
disregard the plain language of the state statute which provides 
for a special election within 60 days. See H.R.S. § 11-174.5(b).

99a 


CONCLUSION 


For the reasons stated above, the court GRANTS in 
part and DENIES in part Defendants’ Motion to Stay 
Pending Appeal. 


IT IS SO ORDERED. 


DATED: Honolulu, Hawaii, July 25, 1997.

(Filed Sep. 26, 1997) 


ORDER DENYING DEFENDANTS’ 
MOTION FOR RELIEF FROM JUDGMENT 


Pursuant to Local Rule 220-2(d), the court finds this 
matter suitable for disposition without a hearing. After 
reviewing the motion and the supporting and opposing 


105a 


memoranda, the court DENIES Defendants’ Motion for 
Relief from Judgment. 


BACKGROUND 


The facts of this case have been fully set forth in this 
court’s Order Granting Plaintiffs’ Motion for Class Certi- 
fication and Granting Plaintiffs’ Motion for Summary 
Judgment and Denying Defendants’ Cross-Motion for 
Summary Judgment and Ordering Special Election filed 
on July 10, 1997 (July 10, 1997 Order). Those facts will not 
be repeated here. 


The only facts pertinent to the instant motion are as 
follows. The judgment was entered in this case on July 11, 
1997, one day after the July 10, 1997 Order was filed. 
Defendants’ notice of appeal was filed four days after 
judgment was entered. On August 1, 1997, Defendants 
filed a Motion for Relief from Judgment and for Hearing 
on Motion,} 


It is well settled that “when a judgment is appealed, 
| jurisdiction over the case passes to the appellate court. 
; The filing of a notice of appeal generally divests the 
district court of jurisdiction over the matters appealed.” 
McClatchy Newspapers v. Central Valley Typographical Union 
: No. 46, 686 F.2d 731, 734 (9th Cir.), cert. denied, 459 US. 
1071 (1982); Davis v. United States, 667 F.2d 822, 824 (9th 
| Cir. 1982). Certain exceptions to this rule have been rec- 
ognized. The district court may act to assist the court of 
éppeals in the exercise of its jurisdiction; the district court 


' The court notes that this motion cannot be construed as a 
Rule 59 motion as it -as not filed within the 10-day time limit. 


106a 


may retain jurisdiction under specific statutory authority; 
or where an appeal is taken from an order granting or 
denying an injunction, the district court may suspend, 
modify, restore, or grant an injunction to preserve the 
status quo. McClatchy Newspapers, 686 F.2d at 734. 


None of these circumstances are present in the 
instant case. Rather, Defendants ask this court to recon- 
sider the issues determined in the July 10, 1997 Order 
currently on appeal. This court has no jurisdiction to 
dispose of the motion without a remand from the Ninth 
Circuit. Gould v. Mutual Life Ins. Co., 790 F.2d 769 (9th Cir. 
1986) (filing of notice of appeal divests district court of 
jurisdiction to consider motion to vacate judgment); Smith 
v. Lujan, 588 F.2d 1304, 1307 (9th Cir. 1979) (same). 


Without explanation, Defendants cite the case of 
Stone v. Immigration and Naturalization Serv., 514 U.S. 386 
(1995), for the assertion that this court nevertheless has 
jurisdiction to consider the Rule 60(b) motion. This court 
disagrees. 


In Stone, the Court held that under the Immigra- 
tion and Nationality Act (“INA”), a timely 
motion for reconsideration of a Board of Immi- 
gration Appeals decision does not toll the run- 
ning of the statute of limitations for filing an 
appeal in federal court. The Court clarified that 
its ruling “depart[ed] trom the ordinary judicial 
treatment of agency orders under reconsidera- 
tion,” because Congress intended INA to be dif- 
ferent from other judicial review statutes. 


Acura of Bellevue v. Reich, 90 F.3d 1403, 1407 n.1 (9th Cir. 
1996). Thus, Stone is pertinent only insofar as it relates to 
the finality of agency decisions involving immigration 


107a 


matters. See Pablo v. INS, 72 F.3d 110, 113 (9th Cir. 1995) 
(explaining how Stone overruled Padilla-Agustin v. INS, 
21 F.3d 970, (9th Cir. 1994) and Ogio v. INS, 2 F.3d 959, 
(9th Cir. 1993)). This case contains no such issues. In fact, 
Stone articulated the rule Stating that: “[o]ther motions, 
such as Rule 60(b) motions filed more than 10 days after 
judgment, do not affect the finality of a district court's 
judgment, either when filed before the appeal (no toll- 
ing), or afterwards (appellate court jurisdiction not 
divested).” Stone, 514 US. at 1548 (emphasis added). 
Because this court, rather than the appellate court has 
been divested of jurisdiction by the filing of the appeal, 
this court lacks the authority to hear the Rule 60(b) 
motion absent a remand from the Ninth Circuit. 


CONCLUSION 


For the reasons stated above, the court DENIES 
Applicants’ Motion for Relief from Judgment. 


IT IS SO ORDERED. 


DATED: Honolulu, Hawaii, SEP 26 1997. 


/s/ David A. Ezra 
DAVID ALAN EZRA 
UNITED STATES DISTRICT JUDGE

ORDER DENYING DEFENDANTS’ 
EX-PARTE MOTION FOR AN ORDER 
SHORTENING TIME AND FOR EXPEDITED HEARING; 


Pursuant to Local Rule 7.2 (d), the court finds this 
matter suitable for disposition without a hearing. After 
reviewing Defendants’ motion and the supporting mem- 
oranda, the court DENIES Defendants’ Ex-Parte Motion 


109a 


for an Order Shortening Time and for Expedited Hearing 
and DENIES Defendants’ Motion to Extend Time for Fil- 
ing Notice of Appeal. 


The court finds that Defendants have not shown 
good cause or excusable neglect to warrant an extension 
of time to file an appeal under Federal Rule of Appellate 
Procedure 4(a)(5). The court notes that Defendants have 
already filed a notice of appeal of the court’s order deny- 
ing Defendants’ motion for reconsideration on September 
29, 1997. However, Defendants voluntarily withdrew 
their notice of appeal on October 3, 1997.1 As Defendants 
| clearly had sufficient time to file a notice of appeal of the 
court’s order, the court finds that it is inappropriate to 
grant Defendants a further extension of time under Rule 
4(a)(4). The court also finds that an extension of time is 
not warranted in light of the fact that Defendants’ appeal 
on the merits of this case has already been granted by the 
| Ninth Circuit and the Ninth Circuit has established an 
expedited briefing schedule for this case. 


Accordingly, the court DENIES Defendants’ Ex-Parte 
Motion for an Order Shortening Time and for Expedited 
Hearing and DENIES Defendants’ Motion to Extend Time 
for Filing Notice of Appeal.

second motion, Defendants do not raise any new arguments 
which were not previously addressed by the court in its order 
Denying Defendants’ motion for Reconsideration, filed 
September 26, 1997.

APPENDIX I 
IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT

DWAYNE D. YOSHINA, CHIEF 
ELECTION OFFICER OF THE 
STATE OF HAWAII; OFFICE OF 
ELECTIONS, STATE OF HAWAII; 
MAZIE K. HIRONO, 
LIEUTENANT GOVERNOR OF

ltl 


(Filed Nov. 18, 1997) 


ORDER DENYING DEFENDANTS’ 
MOTION FOR RECONSIDERATION 


Pursuant to Local Rule 7.2 (d), the court finds this 
matter suitable for disposition without a hearing. After 


112a 


reviewing Defendants’ motion and the supporting mem- 
oranda, the court DENIES Defendants’ Motion for Recon- 
sideration. 


BACKGROUND 


On September 26, 1997, this court issued an order 
Denying Defendants’ Motion for Relief from Judgment. 
In the September 26 order, the court held that it had no 
jurisdiction to consider Defendants’ motion as Defen- 
dants had already filed a notice of appeal with the Ninth 
Circuit. On October 3, 1997, Defendants filed the instant 
Motion for Reconsideration of the court’s September 26, 
1997 Order, contending that the court did have jurisdic- 
tion to consider Defendants’ Motion for Relief from Judg- 
ment. 


STANDARD OF REVIEW 


The disposition of a motion for reconsideration is 
within the discretion of the district court and will not be 
reversed absent an abuse of discretion. Plotkin v. Pacific 
Telephone & Telegraph Co., 688 F.2d 1291, 1292 (9th Cir. 
1982) There in a “compelling interest in the finality of 
judgments which should not be lightly disregarded.” 
Rogers v. Watt, 722 F.2d 456, 459 (9th Cir. 1983). 


The Ninth Circuit has articulated two goals that must 
be accomplished in order for a motion for reconsideration 
to succeed: “First, a motion for reconsideration must 
demonstrate some reason why the court should recon- 
sider its prior decision. Second, a motion for reconsidera- 
tion must set forth facts or law of a strongly convincing 


113a 


nature to induce the court to reverse its prior decision.” 
Stein v. State Farm Mutual Automobile Insurance Company, 
934 F. Supp. 1171, 1173 (D. Haw. 1996) Courts have estab- 
lished only three grounds justifying reconsideration: 1) 
an intervening change in controlling law; 2) the availabil- 
ity of new evidence; and, 3) the need to correct clear error 
or to prevent manifest injustice. Great Hawaiian Financial 
Corp. v. Aiu, 116 E.R.D. 612, 616 (D. Haw. 1987), rev’d on 
other grounds, 863 F.2d 517 (9th Cir. 1988). 


DISCUSSION 


In support of their Motion for Reconsideration, 
Defendants only make one argument. Defendants assert 
that this court incorrectly interpreted Stone v. Immigration 
and Naturalization Service, 514 U.S. 386 (1995), and that if 
the court had correctly interpreted the Stone case, the 
court would have concluded that it had jurisdiction to 
consider Defendants’ Motion for Relief from Judgment. 
Defendants make no other arguments and present no 
other evidence in support of their Motion for Recon- 
sideration. 


In the September 26 order, this court quoted at length 
from the Stone decision and thoroughly discussed its 
impact on other Ninth Circuit cases which had concluded 
that an appeal generally divests a district court of juris- 
diction to consider a Rule 60(b) motion. As noted in the 
September 26 order, the Stone case “held that under the 
Immigration and Nationality Act (“INA”), a timely 
motion for reconsideration of a Board of immigration 
Appeals decision does not toll the running of the statute 
of limitations for filing an appeal in federal court.” Acura 


114a 


of Bellevue v. Reich, 90 F.3d 1403, 1407 n.1 (9th Cir. 1996). 
That was the holding of the Stone decision. 


In dicta, the Court discussed the effect of an appeal 
on a Rule 60(b) motion. However, even in this dicta, the 
Court was only discussing whether the appellate court had 
jurisdiction once a Rule 60(b) motion was filed in district 
court. See Stone, 514 U.S. at 401-03. The Stone decision 
does not therefore support Defendants’ contention that 
this court has jurisdiction to consider Defendants’ Motion 
for Relief from Judgment. 


Instead, this court is bound by the Ninth Circuit 
cases which have directly addressed this issue and have 
concluded that the filing of an appeal generally divests 
the court of jurisdiction to consider a Rule 60(b) motion. 
See, e.g., Bermudez v. Duenas, 936 F.2d 1064, 1068 (9th Cir. 
1991) (“The filing of a notice of appeal generally divests 
the district court of jurisdiction over the matters 
appealed.”); Zambrano v. Tafollau, 885 F.2d 1473, 1476 n.7 
(9th Cir. 1989); Donovan v. Mazzola, 761 F.2d 1411, 1414 
(9th Cir. 1984) (“The Ninth Circuit follows the general 
rule, with some exceptions, that the filing of a notice of 
appeal divests the district court of jurisdiction over the 
matters appealed.”); Miranda v. Southern Pacific Transpor- 
tation Co., 710 F.2d 516, 519 (9th Cir. 1983); Davis v. United 
States, 667 F.2d -822, 824 (9th Cir. 1982). The court there- 
fore concludes that the September 26 Crder Denying 
Defendants’ Motion for Relief from Judgment was not 
clearly erroneous. 


However, even if the court were to address the merits 
of Defendants’ Motion for Relief from Judgment, the 
court would still conclude that Defendants’ motion 


~" Peers eh es ee eee | ees owe 
o ore pe ee ey eS Te a eee ee Dav RoS Seals hal ite aa Aaa feos a x 
TRE Bhi BEM RATHI GTS TR : 


115a 


should be denied. On July 11, 1997, this court issued an 
order Granting Plaintiffs’ Motion for Class Certification 
and Granting Plaintiffs’ Motion for Summary Judgment 
and Denying Defendants’ Cross-Motion for Summary 
Judgment and Ordering Special Election. In their motion 
for reconsideration, Defendants only address the court’s 
decision to grant Plaintiffs’ Motion for Summary Judg- 
ment. 


In granting Plaintiffs’ motion for summary judgment, 
this court concluded that, because the average voter had 
no way of knowing that blank ballots would be counted 
as no votes prior to the con-con election, the election was 
fundamentally unfair and in violation of Plaintiffs’ Four- 
teenth Amendment right to due process. See Order, filed 
July 10, 1997, at 30. Defendants contend that the court’s 
decision was clearly erroneous in light of the fact that less 
than 25 voters actually saw the Fact Sheet prepared by 
the Office of Elections, which indicated that blank votes 
would not be counted in the election. 


In making this argument, however, Defendants 
entirely discount the fact that the court’s decision was not 
based exclusively on a finding that the voters who read 
the Fact Sheet were mistakenly informed that blank votes 
would not be counted in the election. The court relied on 
a number of additional factors in concluding that the _ 
counting of blank votes as no votes rendered the election 
fundamentally unfair. Prior to the con-con election in 
1996, blank votes had never been counted in a con-con 
election. As Chief Eleciions Officer Mr. Dwayne Yoshina 
testified, blank ballots were not counted on the con-con 


11l6a 


question in either the 1976 or 1986 election. See, Deposi- 
tion of Mr. Yoshina, p. 16. The court also based its deci- 
sion on the fact that Section 11-151 of the Hawaii Revised 
statutes states that “[i]f a contest or question requires a 
majority of the votes for passage, any blank, spoiled, or 
invalid ballot shall not be tallied for passage or as votes 
cast except that such ballots shall be counted as votes cast 
in a ratification of a constitutional amendment.” H.R.S. 
§ 11-151 (emphasis added). Finally, the court relied on 
language from the Hawaii Constitution which provides 
that, in a con-con election, “[i]f a majority of the ballots 
cast upon such a question be in the affirmative, delegates 
to the convention shall be chosen at the next regular 
election. ... ” Haw. Const. Art. XVII § 2. Based on this 
factual record, the court concluded that, prior to the 
election, the average voter could not have foreseen that 
blank ballots would be counted as no votes. Conse- 
quently, the court held that the Hawaii Supreme Court's 
post-election decision to count the blank ballots as no 
votes resulted in a violation of Plaintiffs’ Fourteenth 
Amendment right to due process. 


In their motion for reconsideration, Defendants do 
not dispute the fact that prior election procedures, the 
Hawaii Revised Statutes, advice from the State’s election 
office, and the Hawaii Constitution led the average voter 
to believe that blank ballots would not be counted in the 
con-con election.! Instead, Defendants solely focus on the 


1 Indeed even the Attorney General of the State of Hawaii 
took the unequivocal position that blank ballots should not be 
counted for any purpose in an election. See Attorney General 
Opinion, no. 88-5. 


117a 


fact that only a few of the voters saw the Fact Sheet 
prepared by the Office of Elections which informed 
voters that blank ballots would not be counted in the 
election. Even if the court were to consider this new 
evidence, and accept it as true, this would not change the 
court’s opinion that, prior to tre Hawaii Supreme Court’s 
post-election decision, the average voter had no way of 
predicting that a blank ballot would be counted as a no 
vote. In fact, the information available to voters prior to 
the election suggested that a blank vote would not be 
counted at all. Therefore, by retroactively counting blank 
ballots as no votes, Plaintiffs’ Fourteenth Amendment 
rights to due process were violated in the most serious 
way — by denying Plaintiffs the right to know in advance 
how their votes would be counted - their right to a fair 
election was lost. 


Under the Fourteenth Amendment, voters are guar- 
anteed the right to know, in advance, the impact of their 
vote. See Jones v. Bates, No. 97-15914, 1997 WL 629803 (9th 
Cir. October 7, 1997). In the Jones decision, the Ninth 
Circuit invalidated the legislative-term-limits provision 
of Proposition 140. The court based its decision on the 
fact that: 


ballot materials failed adequately to advise the 
voters of [Proposition 140’s] principal and most 
drastic effect. Thc >eople were required to vote 
on an initiative measure without adequate 
notice of the Severity of the burden it imposed 
on their fundamental voting rights - a measure 
that did not clearly inform them that they were 
being asked to adopt a lifetime ban on future 
legislative service by the persons they choose to 
be their representatives. Moreover, it is likely 


acai SM ea a ae ey ee | 


118a 


that the number of voters who were riot aware 
of the initiative’s principal effect was suffi- 
ciently large that it could have affected the out- 
come of the election. We conclude that under 
these circumstances, even if a majority of the 
voters of the state of California may, uncer other 
circumstances, lawfully decide to adopt lifetime 
legislative term limits, the passage at Proposi- 
tion 140 did not Constitutionally achieve that 
objective. 


ig. at *3zZ. 


In the Jones decision, the court focused on the process 
by which the voters were informed of their voting rights. 
As the court explained, “we carefully scrutinize the pro- 
cess by which initiatives, such as Proposition 140 .. . are 
enacted. We must do so in order to ensure that sufficient 
notice has been given that fundamental rights will be so 
burdened. Simply put, we believe that the careful scru- 
tiny required by Burdick must apply to the process as well 
as the effect of such laws.” Id. at *13. “Furthermore, when 
fundamental, constitutional rights, such as voting rights, 
are at stake, basic tenets of procedural due process 
require states to adopt minimally burdensome safeguards 
if there is a significant risk of an erroneous deprivation of 
that right.” Id. as *17.2 


2 The court recogn izes that the Jones case is in the process of 
en banc review. How ‘ver, regardless of the outcome of that 
review, the principles stated in Jones, with regard to a voter’s 
fundamental right to be informed of the effect of his or her vote 
before an election, are well-established. 


Sate abc RE Nae ES Big ESR REY SP NEB, Fan Ng RE RY! Calan sichet hh Le RARE Monee SS Dia VANE Ce: 


id TRY et Sait 


EAGER ALAS RSE a et 


Bae peta elt 


119a 


As the Jones opinion suggests, voters must be ade- 
quately informed of their voting rights prior to any elec- 
tion. In this case, the average voter had no way of 
knowing, prior to the con-con election, that blank ballots 
would be counted as no votes. Consequently, by counting 
the blank ballots as no votes, Plaintiffs were denied due 
process under the Fourteenth Amendment. The court 
therefore concludes that its prior decision granting Plain- 
tiffs’ Motion for Summary Judgment was not in clear 
error. Accordingly, the court denies Defendants’ Motion 
for Reconsideration. 


CONCLUSION 


For the reasons stated above, the court DENIES 
Defendants’ Motion for Reconsideration. 


IT IS SO ORDERED. 


DATED: Honolulu, Hawaii, Nov. 18, 1997. 


/s/ David A. Ezra

matter suitable for disposition without a hearing. After 


128a 


reviewing Plaintiffs’ motions and the supporting mem- 
oranda, the court DISMISSES WITHOUT PREJUDICE 
Plaintiffs’ Motions for Attorneys’ Fees, to Vacate Taxation

are premature at this time. However, the court will con- 
sider Plaintiffs’ motions if, and when, they become ripe 
for review.

135a 


Appeals from the United States District Court 
for the District of Hawaii 
David A. Ezra, District Judge, Presiding 


Argued and Submitted 
January 16, 1998 — San Francisco, California 


Filed March 27, 1998 
Amended June 23, 1998 


Before: Charles Wiggins, John T. Noonan, and 
A. Wallace Tashima, Circuit Judges. 


ORDER 


The opinion filed March 27, 1998, is amended as 
follows (page references being to the slip opinion): 


(1) Page 2782, last full paragraph, line 2: Delete 
“substantive”. 


(2) Page 2782, last full paragraph, lines 3-6: Amend 
sentence to read: 


In Bates, we upheld a California term limits 
initiative against the claim that the initiative 
“did not provide California voters with suffi- 
cient notice that the Proposition imposed life- 
time limits” on officeholders, 


(3) Page 2782, lines 1-7 from bottom: These two 
sentences are amended to read: 


In Bates, the court dealt with whether voters had 
adequate notice of the substantive content of 
what they were voting on. The lack-of-notice 
argument asserted that federal courts could 
strike down voter initiatives whenever the 
voters did not receive sufficient notice to under- 
stand what they were voting for or against. 


136a 


(4) Page 2783, line 1: Delete “substantive”. 


(5) Page 2783, lines 4-5: This phrase is amended to 
read: “not that the state must inform them of the sub- 
stance of what they are voting on, or anything else.” 


With these amendments, the panel as constituted 
above has voted to deny the petition for rehearing. Judge 
Tashima has voted to reject the suggestion for rehearing 
en banc, and Judges Wiggins and Noonan so recommend. 


The iull court has been advised of the suggestion for 
rehearing en banc, and no judge of the court has 
requested a vote on the suggestion for rehearing en banc. 
Fed. R. App. P. 35(b). 


The petition for rehearing is denied, and the sugges- 
tion for rehearing en banc is rejected. 


137a

Q. Could you state your full name, please. 


A. Dwayne Yoshina. 


Q. And what is your position, Mr. Yoshina, with the 
State of Hawaii? 


A. I’m the chief elections officer. 


Q. And how long have you been chief elections 
officer? 


A. Since February of 1996. 


Q. And were you Hawaii's first chief elections offi- 
cer other than the lieutenant governor? 


A. First appointed chief election officer. 


Q. Prior to that time, the lieutenant governor was 
the chief elections officer? 


A. Yes. Yes.

A. Yes.

[p. 9] A. I oversaw the operations of the office.

Where there was an appointment process. So the the

144a 


A. Right. 


Q. What are your duties generally as chief elections 
officer? 


A. Well, provide election services to the citizens of 
the state of Hawaii, to do planning as [p. 10] it relates to 
that, to oversee implementation, to do liaison work with 
the county clerks because we do work with them, to carry 
out legislative initiatives, basically to administer all the 
election laws. 


Q. And is it also correct that under the statute that 
you are responsible for public education with respect to 
voter registration and information? 


A. Yeah. 


Q. And is it also correct that your office is also 
responsible for public education as to ballot issues? 


A. We don’t have sole responsibility for that. 


During the 1996 legislative session, I believe the LRB 
provides us with a digest of the pros and cons for all the 
amendments, and so we take that and we translate that 
information and make it available to the public. 


Q. Regardless of where you get the information 
from, you are the office that’s charged with disseminating 
it? 

A. Yeah.

Q. I don’t want to put words in your mouth, but it 
appears as if what both Exhibit 2 and Exhibit 3 said is 
that, in determining a majority, blank votes or blank 
ballots would not count and to determine whether the 
question passed you would add the yes and no votes 


148a 


together, and if the yeses were one-half of the total of the 
yeses and nos plus one, then the question passed; is that 
[p. 15] correct? 


A. Yeah. 


Q. How did you determine that that was - prior to 
the elections, how did you determine that that was the 
method of calculating and determining whether the Con 
Con question passed? 


A. I think we looked at the constitution and the 
constitution indicates that, and then we also took some 
direction from Section 11-151(3), sort of as guide, because 
basically the constitution tells us, but 151 also helps us in 
that sense because it says you don’t count the blanks in 
questions or for candidates except in the case of a consti- 
tutional amendment. 


So given those two things together, you kind of come 
to the conclusion that we shouldn’t count blanks. 


Q. Was it your understanding prior to the election 
that blank ballots had never been counted in determining 
whether Con Con questions put on the ballot in the past 
had received an affirmative majority? 


A. Yeah. To my knowledge, we had not counted the 
blank ballots prior to that.

A. Uh-huh.

Q. Is it correct that your office learned maybe a 
week to ten days or so prior to the election there was a

which was telling people that blank ballots would count

A. This is that group on Kauai? 
QO. Yes. 


A. I learned - I’m not certain how I learned that. It

may have read an article in the Kauai — the Garden Aisle

that. So I was aware of it. I became aware of it. 


Q. It was your position and your office’s position

information as to how the majority would be calculated? 


A. Right. So I think there was contact made with the 
group, and I know that I earlier said that I believe we sent 
a letter out to them. 


152a 


And we went back to our office to look for that letter,

I have since found out that there was telephone con-

Subsequent to that, there was a fax of this Fact Sheet 
that was sent to them. I don’t [p. 20] know which specific 
Fact Sheet, but there was some fax of a Fact Sheet sent 
out. I’m trying to get a copy of the record of that fax, but I 
have been unable to find that. 


Q. The woman with the group was called by your

A. I’m not certain if we called her or she called us,

A. Yes.

A. That's right.

A. She would probably be told not to disseminate

Q. Would you agree with me that it is very impor- 
tant, to have a fundamentally fair election, for citizens to

A. Yes.

Q. Yes.

A. I think the answer to that is yes, 


154a 


[p. 22] because the information we provided was that 
blanks would not count. 


Q. Do you agree with me that it is fundamentally 
unfair to count the blank Con Con ballots as ballots cast 
when the voters were specifically told that the blank 
ballots would not count as ballots cast? 


A. Fundamentally unfair? 


Well, I think we should count the ballots in the 
manner in which we tell the public that we are going to 
count the ballots. I’m not certain if it is fundamentally 
unfair. It probably is unfair. 


Q. So you would agree — I'll take out the word 
“fundamentally.” 


You would agree it is unfair to count the blank Con 
Con ballots as ballots cast when the voters were speci- 
fically told that the blank ballots would not count as 
ballots cast? 


A. When the information we disseminated was that 
we would not count blanks. 


Q. So then you agree with my statement? 


A. Yeah. 
Q. Yes? 
A. Yes. 


Q. And I take it that if you had known that [p. 23]

A. Of course.

When we learned of that, we imniediately tried to 
correct that. 


But beyond that, I don’t think anyone else did.

over at the legislature. So I haven’t checked with her. She 
no longer works with us. 


Q. You don’t know what she told them? 
A. I don’t know. 


Q. Now - 


A. I would have to say that if someone in the public 


tells me that someone told them something, I would take 
that at its face.

general?

site.

OFFICE OF ELECTIONS DWAYNE D. YOSHINA 
State of Hawaii Chief Election Officer 
FACT SHEET 


CALCULATING A MAJORITY 
ON THE QUESTION ON A 
CONSTITUTIONAL CONVENTION 


GENERAL INFORMATION 


Pursuant to Section 2, Article XVII, Hawaii State Consti- 
tution, the legislature may submit at any general election 
a question asking the electorate whether or not a consti- 
tutional convention should be convened. If a nine-year 
period elapses during which the question has not been 
submitted, then the Lieutenant Governor will certify the 
question, to be voted on at the first general election 
following the expiration of the nine-year period. 


The last time voters were asked whether there should be 
a constitutional convention was in 1986. Since a nine-year 
period during which the question was not submitted to 
the voters elapsed, the Lieutenant Governor certified the 
question to be placed on the 1996 General Election ballot. 


QUESTION 


During the 1996 General Election, voters will be asked the 
following question: 


“Shall there be a convention to propose a revi- 
sion of or amendments to the Constitution?” 


If a majority of the voters vote YES, then a constitutional 
convention will be held. If a majority of the voters vote 
NO, then a constitutional convention will not be held. 


174a 


Ex: 2 
Wit: Yoshina 
Date: 04-23-1997 Rep: ms 


CALCULATING A MAJORITY 


Pursuant to Section 2, Article XVII, Hawaii State Consti- 
tution, if a majority of the ballots cast upon such a ques- 
tion be in the affirmative, delegates to the convention 
shall be chosen at the next regular election. Section 
11-151(3), Hawaii Revised Statutes, states that: 


If a contest or question requires a majority of the 
votes for passage, any blank, spoiled, or invalid 
ballot shall not be tallied for passage or as votes 
case except that such ballots shall be counted as 
votes cast in ratification of a constitutional 
amendment. 


NOTE: Majority means 50% of the votes plus one vote. In 
this case, a majority of the votes cast upon the 
question means that the “YES” votes divided by 
the total of the “YES” and “NO” votes must be at 
least 50% of the votes plus one vote. 


Accordingly, to determine whether the constitutional con- 
vention will be held, more than 50% of the voters must 
vote YES. Spoiled, blank, and invalid ballots are not 
included in the calculation of a majority for this question. 


Example: 
-“YES” votes= 231,859 
“NO” votes= 102,721 


Votes cast upon the question = 334,580

334,580 votes x 50% = 167,290 votes + 1 vote = 
167,291 votes needed for a majority 


Since 231,859 is more than the 157,291 votes 


needed, the question received a majority of the 
votes.

SUGGESTION OF PARTIAL MOOTNESS 
AND PLAINTIFFS’ MOTIONS 

(1) TO TRANSFER PLAINTIFFS’ CLAIM 
FOR ATTORNEYS’ FEES TO THE 
DISTRICT COURT, OR, IN THE 
ALTERNATIVE, FOR AWARD OF 
ATTORNEYS’ FEES ON APPEAL, 

(2) TO RECALL THE MANDATE, AND 
(3) TO VACATE THE JUDGMENT 

AND REMAND WITH INSTRUCTIONS 


179a 


[MATERIAL DELETED IN PRINTING] 


Plaintiffs-Appellees, Cross-Appellants respectfully 
suggest that the issue of whether there must be a new 
election in 1998 upon whether “there [shall] be a conven- 
tion to propose a revision of or amendments to the Con- 
stitution [of Hawaii],” Haw. Const. art. XVII, § 2, is moot. 
The basis for this suggestion is the action of Defendant 
Benjamin Cayetano, in his capacity as Governor of 
Hawaii, in signing into law on Friday, June 26, 1998, 
Hawaii House Bill No. 3130. House Draft 1, Senate Draft 
1 (“H.B. 3130” or “the Act of June 26, 1995”). That law, 
signed three days after the Plaintiffs’ Petition for Rehear- 
ing was denied, and while the time in which to seek 
review of this Court’s judgment had not expired, grants 
Plaintiffs the principal relief sought in this litigation — a 
new election on the convention question. 


As explained more fully in the accompanying mem- 
orandum, as a consequence of this out-of-court victory. 
Plaintiffs are now “prevailing parties” under the Civil 
Rights Attorneys Fees Act of 1976, 42 U.S.C. § 1988. 
Pursuant to Ninth Circuit Rules 39-1.6 and 39-1.8, Plain- 
tiffs now move this Court to transfer to the District Court 
the issues of (1) whether Plaintiffs are “prevailing par- 
ties” under § 1988; and (2) what amounts should be 
awarded to them as attorneys fees and costs on appeal. 


In the alternative, and without waiving their right to 
an evidentiary hearing on their claim for fees and other 
costs on appeal, Plaintiffs move this Court to grant Plain- 
tiffs’ fees and cost request, or to refer such parts of the 
petition as appropriate to the Appellate Commissioner 
under Rule 39-1.9 for decision or recommendation. 


180a 


To the extent necessary to accomplish either of the 
foregoing, Plaintiffs also move this Court to recall the 
mandate under its inherent power and 28 U.S.C. § 1651, 
and to vacate the part of the judgment awarding Defen- 
dants their taxable costs on appeal and denying Plaintiffs 
their taxable costs on appeal. 


Finally, to relieve the United States Supreme Court of 
the task of vacating this Court’s judgment on the grounds 
of mootness, Plaintiffs independently, and particularly if 
the Court otherwise recalls the mandate, move the Court 
to recall the mandate for the purpose of vacating the 
judgment, to vacate the judgment in No. 97-16408, to 
dismiss the appeal as moot, to affirm the judgment in No. 
97-16543 (the cross-appeal) for the reasons stated by the 
District Court, and to remand with instructions to dismiss 
the complaint’s prayer for a new election as moot. 


This motion is made pursuant to Article III of the 
United States Constitution, 28 U.S.C. § 1651, 42 U.S.C. 
§ 1988, and the Court’s inherent power, as well as pur- 
suant to Fed. R. App. P. 1 and 27, Ninth Circuit Rules 
39-1.6, 39-1.8, and 39-1.9, and is further based upon the 
attached memorandum and exhibits, and the record of 
proceedings in these and the related appeals. 


Respectfully submitted: July 6, 1998. 


McCORRISTON MIHO MILLER MUKAI 
MARK J. BENNETT, ESQ. 


DEBEVOISE & PLIMPTON 


/s/ Steven S. Michaels 
STEVEN S. MICHAELS, ESQ. 
Attorneys for Plaintiffs- 
Appellees, Cross-Appellants 


18la 


[Caption Deleted] 


MEMORANDUM IN SUPPORT OF PLAINTIFFS’ 
MOTIONS (1) TO TRANFER [sic] PLAINTIFFS’ CLAIM 
FOR ATTORNEYS’ FEES TO THE DISTRICT COURT, 

OR, IN THE ALTERNATIVE, FOR AWARD OF 
ATTORNEYS’ FEES ON APPEAL, (2) TO RECALL THE 
MANDATE, AND (3) TO VACATE THE JUDGMENT 

AND REMAND WITH INSTRUCTIONS 


INTRODUCTION AND SUMMARY OF ARGUMENT 


On June 26, 1998, only three days after the panel 
denied Plaintiffs’ Petition for Rehearing, Defendant Ben- 
jamin Cayetano, in his capacity as Governor of Hawaii, 
signed into law legislation (H.B. 3130) that gives the 
citizens of Hawaii this November a right that has never 
before existed in the history of the Fiftieth State: The right 
to vote upon whether to hold a constitutional convention 
only two years after the preceding vote on that issue. This 
was the relief principally at issue in these appeals. See 
Bennett v. Yoshina, Nos. 97-16408, 97-16543, 97-16540, and 
97-16596, Slip Op. at 6295 (9th Cir. June 23, 1998) (as 
amended on denial of rehearing) (“Amended Slip Op.”)! 
And, for this reason, the Governor’s action largely moots 
this case. 


The passage of HB 3130 was in material part a prod- 
uct of this suit and the pressure it placed upon the State 


1 The District Court had originally ordered a new election 
within sixty days of its entry of summary judgment on Ju’y 10, 
1997, but modified its injunction on two occasions to permit the 
State to hold the new election on December 8, 1997. A stay of the 
injunction pending appeal was granted, and, had the judgment 
been affirmed on appeal, it was likely that the judicially-ordered 
election would have occurred this November. 


182a 


of Hawaii to redress the unfairness generated by the 
decision in Hawaii State AFL-CIO v. Yoshina, 935 P.2d 89 
(Haw. 1997). That decision retroactively and unexpec- 
tedly counted as “ballots cast upon [the] question” of 
whether to hold a constitutional convention more than 
45,000 “blank ballots” that had been returned by voters at 
the November, 1996, general election. If, at any time 
before the November elections, state officials indicate 
they will not be holding a new election on the convention 
question, Plaintiffs will be able to seek immediate relief in 
the state courts to obtain equitable orders mandating 
compliance with the Act of June 26, 1998. See Bush v. 
Watson, 918 P.2d 474, 1157-38 (1996) (state court suits to 
compel compliance with statutes not barred by sovereign 
immunity). These enforceable rights sharply and mate- 
rially alter the legal relationship of the parties. 


Before this suit was filed, no action had been taken 
by any state official to redress Yoshina’s unfairness. The 
Attorney General of Hawaii forfeited the State’s right to 
seek Yoshina’s review, and, even though weeks remained 
in the 1997 legislative session when Yoshina was decided, 
no legislative action was initiated. Not until January 28, 
1998, after the case here was briefed and argued, was HB 
3130 introduced. The committee report by the Hawaii 
House Judiciary Committee recommending passage spec- 
ifically referred to the injunction issued by the District 
Court as a justification for the bill. Both the House and 
Senate committee reports expressly or impliedly referred 
to the “controversy” and “confusion” unearthed in, or 
generated by, this lawsuit, as justifying a new plebescite. 
The deposition of the Chief Election Officer in this case, 
which would not have existed but for the litigation, was 


183a 


cited by Plaintiffs’ counsel to the committees in both 
Houses as demonstrating Yoshina’s unfairness, and how 
the 1996 election was marred by confusion over the rule 
for counting “blank ballots.” Only after he was deposed 
in this case and these appeals were submitted did the 
Chief Election officer himself testify in favor of the cura- 
tive legislation. 


The Governor’s action here was hardly gratuitous. 
Plaintiffs’ claims, which Judge Ezra upheld, were not 
“frivolous, unreasonable or groundless.’ ” Sablan v. 
Department of Finance of the Northern Mariana Islands, 856 
F.2d 1317, 1325 (9th Cir. 1988). Passage of HB 3130 
mooted a substantial federal controversy and spared the 
State the burdens — and risks — of defending Yoshina in the 
Supreme Court of the United States. 


Under these circumstances, Plaintiffs are “prevailing 
parties” under the Civil Rights Attorneys Fees Act of 
1976, 42 U.S.C. § 1988, and, at a minimum, have a suffi- 
cient claim of entitlement under that Act that they have a 
federally protected right under the Due Process Clause to 
have their claim for fees and costs litigated on their 
merits based on HB 3130's passage. See Logan v. Zimmer- 
man Brush Co., 455 U.S. 422, 430 (1982); Verrilli v. City of 
Concord, 557 F.2d 664, 665 (9th Cir. 1977). Out of fees and 
disbursements of approximately $200,000 that the Plain- 
tiffs’ firms have billed to these appeals, they seek only 
$86,366.25 as compensation for attorney time, and dis- 
bursements of only $7411.90, plus the customary 4.16 
percent Hawaii charge for general excise taxes (which 
charge will be rebated to the State of Hawaii when taxes 


on the award here are paid). 


184a 


The requested fee, which pursuant to Kentucky v. 
Graham, 473 U.S. 159 (1985), will be funded by the tax- 
payers of Hawaii, amounts to somewhat less than ten 
cents per each of Hawaii’s approximately one million 
plus current citizens. 


Presently, the judgment of this Court provides that 
“Yoshina shall recover his costs on appeal from Bennett” 
and “Bennett and the AFL-CIO shall bear their own costs 
on appeal.” Bennett v. Yoshina, Nos. 97-16408, 97-16543, 
97-16540, and 97-Amended Slip Op. at 6305. Plaintiffs 
have also filed the one Petition for Rehearing allowed by 
Fed. R. App. P. 40, and the Court has by order denied in 
toto Plaintiffs’ motion to stay consideration of the Petition 
for Rehearing and for leave to file an Amended Petition 
for Rehearing. Because § 1988 provides that attorneys’ 
fees are awarded “as part of the costs,” the issue now 
arises as to how, in light of the mandate (which issued 
automatically on June 30, 1998, under Fed. R. App. P. 
41(a)), Plaintiffs’ claim for fees and costs should be 
addressed. 


Due Process and the Ninth Circuit Rules leave this 
Court several options. One is for the Court to do nothing, 
and to allow Plaintiffs to file their attorneys fees and 
costs petition in the District Court without express per- 
mission by this Court, on the view that the mandate does 
not preclude the entry of a judgment for fees and costs in 
favor of the Plaintiffs based on HB 3130. “[A]lthough the 
mandate of an appellate court forecloses the lower court 
from reconsidering matters determined in the appellate 
court, it ‘leaves to the district court any issue not 
expressly or impliedly disposed of on appeal.’ ” Nguyen v. 
United States, 792 F.2d 1500, 1502 (9th Cir. 1986). The 


185a 


specific claim of whether, in light of the action of the 
Governor on June 26, 1998, Plaintiffs are entitled to their 
reasonable attorneys’ fees and costs, was not “expressly 
or impliedly disposed of on appeal” because the facts 
underlying that claim did not exist when this Court issued 
either its original opinion on March 27, 1998, or denied 
rehearing on June 23, 1998. To hold otherwise would 
allow the ministerial issuance of the mandate pursuant to 
Fed. R. App. P. 41(a) to generate an “adjudication” of 
claims dependent on facts that the Court not only was not 
aware of, but which did not exist, when the Court last 
reviewed the case. That result would violate Logan, and 
the Due Process Clause of the Fifth Amendment. This 
Court’s mandates should not be interpreted to visit a 
denial of Due Process, and a denial of this motion accord- 
ingly should not signify that this Court has barred Plain- 
tiffs from filing directly in the District Court. 


Another option, and one that would avoid confusion 
and needless litigation, would be for this Court to inter- 
pret Ninth Circuit Rule 39-1.8 as empowering the panel 
by express written order to transfer Plaintiffs’ fees and 
cost petition, which is timely under the Rule, for adjudi- 
cation in the District Court. In addition to providing clear 
guidance, such a disposition (as opposed to adjudication 
of the fee petition in this Court) is required unless Defen- 
dants concede Plaintiffs are “prevailing parties.” As this 
Court has long held, “[wJhether a party has shown a 
causal nexus between the lawsuit and the action taken is 
a factual determination for the district court.” McQuiston 
v. Marsh, 707 F.2d 1082, 1085 (9th Cir. 1983) (emphasis 
adcled). Accord Goehring v. Brophy, 94 F.3d 1294, 1304 (9th 
Cir. 1996).
