186a 


It is unclear whether the mandate must be recalled 
for a transfer order to be entered. Under Rule 41, Fed. R. 
App. P., the mandate automatically issues seven days 
after the denial of a petition for rehearing, while Circuit 
Rules 39-1.6 through 39-1.9 contemplate that adjudication 
of fee petitions will not take place until at least a week 
after the mandate issues. However, if it is necessary for 
the mandate to be recalled to issue a transfer order, ample 
authority for that action lies under this Court's ruling in 
Verrilli v. City of Concord, 557 F.2d voi, 665 (9th Cir. 1977) 
(recalling mandate even though the entitlement to fees 
could have been raised in petition for rehearing, because 
to do otherwise would visit “an unintended unjust result 
in that it deprived the appellant of a lawful statutory 
right to invoke the discretion of the district court under 
the [Fees] Act.”). 


If, however, the Court disagrees (1) that the District 
Court is free to adjudicate Plaintiffs’ fee and cost petition 
under the mandate as currently written, and (2) that it is 
appropriate to transfer the petition to the District Court, 
the Court itself must, at a minimum, adjudicate the peti- 
tion on its merits. Under this option, the Court should 
hold directly, based on the submission herein, that Plain- 
tiffs are “prevailing parties,” and it should decide the 
reasonable fees and costs to be taxed against Defendants, 
or refer the matter of the amount to be awarded to the 
Appellate Commissioner. In Section IV below, Plaintiffs, 
based on the declarations of counsel, have set forth the 
hours reasonably worked, the rates claimed, and the 
other costs incurred, and made appropriate deductions 
based on the controlling case law. As in the case of a 
transfer, if the mandate must be recalled to permit this 


187a 


Court to award fees and costs to Plaintiffs, the Court 
should do so pursuant to Verrilli. 


Finally, though unnecessary to the success of the fees 
and cost petition, the Court should, particularly if it 
decides to recall the mandate for other purposes, recall 
the mandate in light of the mootness of the principal 
issue presented, vacate the panel judgment and remand 
to the District Court with instructions to dismiss the 
complaint as moot to the extent it sought the remedy of a 
new election. Because Plaintiffs filed the one Petition for 
Rehearing contemplated by Fed. R. App. P. 40, and 
because the Court has denied their request to file an 
amended petition, Plaintiffs suggest, subject to their right 
to seek review of this Court’s denial of relief in No. 
97-16543, that the disposition in their cross-appeal should 
be vacated, and the juigment of the District Court, to the 
extent that it was favorable to Defendants, be affirmed 
for the reasons expressed by the District Court, namely, 
that the remedy prayed for in No. 97-16543 is not appro- 
priate if a new election is ordered. Under the reasoning of 
the panel decision, this disposition would not require any 
further action in the intervention appeals. These appeals 
would remain moot. 


ARGUMENT 


I. Under Controlling Precedent, the Governor’s Action 
of June 26, 1998, in Signing HB 3130 Renders the 
Plaintiffs “Prevailing Parties” Under 42 U.S.C. 
§ 1988. 


Section 1988 of Title 42, U.S.C., provides that “the 
court, in its discretion, may allow the prevailing party, 


—e—e——E—EOeeEeEeEeEeEeeeEeEeEeEeEeEeEEeEee Oe eee eee eee eee eee eee 


188a 


other than the United States, a reasonable attorneys’ fee 
as part of the costs.” The discretion to deny fees to a 
prevailing plaintiff is narrowly subscribed, and when 
civil rights plaintiffs attain the status of “prevailing par- 
ties” they “should ordinarily receive attorneys’ fees 
unless special circumstances would render an award 
unjust.” Sable Communications of California v. Pacific Tele- 
phone & Telegraph Co., 890 F.2d 184, 193 (9th Cir. 1989). 


Civil rights plaintiffs are deemed to be “prevailing 
parties” if “they succeed on any significant issue in litiga- 
tion which achieves some of the benefit the parties sought 
in bringing suit.” Hensley v. Eckerhart, 461 U.S. 424, 433 
(1983). “Hensley approves a generous standard for deter- 
mining when a plaintiff is a ‘prevailing party.’ The extent 
of the plaintiff's success is considered only in determin- 
ing the amount of the award.” Lummi Indian Tribe v. 
Oltman, 720 F.2d 1124, 1125 (9th Cir. 1983) (emphasis in 
original), quoted, Sable Communications of California v. 
Pacific Tel. & Tel., 890 F.2d 184, 192 (9th Cir. 1989)). 


As this Court has held for many years, “even in the 
absence of a favorable judgment, a litigant may be a 
prevailing party for purposes of an attorney fee award if 
his action was a ‘catalyst’ which motivated the defendant 
to provide the relief originally sought through litigation.” 
Beach v. Smith, 743 F.2d 1303, 1306 (9th Cir. 1984). Despite 
dicta to the contrary in Farrar v. Hobby, 506 U.S. 1._ (1992), 
this Ccurt has reaffirmed that a plaintiff seeking equita- 
ble relief need not obtain “an enforceable judgment 
against the defendant from whom fees are sought, . . . or 
leven] comparable relief through a consent decree or 
settlement,” Farrar, 506 U.S. at 111, holding that “Farrar 


189a 


does not preclude a catalyst test.” Kilgour v. City of Pas- 
adena, 53 F.3d 1007, 1010 (9th Cir. 1995). As stated in 
Stivers v. Pierce, 71 F.3d 732 (9th Cir. 1995), even under a 
catalyst theory, “[t]he degree of success is irrelevant to 
the question whether the plaintiff is the prevailing party,” 
and “voluntary action, such as a change in conduct that 
addresses the grievance, is sufficient.” Id. at 751. See 
Wilderness Society v. Babbitt, 5 F.3d 383, 388 (9th Cir. 1993) 
(group’s lawsuit was catalytic factor in government’s 
decision to conduct environmental study). 


In determining whether plaintiffs may obtain fees 
and costs under a catalyst theory, this Court has set forth 
“two subsidiary inquiries.” Sablan v. Department of Finance 
of the Northern Mariana Islands, 856 F.2d 1317, 1325 (9th 
Cir. 1988): 


First, we must determine what [plaintiffs] 
sought to accomplish in bringing [their] lawsuit 
and then determine whether the lawsuit was 
causally linked to the relief actually obtained 
.... Second, there must be a legal basis for the 
[plaintiffs’] claim - it must not be “frivolous, 
unreasonable or groundless.” © 


Id. (citations omitted); see also Stivers, 71 F.3d at 752 
(reiterating the two-prong “causal link” and “legal basis” 
test). 


There “can be no doubt” Plaintiffs have met this test. 
Stivers, 71 F.3d at 753. More important, Plaintiffs have 
sufficient factual support for the claim that they met the 
test for a “catalyst” recovery that the issue of fees and 
costs should be heard first in the District Court, even if 
(indeed especially if} this Court believes the causal 
inquiry could “go either way.” 


190a 


A. Plaintiffs Meet the “Causal Link” Test. 


“The ‘causal link’ prong of this [Court’s] two part 
‘catalyst’ test for determining ‘prevailing party’ status is 
nothing more than an inquiry into factual causation.” 
Satlan, 856 F.2d at 1325. And, in assessing the evidence, 
this Court has followed the pragmatic reasoning of the 
Fifth Circuit that “ ‘the provocative effects of the plain- 
tiffs’ legal efforts are often best gleaned from the chronol- 
ogy of events; defendants, on the whole, are usually 
rather reluctant to concede that the litigation prompted 
them to mend their ways.’ ” Id. at 1326 (quoting Posada v. 
Lamb County, 716 F.2d 1066, 1072 (5th Cir. 1983)). 


The recently-enacted legislation grants Plaintiffs one 
of the two remedies sought by the Plaintiffs, and the one 
granted by the District Court — a new election upon the 
conventicn question. This legislative relief comes no later 
than would have likely have been ordered by this Court 
had Plaintiffs prevailed in this Court’s March 27, 1998, 
decision, or June 23, 1998, order, and, in any case, demon- 
strably sooner than would have been ordered by the 
Supreme Court of the United States if this case were 
granted review on the merits and argued during the 
October 1998 Term. “Cessation of the government's erst- 
while policy . . . is, after all, ‘essentially the same as the 
injunctive relief sought in the complaint,’” Sablan, 856 
F.2d at 1327. 


The chronology of events as well as the contempora- 
neous history of HB 3130 also show a “clear, causal 
relationship between the litigation brought and the prac- 
tical outcome realized.” Rutherford v. Pitchess, 713 F.2d 
1416, 1419 (9th Cir. 1983). 


19la 


Prior to the filing of this lawsuit and the issuance of 
an injunction by the District Court, no one in state gov- 
ernment took a single step to address the unfairness of 
the retroactive enforcement of the Yoshina decision. The 
Attorney General, as the chief legal officer of the State, 
did not seek reconsideration of the decision in the Hawaii 
Supreme Court pursuant to Haw. R. App. P. 40, and she 
let expire the time in which to seek review in the 
Supreme Court of the United States pursuant to 28 U.S.C. 
§ 1257. The Chief Election Officer, so far as the record 
shows, did not request the Attorney General to pursue 
either of these two avenues for correcting Yoshina. Nor 
did the Governor. No member of the Legislature took 
steps in the 1997 legislative session to hold a new 
plebescite; likewise, the Governor did not call on the 
Legislature to act in the time remaining in the regular 
session; nor did he call a special legislative session. 


On January 28, 1998, after the briefs in this case were 
filed and the case was argued, HB 3130 was introduced 
by Speaker of the Hawaii House of Representatives 
Joseph Souki. See Michaels Ex. 1. At February hearings 
before the House Judiciary Committee, counsel for plain- 
tiffs advanced testimony of Defendant Yoshina, other evi- 
dence that had been gathered below, and the ruling of the 
District Court — support that would not have existed but 
for prosect-ion of the suit — as justifying passage: 


Regardless of whether one believes the Hawaii 
Supreme Court was right or wrong in deciding 
that blank ballots count in determining a major- 
ity on the constitutional convention question, it 
is beyond dispute that no one knew in advance 
of the last election that those were the ground 


192a 


rules. In fact, as we pointed out in our lawsuit, 
the State Office of Elections told voters over the 
telephone, through written fact sheets, and on 
the Internet, that blank ballots would not count 
in determining a majority on the convention 
question. In a deposition in the Con Con law- 
suit, Chief Election Officer Dwayne Yoshina tes- 
tified it would have been impossible for any 
voter to have known prior to voting that blank 
votes would count as ballots cast on the ques- 
tion — essentially as ‘no’ votes. It is not possible 
that the election could have been fair, if those 
40,000 people who thought they were not vot- 
ing, had no way of knowing that their blank 
ballots were ‘no’ votes. This is exactly what 
Judge Ezra found. Even Mr. Yoshina testified 
that the election was not fair, and that it would 
be more fair to have a new election.

This historical accounting shows that “the lawsuit 
was causally linked to the relief actually obtained.” 
Sablan, 856 F.2d at 1335. See Sable Communications, 890 
F.2d at 192 (mere contemporaneous notation in the 
administrative record that a defendant “is in federal court 
litigating this issue” is enough to support finding of 
causation, even if state administrative judge testifies that 
the federal litigation “was not a catalyst.”). Here, at every 
stage in the legislative history, there was express or 
implied acknowledgment by the House and Senate Judi- 
ciary Committees of the pendency of the federal case and 
the “controversy” it reflected and raised in the public 
mind. Ai every stage in the legislative process, Defen- 
dants’ admissions and the documentary evidence gath- 
ered in the Court below were submitted as support for 
legislative action. This is not a case where Plaintiffs rely 
solely on the sequence of chronological events. See 
Braafladt v. Board of Governors, 778 F.2d 1442, 1444 (9th Cir. 
1985); Beach v. Smith, 743 F.2d 1303, 1306-07 (9th Cir.

1994). Bearing in mind the rule in this Circuit that “defen- 
dants, on the whole, are usually rather reluctant to con-

shows that Plaintiffs’ lawsuit “had an impact” at the 
legislative level. See Rutherford v. Pitchess, 713 F.2d 1416, 
1419 (9th Cir. 1983). 


The facts of the instant case contrast sharply with 
those in American Constitutional Party v. Munro, 650 F.2d 
184 (9th Cir. 1981), where a District Court, sitting as the 
finder of fact, rejected a “catalyst” claim by a group that 
had sued the Washington Secretary of State to compel 
changes in certain election laws. Unlike Plaintiffs here, 
the American Constitutional Party never won any relief 
in the District Court, and, consequently, there was never 
the sort of litigation pressure that attended this case, 
where Defendants, as a result of the Plaintiffs’ victory in 
the District Court, bore the risks of proceeding as appel- 
lants in the federal judicial system. See 650 F.2d at 186 n.4. 
In addition to this important difference, the legislative 
history in Washington State indicated that the requested 
legislative changes had been proposed by the Secretary of 
State himself more than two years before the suit was 
filed, and had been supported by a lengthy staff analysis 
stating that the changes were required by “recent U.S. 


+ Indeed, that Governor Cayetano not only signed HB 3130

Supreme Court decisions.” Jd. at 189 (Thompson, J., con-

In Munro, in contrast to this case, the only evidence 
of the lawsuit’s causal effect was an affidavit of a single 
conference committee member a year after the fact stating 
that the suit at issue had been “discussed” during the 
legislative process. This Court upheld the District Court's 
factual finding of no causation, noting that the affidavits 
“were not made contemporaneously with the passage of 
the legislation” and stated only that the suit was “dis- 
cussed.” 650 F.2d at 188. “[P]roposed changes in the 
statute had been kicking around for years, often initiated 
by the Secretary’s own office.” Id. 


Here, by contrast, there is no evidence that, prior to 
bringing suit, or even prior to Judge Ezra’s entry of

issues presented by this case seriously enough to act. In 
addition, here, unlike Munro, Plaintiffs have presented 
the contemporanecus reports of the Hawaii House and

(Thompson, J.) (lawsuit filed “[a]fter all this spade work

- B. There Is Also a Legal Basis for Plaintiffs’ 
: Claims. 

: Even though Plaintiffs lost on the merits of their legal 
a claims before this Court, “the law of this circuit . . . [is 
e that] a claim has a basis in law so long as it is not 
‘ ‘frivolous, unreasonable, or groundless.’ ” Stivers, 71 F.3d 
| 732, 752 (9th Cir. 1995) (quoting Sablan, 856 F.2d at 1327 
4 (quoting in turn Fitzharris v. Wolff, 702 F.2d 836, 836 (9th 


Cir. 1983))). See Doty v. County of Lassen, 37 F.3d 540, 548 
(9th Cir. 1994). Indeed, the issue of whether Plaintiffs’ 
claim for a new election was “frivolous, unreasonable, or 
groundless” is properly one upon which Defendants bear 
the burden of proof. See Richard v. City of Pasadena, 889 F. 
Supp. 384, 388 (C.D. Cal. 1995) (noting that other circuits 
place the burden on defendant for second prong of “cata- 
lyst” test, and holding that plaintiff had legal basis for 
First Amendment claim). Whoever has the burden on this 
issue, it is obvious that Plaintiffs meet the second require- 
ment. 


Here, no one has ever asserted that Plaintiffs’ claims 
were frivolous; indeed, the District Court below granted 
summary judgment on the principal federal claim for 
relief asserted below. This Court’s lengthy published 
opinions in this case, coming after the Court granted 
extended argument, further reflect the substantiality of 
Plaintiffs’ claims. There was a “legal basis” for the claim 
for the new election sought here.

II. Given the Nature of the “Catalyst” Inquiry, And To 
Avoid Confusion in the District Court, the Court 
Should Transfer this Request for Attorneys Fees 
and Costs to the District Court Pursuant to Ninth 
Circuit Rule 39-1.8; This Court May, And Should, 
Recall the Mandate If That is Necessary to this 
Disposition. 


A. The District Court Sheuld in the First Instance 
Resolve the Issue of “Prevailing Party” Status. 


Although the foregoing analysis shows that Plaintiffs 
must, as a matter of law, be found to have “prevailed,” at 
a minimum it demonstrates why the issue of “prevailing 
party” status should be dealt with in the first instance by 
the District Court. As this Court has repeatedly held, 
“{w]hether a party has shown a causal nexus between the 
lawsuit and the action taken is a factual determination for 
the district court.” McQuiston v. Marsh, 707 F.2d 1082, 1085 
(9th Cir. 1983) (emphasis added). This Court is “loath to 
second-guess the probative value that the district court 
assign{s}] to the sequence of events prior and subsequent 
to the commencement of . . . suit.” Sablan, 856 F.2d at 
1326. Accord, Goehring v. Brophy, 94 F.3d 1294, 1304 (9th 
Cir. 1995): Stivers, 71 F.3d at 752; Sable Communications, 
890 F.2d at 192-93. See also Munro, 650 F.2d at 186 (ruling 
on “catalyst” claim is not “a summary judgment,” but is 
factual question that is subject to review for “abuse of 
discretion”); id. at 188 n.17 (litigants who request as do 
Plaintiffs here are entitled to “an evidentiary hearing on 
whether the plaintiff’s lawsuit was a significant catalyst 


for change”). 


LM 


200a 


Here, the District Court has never had an oppor- 
tunity to make a “catalyst” determination, and, at a mini- 
mum, Plaintiffs have sufficient evidence that the passage 
of HB 3130 was causally linked to their lawsuit that they 
are entitled to an evidentiary hearing on that issue. 
Under this Court’s precedents, and as a matter of general 
appellate procedure, this Court generally does not sit as a 
fact finder, in attorneys’ fees matters or otherwise, and 
the proper disposition of Plaintiffs’ fee petition is for that 
petition to be adjudicated first by the District Court.4 


B. The Mandate Does Not Preclude Consideration 
By the District Court of Plaintiffs’ Claim For 
Fees and Costs Based on Facts that Arose Three 
Days After this Court Denied Rehearing. 


Because the facts giving rise to Plaintiffs’ prevailing 
party status did not arise until after this Court denied 
rehearing, the rules governing the construction of the 
appellate mandates and federal Due Process precepts do 
not render the mandate in this case preclusive of a “cata- 
lyst” recovery. 


+ Because numerous other issues, such as the prevailing 
rates for legal services in Hawaii, and the experience of counsel, 
are peculiarly within the expertise of the trial court, transfer of 
the fee petition is warranted not only as to whether Plaintiffs 
were “prevailing parties,” but what the “reasonable fee” should 
be. 


201a 


1. Aside From Constitutional Considerations, 
the “Mandate Rule” Does Not Bar the Dis- 
trict Court From Considering Plaintiffs’ 
Fees/Costs Claim. 


The general rule is that “although the mandate of an 
appellate court forecloses the lower court from recon- 
sidering matters determined in the appellate court, it 
‘leaves to the district court any issues not expressly or 
impliedly disposed of on appeal.’” Nguyen v. United 
States, 792 F.2d 1500, 1501 (9th Cir. 1986) (quoting Stevens 
v. F/V/ Bonnie Doon, 731 F.2d 1433, 1435 (9th Cir. 1984)). 
The “mandate rule,” on its face, clearly permits the Dis- 
trict Court, without a recall of the mandate, to determine 
that Plaintiffs were “prevailing parties,” and to award 
them their reasonable attorneys fees and costs on appeal, 
as the panel could not have “expressly or impliedly dis- 
posed of” the issue of whether the Governor’s June 26, 
1998, action rendered Plaintiffs “Prevailing parties” 
under § 1988, because the fact of that action did not exist 
when the Court acted on the Petition for Rehearing three 
days earlier. That the mandate states that “Yoshina shall 
recover his costs on appeal from Bennett” and “Bennett 
and the AFL-CIO shall bear their own costs on appeal” 
simply does not resolve, expressly, or impliedly, the issue 
presented by the post-June 23, 1998, developments. 
Accordingly, it would be proper for the District Court to 
grant Plaintiffs their fees and costs on appeal, even with- 


out a recall of the mandate or even without further 
express action by this Court to transfer the consideration 
of the fees and cost petition to the Court below.

2. The Due Process Clause of the Fifth 
Amendment Requires this Court to Con- 
strue the Mandate as Not Barring Plaintiffs 
From Seeking Their Fees and Costs on 
Appeal Pursuant to 42 U.S.C. § 1988. 


Even apart from the foregoing, the mandate cannot 
be viewed as barring Plaintiffs’ claims under § 1988 
because to hold otherwise would be unconstitutional. 


litigant’s state-created employment practices claim vio- 
lated the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amend- 
ment. Under the Illinois scheme, if the state fair 
employment practices agency failed to issue a complaint 
within 180 days following a statutory conciliation period, 
the claimant's charge was deemed forfeited. The Supreme 
court held this procedure unconstitutional, because it 
deprived Mr. Logan of the right “to have [a factfinder] 
consider the merits of his charge, based upon the sub- 
Stantiality of the available evidence, before deciding 
whether to terminate a claim.” 455 U.S. at 434. Because 
the Illinois system “deprives persons of their claims in a 
random manner{[,]” it was invalid. Id. 


ESN 


203a 


Here, to construe the mandate as precluding a § 1988 
recovery would violate Logan's core holding. No judicial 
officer has yet passed on Plaintiffs’ claim based on the 
June 26, 1998, order, and any argument that the mandate 
adjudicated that claim would arise not from any review 
of the merits of the claim, but rather because of the 
automatic operation of Fed. R. App. P. 41(a). Thus, the 
mandate cannot bar Plaintiffs § 1988 claim. 


C. The Court Should, Nonetheless, Transfer By 
Express Order the Plaintiffs’ Fees and Costs 
Petition to the District Court For a Ruling on its 
Merits; If the Court Determines that it is Neces- 
sary to Recall the Mandate to Achieve This, 
This Court’s Rulings Require Such a Recall. 


Despite the above, it would obviously curtail need- 
less litigation if the Court were to enter an express order 
transferring Plaintiffs’ fees and costs petition to the Dis- 
trict Court for decision on its merits. Ninth Circuit Rule 
39-1.8 states: 


Any party who is or may be eligible for 
attorneys fees on appeal to this Court may, 
within the time permitted in Circuit Rule 39-1.6, 
file a motion to transfer consideration of attor- 
neys fees on appeal to the district court or 
administrative agency from which the appeal is 
taken. 


Ninth Cir. R. 39-1.8 (emphasis added). This language is 
plainly applicable to this circumstance, and Plaintiffs’ 
motion is timely. See Ninth Cir. R. 39-1.6 (fourteen day 
deadline).

bk ae DES, 


RDS RUS ER GE NT Re 


204a 


Per the foregoing, a recall of the mandate should not 
be required to effect a transfer permitting the District 
Court to grant Plaintiffs fees and costs on appeal. More- 
over, that Fed. R. App. P. 41(a) triggers an automatic 
issuance of the mandate seven days before Ninth Circuit 
Rule 39-1.8 contemplates a motion for transfer being filed 
suggests that Plaintiffs’ motion for transfer may be 
granted without a recall of the mandate. 


However, if the Court views the mandate as in any 
way preclusive of Plaintiffs’ claims under § 1988 and their 
motion for transfer, this Court’s ruling in Verrilli v. City of 
Concord, 557 F.2d 664 (9th Cir. 1977), fully supports a 
recall of the mandate and an amendment thereof under 
the instant circumstances. 


In Verrilli, a civil rights plaintiff in an attorneys’ fees 
appeal had failed to call to the panel’s attention the 
passage of the 1976 amendments to § 1988, even though 
the Act became law only five weeks after argument, and 
nearly three months before the time expired for petition- 
ing for rehearing. See 557 F.2d at 664. As a consequence, 
the panel’s opinion (and the resulting mandate) limited 
Plaintiff's fees and costs claim on remand to the “bad 
faith” standard. Id. on appeal from the District Court's 
denial of attorneys fees, the Court held: 


Because of an overpowering sense of fair- 
ness and a firm belief that this is the exceptional 
case requiring recall of the mandate in order to 
prevent an injustice, we act to free the hand of 
the district court from any strictures of the “law 
of the case” on the former remand. Aerojet-Gen- 
eral Corp. v. American Arbitration Ass'n, 478 F.2d 
248 (9th Cir. 1975); Greater Boston Television Corp. 


205a 


v. F.C.C., 463 F.2d 268 (D.C. Cir. 1971). Our prior 
ruling was not merely an erroneous one, Powers 
v. Bethlehem Steel Corporation, 483 F.2d 963 (ist 
Cir. 1973); it was an unintended unjust result in 
that it deprived the appellant of a lawful statu- 
tory right to invoke the discretion of the district 
court under the Act and the holding in Stanford 
Daily [Act applies to pending case]. 


557 F.2d at 665. 


This reasoning cuts in Plaintiffs’ favor a fortiori. For 
here, unlike Verrilli itself, Plaintiffs could not have 
brought the fee-entitling action to the attention of this 
Court prior to the denial of rehearing — that action did 
not yet exist. At a very minimum, if the mandate is 
viewed by the Court as in any manner preclusive of 
Plaintiffs’ claims under § 1988, Verrilli not only provides 
authority for, but compels, a recall of the mandate and an 
amendment thereto transferring Plaintiffs’ claim for con- 
sideration on its merits by the District Court. 


Ill. At a Very Minimum, The Court Should Grant 
Plaintiffs’ § 1988 Fees and Costs Petition Itself Or 
Refer the Petition to the Appellate Commissioner; 
if the Mandate Must Be Recalled to Aid This 
Result, The Court Should Do So Pursuant to Ver- 
rilli. 


Although the proper analysis requires Plaintiffs’ fees 
and costs petition to be adjudicated first in the District 
Court, if the Court believes otherwise Plaintiffs request 
this Court to adjudicate the petition itself, or to refer such 
parts of it as the Court finds appropriate to the Appellate 
Commissioner pursuant to Ninth Circuit Rule 39-1.9. If

the Court believes that a recall of the mandate is required

IV. Plaintiffs’ Reasonable Fees and Costs on Appeal. 


Under the controlling precedents interpreting § 1988, 
the Court's calculation of the attorneys’ fee and costs 
award begins with the determination of the “lodestar” 
figure, which is “ ‘the product of reasonable hours times 
a reasonable rate.’ ” Burlington v. Dague, 505 U.S. 557, 559 
(1992) (quoting Pennsylvania v. Delaware Valley Citizens 
Council for Clean Air, 478 U.S. 546, 565 (1986)). As this 
Court explained recently: 


The ‘lodestar’ is calculated by multiplying the 
number of hours the prevailing party reasonably 
expended on the litigation by a reasonable 
hourly rate. McGrath v. County of Nevada, 67 F.3d 
248, 252 (9th Cir. 1995). After making that com- 
putation, the district court then assesses 
whether it is necessary to adjust the presump- 
tively reasonable lodestar figure on the basis of 
the Kerr factors that are not already subsumed 
in the initial lodestar calculation. 


Morales v. City of San Rafael, 96 F.3d 359, 363-64 (9th Cir. 
1997). The twelve Kerr factors, see Kerr v. Screen Guild 
Extras, Inc., 526 F.2d 67, 70 (9th Cir. 1975), cert denied, 425 
U.S. 951 (1976), are as follows: 


(1) The time and labor required; 


(2) The novelty and difficulty of the questions 
involved; 


207a 


(3) The skill requisite to perform the legal ser- 
vice properly; 


(4) The preclusion of other employment by the 
attorney due to acceptance of the case; 


(5) The customary fee; 
(6) Whether the fee is fixed or contingent; 


(7) Time limitations imposed by the client or 
the circumstances; 


(8) The amount involved and the results 
obtained; 


(9) The experience, reputation, and ability of 
the attorneys; 


(10) The “undesirability” of the case; 


(11) The nature and length of the professional 
relationship with the client; 


(12) Awards in similar cases. 


Morales, 96 F.3d at 364 n.8. “There is a strong presumption 
that the lodestar figure represents a reasonable fee[,]” 
and ‘[o]nly in rare instances should the lodestar figure be 
adjusted on the basis of other considerations.’ ” Id. In this 
instance, Plaintiffs seek a “lodestar” recovery of 
$86,366.25 in attorneys fees for the work of two attorneys 
at the following three stages of this litigation: (1) the 
“application for stay” stage; (2) the “merits briefing and 
oral argument” stage; and 3) the “rehearing” stage. No 


compensation for time since the motion to stay consider- 
ation of the petition for rehearing is requested, though 


OE hie Re te a 


208a 


such work, including preparaing [sic] this petition, is 
compensable.> 


Plaintiffs do not request an upward multiplier based 
upon the Kerr factors, but likewise they submit that the 
Kerr factors do not permit any downward adjustment of 
the lodestar. 


Plaintiffs’ attorneys and legal assistants (paralegals) 
and library staff billed at least 648.3 hours to this matter 
since August 1, 1997, but Plaintiffs, in the exercise of 
billing judgment, and in light of the rule of Independent 
Federation of Flight Attendants v. Zipes, 491 U.S. 754, 761 
(1989) (holding that fees may be awarded against puta- 
tive intervenors only if the intervenors’ action was “frivo- 
lous, unreasonable, or without foundation”), seek 
recovery for only a portion of those hours.

Mark Bennett (136.7 hours) of McCorriston Miho, Miller, 
Mukai, in Honolulu, Hawaii, and Steven Michaels

Plimpton, in New York City. The amounts to be received 
by each firm for this work is as follows:

The requested amount, which should be taxed 
against Defendants in their official capacities,” is reason-

? Pursuant to Kentucky v. Graham, 473 U.S. 159 (1985), none 
of the fees and requested disbursements will be paid by 
Defendants out of their individual funds. Rather, because this is 
an “official capacity” action, the costs of the litigation will be 
spread over the taxpayers of Hawaii. With a population of 
approximately c:.e million, this translates into 9.4 cents per

obtained.’ ” Farrar v. Hobby, 506 U.S. 103, 114 (1992) (quot- 
ing Hensley, 461 U.S. at 436). Only sixteen months after 
Yoshina was issued, a new election where the Yoshina rule 
will be disclosed to the voters in advar.ce has been 
secured. That election will take place only two years after 
the last election, less than eleven months after the date 
the trial court itself mandated in its injunction. Had the 
legislation not been passed, the citizens of Hawaii would 
have had to wait until the year 2006 to be able to vote as 
of right on the question. 


The fact that the Plaintiffs lost the battle in court but 
won the war legislatively is of no moment. As stated in 
Cabrales v. ©ounty of Los Angeles, 935 F.2d 1051 (9th Cir. 
1991): 


We read Hensley as establishing the general 
rule that plaintiffs are to be compensated for 
attorney’s fees incurred for services that contrib- 
ute to the ultimate victory in the lawsuit. Thus, 
even if a specific claim fails, the time spent on 
that claim may be compensable, in full or in 
part, if it contributes to the success of other 
claims. See id. at 435. We analogize from 
Hensley’s discussion of different claims to the 
different stages of litigation at issue here. Just as 
time spent on losing claims can contribute to the 
success of other claims, time spent on a losing 
stage of litigation contributes to success because 
it constitutes a step toward victory. [footnote 
omitted] 


citizen for the benefit of having a new election that offers to 
change the course of the State’s history. 


214a 


935 F.2d at 1052. As in Cabrales, even though Plaintiffs 
were unable to defeat Defendants’ application for stay, 
and were not able to obtain a favorable judgment in this 
Court, this Court’s orders were “simply a temporary 
setback on the way to a complete victory for plaintiffs” 
on their prayer for a new election. Id. at 1053. Moreover, 
Plaintiffs’ request in their cross-appeal for the remedy of 
an order certifying that the convention question had 
passed in 1996, was “related” for purposes of Hensley’s 
analysis, and was part of the litigation package that con- 
tributed to the success of other claims. Cabrales, 935 F.2d 
at 1052. Plaintiffs’ two requested remedies, one which 
was granted, and one which was not, were not “distinctly 
different claims for relief that are based on different facts 
and legal theories.” Hensley, 461 U.S. at 434. Both forms of 
relief were inextricably related to the single central claim 
in the litigation - namely, that Yoshina’s retroactive 
enforcement violated the Constitution. 


“Where a plaintiff has obtained excellent results, his 
attorney should recover a fully compensatory fee.” 
Hensley, 461 U.S. at 435. Certainly, no one can deny that 
the passage of unprecedented corrective legislation grant- 
ing a new election on whether the people should hold a 
constitutional convention is anything less than an “excel- 
lent result” for the Plaintiffs. 


Plaintiffs’ requested fees are reasonable not only 
under this line of analysis, but withstand scrutiny even 
when examined at a litigation-stage-by-litigation-stage 
level.

V. Vacatur of the Judgment on the Merits. 


Had Defendants’ appeal “become moot while the 
petition for rehearing and suggestion for rehearing en 
banc were still pending and before the mandate issued, 
the appropriate disposition [would have been] to vacate 
the panel’s opinion and dismiss the appeal.” Independent 
Union of Flight Attendants v. Pan American World Airways, 
966 F.2d 457, 459 (9th Cir. 1992). As briefed above, the 
continued existence of the panel judgment does not pre- 
clude Plaintiffs’ fees and costs petition, and, in any case, 
the constitutional considerations implicated in recalling 
the mandate for purposes of expressly allowing Plaintiffs’ 
petition under § 1988 to go forward place such a recall on 
a different, 1.e., stronger, footing than are implicated by a 
recall of the panel judgment on the merits. If the Court, 
for example, recalls the mandate for the purpose only of 
addressing fees and costs, Plaintiffs still could petition for 
certiorari so that the Supreme Court itself could vacate 
this Court’s judgment as moot. Plaintiffs could also possi- 
bly seek dismissal in the court below. 


Nonetheless, particularly if the Court 1:_calls the 
mandate for other purposes, it would be appropriate to 
recall the mandate for the purposes of vacating the 
panel’s merits judgment as well. This is not an instance 
where a recall of the mandate is sought after certiorari is 
denied. Rather, here, recall of the mandate for purposes 


of vacating the judgment, and dismissing the appeal in 
No. 96-16408, would efficiently spare the Supreme Court 
of having to exercise its certiorari powers for the purpose 
of granting a routine vacatur. See, e.g., Department of the 
Treasury v. Galioto, 477 U.S. 556, 560 (1986). Accordingly, 
the panel judgment in No. 97-16408 should be vacated, 


DR ET ER re 


2l6a 


the appeal dismissed, and the cause remanded to the 
District Court with instructions to dismiss the Com- 
plaint’s prayer for a new election. Because such a result 
would “de-moot” the cross appeal, Plaintiffs suggest that, 
because they have already sought rehearing once, it 
would be appropriate to affirm the judgment of the Dis- 
trict Court as to the remeay sought in Cross-Appeal No. 
97-16543, for the reasons found by the District Court. 
That disposition would leave only a narrow opinion as to 
remedies and would leave the issues of urderlying Due 
Process liability to be litigated another day in a case 
where such equitable adjudication is fully required 
throughout the entire appellate process. 


CONCLUSION 


For the foregoing reasons, the Court should: 


(1) By express order pursuant to Circuit Rule 39-1.8, 
transfer Plaintiffs’ claim for attorneys’ fees and other 
costs to the District Court for an adjudication on its 
merits in light of the Governor’s action in signing HB 
3130 into law, and recall the mandate and vacate the 
Court's judgment as to costs if that is necessary to effect 
such a tranefz,, 


(2) Alternatively, grant Plaintiffs, motion for an 
award of attorneys’ fees and other costs on appeal in the 
amount of $86,366.25 in fees and $7,411.90 in disburse- 
ments, plus customarily-billed state general excise taxes 
of $3,592.83, or refer the matter to the Appellate Commis- 
sioner for final adjudication or recommendation under 
Circuit Rule 31-1.9, and recall the mandate and vacate the 


i 


217a 


Court's judgment as to costs if that is necessary to effect 
such a result; 


(3) Particularly if the Court is inclined to recall the 
mandate for other purposes, recall the mandate and 
vacate the judgment as to its merits in No. 97-16408, 
dismiss Defendants’ appeal, affirm the judgment in No. 
97-16543 for the reasons stated by the District Court, and 
remand with instructions to dismiss the complaint’s 
prayer for a new election on the convention question as 
moot.

EXHIBIT 1 
H.B. NO. 3130 


HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES 
NINETEENTH LEGISLATURE, 1998 
STATE OF HAWAII 


A BILL FOR AN ACT 
RELATING TO A CONSTITUTIONAL CONVENTION. 


BE IT ENACTED BY THE LEGISLATURE OF THE 
STATE OF HAWAII: 


SECTION 1. Article XVIL, Section 2, of the Constitu- 
tion of the State of Hawaii provides that the legislature 
may put to the electorate at any general or special elec- 
tion the question of whether there shall be a convention 
to propose a revision of or amendments to the State 
Constitution. The purpose of this Act is to submit such 
question to be placed on the ballot and to be voted upon 
at the general election of 1998. 


SECTION 2. The question to be placed on the ballot 
shall be as follows: 


“Shall there be a convention to propose a revi- 
sion of or amendments to the constitution?” 


SECTION 3. The ballot shall have designated spaces 
to mark YES or NO. The proposal shall be considered 
approved only if approved by a majority of all votes 
tallied upon the question. 


SECTION 4. This Act shall take effect upon its 
approval.

Representative Terrance W.H. Tom

Re: Written Testimony of Mark J. Bennett Regarding 
House Bill 3130 Set for Hearing Before the 
House Judiciary Committee on February 12, 
1998 at 2:00 p.m. 


Dear Representative Tom: 


I write this letter in support of House Bill 3130, which 
would place on the 1998 General Election Ballot, the 
question of whether Hawaii should hold a constitutional 
convention. I have been in the forefront of the battle over 
the 1996 general election, and as you know, filed a law- 
suit against the State, seeking either an order that a 
convention be held, or a new election. Judge Ezra ordered 


Pet ee eee re a ee 


220a 


a new election, and that issue is currently on appeal 
before the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth 
Circuit. It is my belief that were House Bill 3130 to pass, 
that would moot that lawsuit. 


Regardless of whether one believes the Hawaii 
Supreme Court was right or wrong in deciding that blank 
ballots count in determining a majority on the constitu- 
tional convention question, it is beyond dispute that no 
one knew in advance of the last election that those were 
the ground rules. In fact, as we pointed out in our law- 
suit, the State Office of Elections told voters over the 
telephone, through written fact sheets, and on the Inter- 
net, that blank ballots would not count in determining a 
majority on the convention question. In a deposition in 
the Con-Con lawsuit, Chief Election Officer Dwayne 
Yoshina testified it would have been impossible for any 
voter to have knowr prior to voting that blank votes 
would count as ballots cast on the question — essentially 
as “no” votes. It is not possible that the election could 
have been fair, if those 40,000 people who thought they 
were not voting, had no way of knowing that their blank 
ballots were “no” votes. This is exactly what Judge Ezra 
found. Even Mr. Yoshina testified that the election was 
not fair, and that it would be more fair to have a new 
election. 


It is of course not possible for the Legislature to right 
every wrong that occurs. The difficulty of righting 
wrongs is compounded by the fact that one person’s 
grave injustice is another person’s correct decision. In 
this case, however, it is hard to see how any person could 
argue that putting this question back on the ballot would 
be anything other than the right thing to do. 


221a 


Even if the matter is put on the 1998 ballot, those 
who want a convention will have a difficult time, because 
blank ballots will count as “no” votes pursuant to the 
Supreme Court's decision. At least, however, this time 
everyone will know the rules in advance. Everyone will 
have a fair opportunity to campaign, and those voters 
who still wish not to vote will at least do so knowingly - 
they will know that not casting a ballot on the constitu- 
tional convention question will mean that they will be 
voting “no’. 


On my own behalf, and on behalf of those who joined 
with me in the Con-Con lawsuit, I urge the Committee to 
pass this Bill as swiftly as possible. 


Very truly yours, 


/s/ Mark J. Bennett 
Mark J. Bennett

EXHIBIT 3 
STAND. COM. REP. NO. 229-98 


Honolulu, Hawaii 
February 18, 1998 


RE: H.B. No. 3130 
fe | 


Honorable Joseph M. Souki 
Speaker, House of Representatives 
Nineteenth State Legislature 
Regular Session of 1998 

State of Hawaii 


Sir: 


Your Committee on Judiciary, to which was referred 
H.B. No. 3130 entitled: 


“A BILL FOR AN ACT RELATING TO A CONSTITU- 
TIONAL CONVENTION,” begs leave to report as fol- 
lows: 


The purpose of this bill is to present to the electorate 
at the general election cf 1998 the question of whether 
there shail be a convention to propose a revision of or 
amendments to the State Constitution. 


The Chief Election Officer, the Mayor of the City and 
County of Honolulu, representatives from Let the People 
Decide, Citizens for a Constitutional Convention, and 
several individuals testified in support of this bill. The 


Office of Hawaiian Affairs testified in opposition to this 
bill. 


Your Committee finds that there was a tremendous 
amount of controversy over the constitutional convention 


223a 


question that was placed on the ballot at the 1996 general 
election. There was disagreement over whether blank 
ballots and spoiled ballots, or ballots marked both “yes” 
and “no”, should be counted in determining a majority 
on the convention question. 


Lawsuits were filed in both state and federal courts 
on this issue. The Hawaii Supreme Court decided in 
March 1997, that blank ballots and spoiled ballots did 
count in determining the amount of ballots cast. As a 
result, the court held that the proposition failed. 


The United States District Court for the District of 
Hawaii decided in July 1997, that, because the electorate 
was told prior to the 1996 election that blank ballots 
would not be counted for any purpose, the Hawaii 
Supreme Court's post-election decision rendered the elec- 
tion on the constitutional convention question fundamen- 
tally unfair. As a result, the Supreme Court held that the 
results of the constitutional convention question were 
void and that the State had to hold a new special election 
on this issue within sixty days. That decision was 
appealed to the United States Court of Appeals for the 
Ninth Circuit. 


Your Committee concludes that because of the confu- 
sion over the constitutional convention question at the 
1996 general election, the Legislature again should place 
this question on the ballot to be voted upon at the 1998 
general election. 


Your Committee, however, finds that section 3 of the 
bill may be misleading. This .~ztion provides that the 
proposal shall be considered only if approved by a major- 
ity of all “votes tallied upon the question.” This language 


4 
a 
if 


224a 


differs from the language in Article XVII of the Hawaii 
Constitution, which requires a majority of all “ballots cast 
upon such a question.” 


Because of this potential ambiguity, your Committee 
finds that section 3 of this bill should be deleted. Your 
Committee has determined that deletion of this section 
would not affect the purpose of this bill. 


Accordingly, your Committee has amended this bill 
by deleting section 3 of the bill. Your Committee further 
amended the bill by adding the word “state” in section 2. 


As affirmed by the record of votes of the members of 
your Committee on Judiciary that is attached to this 
report, your Committee is in accord with the intent and 
purpose of H.B. No. 3130, as amended herein, and recom- 
mends that it pass Second Reading in the form attached

McCorriston Mino Miter Mukai 
ATTORNEYS AT LAW 


April 6, 1998 


Senator Matthew Matsunaga 

Senator Avery Chumbley 

Co-Chairmen, Senate Judiciary Committee

Honolulu, Hawaii 96813 


Re: Written Testimony of Mark J. Bennett regarding 
House Bill 3130 relating to a Constitutional 
Convention — April 9, 1998, 9:00 a.m. 


Dear Senators Matsunaga and Chumbley: 


I submit this written testimony in support of House 
Bill 3130, which provides that the voters shall have the 
opportunity in the 1998 general election, to vote on 
whether or not to hold a Constitutional Convention. 
Obviously one focus of the Committee’s hearing will be 
whether there should be a new vote or not. I have also 
heard expressed the question of whether there is a need 
for a convention. I would respectfully suggest that the 
second question should be of much less importance, 
because if the circumstances make it appropriate to put 
the question back on the ballot, the need for a convention 
is what will be specifically addressed by the voters. If a 
majority of all of those going to the polls (which is a 
tough standard to meet) approve a convention, I think 
that in itself establishes the need. Nevertheless, I wil! 


address both issues in this testimony. 


ap ke CONN Ur en Ee ere ee eee ee a 
+o LES eee ee oe 3 SS 


226a 


I belief that it is fair, just, and appropriate for the 
voters to have an opportunity in 1998 to vote on the 
convention question, because I do not think they had a 
fair opportunity to do so in 1996, nor do I think the 
election was fair. In this regard, I am joined by Duane 
[sic] Yoshina, head of the Office of Elections, who testi- 
fied in the Con Con lawsuit (as set forth below), that 
because blank votes were counted as no votes, the elec- 
tion was unfair. 


It is very important to lay out what I believe to be the 
undisputed facts regarding the election. First, it appeared 
absolutely clear prior to the election that blank ballots 
would not be counted in deciding the Con Con question. 
In Republican Party of Hawaii v. Waihee, 68 Haw. 258, 709 
P.2d 980 (1985), the Supreme Court, in interpreting lan- 
guage in the City and County of Honolulu’s Charter, very 
similar to the Con Con language in Article XVII of the 
Constitution, appeared to hold that blank ballots would 
never be counted in Hawaii elections, except with regard 
to the passage of constitutional amendments. As a result 
of this decision, Haw. Rev. Stat. § 11-151(3) was passed, 
providing that blank ballots do not count in Hawaii 
except with regard to ratification of constitutional amend- 
ments. Indeed, Haw. Rev. Stat. § 16-27 actually differenti- 
ates between a valid ballot and blank ballot. The 


Legislative Reference Bureau's 1968 and 1978 “Constitu- 
tional Convention Studies” specifically stated that in 
Hawaii, blank ballots do not count in determining 
whether or not a Con Con is approved. Mr. Yoshina testi- 
fied in his deposition that blank ballots had never been counted 
in determining whether the Con Con Questions have received a 
majority. He testified that it was the historical practice of 


227a 


the State “th.t in determining whether the Con question 
received a majut:ty, blank ballots did not count as ballots 
cast.” He testified that he thought the constitution clearly 
indicated this, and that prior to the election his office 
believed that was really “no question at all” — “it was 
clear.” A Con Con fact sheet for public distribution speci- 
fically told the public that blank ballots would not count. 
This fact sheet is attached as Exhibit 1 to this testimony. 
The Office of Elections also put identical language on the 
Internet. A copy of this Internet information is attached 
as Exhibit 2. In addition, an information sheet available to 
the voters at every polling place told the voters that “yes” 
votes and “no” votes had meaning, but did not ascribe 
any meaning to a blank vote. In contrast, the same infor- 
mation sheets, with regard to constitutional amendments, 
stated exactly what the effect of blank votes was — essen- 
tially that they were “no” votes. Indeed, the office of the 
elections actually called up Citizens for a Constitutional 
Convention, the only organization campaigning in favor 
of a Con Con, and told them that they could not tell 
voters that blank ballots counted in determining a major- 


ity. 


After the election, the Office of Elections was ready 
to certify the Con Con question as having passed, and the 
Attorney General issued a thorough opinion, stating that 
the question clearly passed. The Attorney General’s opin- 
ion is attached as Exhibit 3. Perhaps most important to 
House Bill 3130, the Chief Elections Officer testified: “It 
would have been impossible for voters to have known prior to 
the election that their blank ballots would count as ballots cast 
in determining whether or not there was a majority in favor of 
the Con Con question.” Mr. Yoshina also testified that: “It is 


PT ee See Pe we ee OTe 


See ee ee ST ee 


228a 


unfair to count the blank Con Con ballots as ballots cast when 
the voters weve specifically told that the blank ballots would 
not count as ballots cast.” Moreover, legal briefs which we 
submitted to various courts established that the Hawaii 
Supreme Court’s decision in this matter was unprece- 
dented, and that no other court had ever held that blank 
ballots counted in determining whether a proposition 
received the majority of ballots cast “upon a question.” 
As the Committee knows, Judge Ezra agreed with our 
position, and found that the election was fundamentally 
unfair. As the Committee is also aware, the Ninth Circuit 
disagreed, but I believe that there is nothing in the Ninth 
Circuit’s opinion which should lead the Committee to 
vote against House Bill 3130, and there is much in the 
opinion which would support the view that a new elec- 
tion ought to be held. 


The Ninth Circuit noted that election “irregularities” 
do not rise tu a federal violation “even if they control the 
outcome of the vote or election.” The Ninth Circuit also 
held that in order for there to be a federal violation there 
had to be “significant disenfranchisement.” The court 
noted that even if the Hawaii Supreme Court’s decision 
“was wrong or contrary to general principles of election 
laws that did not raise a federal question. Perhaps the 
most telling statement in the Ninth Circuit’s decision was 
that: “Hawaii used to interpret voter indifference as a 
reason neither for nor against amending the state consti- 
tution, but now interprets voter indifference as a reason 
not to convene a constitutional convention. This ex post 
clarification of the state’s election laws” does not amount 
to federal violation. While this may be the case, an ex 
post facto change in the rules, which changes the results 


229a 


of an election, give a very strong reason for allowing the 
voters a new election. While I disagree with the Ninth 
Circuit’s view of federal law, there is nothing in the 
opinion which detracts from the proposition that it would 
have been impossible for any voter going to the polls to 
know that his turning in a blank ballot would count as a 
no vote, and there is nothing in the opinion which 
detracts from the proposition that the Hawaii Supreme 
Court changed the rules after the election. 


The House unanimously decided that a new election 
was called for, with the rules known to all. Mr. Yoshina 
testified that the election was unfair, and that it would be 
fair if there were a new election in which the voters knew 
the blank vote rule in advance. We believe that it would 
be the height of cynicism to assert that the voters do not 
have the right to know in advance the meaning of their 
votes. If the Hawaii Supreme Court's retroactive rule 
change had resulted in a result detrimental to those who 
oppose the convention, they would be the first ones 
claiming that they should be entitled to a new fair elec- 
tion, when the rules are known by everyone in advance. 


In court, the State conceded that it woula have been 
impossible for a voter to have known the meaning of his 
blank ballot going into the polling booth, but claimed that 
that did not amount to a federal violation. The Ninth 
Circuit has agreed with the State, stating that this is a 
State issue. There are a great many people who believe 
that if the rules had been known in advance, election 
campaigns could have been tailored to address the issue, 
and to try to combat apathy, by letting voters know quite 
clearly that blank votes would count as no votes. To the 
extent that the voters are led astray as to the meaning of 


230a 


their votes, an election cannot be said to be fundamen- 
tally fair. While the Supreme Court's changing the vote 
counting rules cannot be rectified by the Legislature, the 
unfairness of the process by which people did not know 
the rules can be rectified, by the relatively simply expe- 
dient of putting the question back on the 1998 general 
election ballot. It will cost very little, and while, as stated 
above, it will be difficult for the proponents to prevail, at 
least everyone will know the rules in advance. I would ask 
the committee members to consider one additional question 
when deciding how to vote on this issue. If the election circum- 
stances described above had resulted in the defeat of something 
that they cared passionately about, would not they want a new 
fair election on that issue. 


I truly believe that the question of whether Hawaii 
needs a Constitutional Convention is not germane to the 
issue of whether this question should be put back on the 
1998 ballot. The need for a convention is an issue up to 
the people. I think that is why both newspapers have 
stated in editorials that even though they might not see a 
great need for a convention, there ought to be a new fair 
election, with people knowing all the rules in advance. 
However, there are a great many issues that could be 
taken up by a convention. The issues of initiative, refer- 
endum and recall, which have never been presented to 
the people of Hawaii for a vote, could be considered. The 
victims of crime bill of rights, a part of which was 
recently considered by this Committee, could be consid- 
ered. In fact, in holding the bill, the Committee stated 
that this would be an issue which it believed could very 


well be taken up by a convention, if the voters approved 


23la 


one. Issues relating to Hawaii's economy and tax struc- 
ture could be taken up by a convention. Issues relating to 
the school board could be taken up by a convention. 
Issues relating to State/County relationships and Home 
Rule could be considered. And, issues relating to a great 
many other matters could be taken up by a convention, 
and there are many people with many interests far 
beyond my own who could raise many issues that they 
feel should be considered by a convention. 


Regardless, however, there has been no convention 
since 1978. The people should have a fair opportunity to 
decide whether to have a convention, with everyone 
knowing the rules in advance. I strongly urge you to vote 
for House Bill 3130. 


Very truly yours, 
/s/ Mark J. Bennett 
Mark J. Bennett

Your Committee on Judiciary, to which was referred 
H.B. No. 3130, H.D. 1, entitled: 


“A BILL FOR AN ACT RELATING TO A 
CONSTITUTIONAL CONVENTION” 
begs leave to report as follows: 


The purpose of this bill is to place on the 1998 elec- 
tion ballot the question of whether there shall be a con- 
vention to propose a revision or amendments to the 
Constitution. 


Your Committee finds that there has been an 
immense amount of controversy surrounding the results 
of the vote on the constitutional convention question that 
was placed on the 1996 general election ballot. Your Com- 
mittee further finds that the controversy was focussed 
[sic] on whether blank and spoiled ballots should be 
counted in determining a majority on the convention 
question. 


233a 


Therefore, because of this controversy and the ensu- 
ing debate regarding placing the question before the 
voters at the next general election, your Committee is 
choosing to exercise the power granted in Article XVII, 
section 2 of our state constitution, which allows the legis- 
lature to submit to the electorate at any general election 
the question regarding the convening of a constitutional 
convention. Your Committee further finds that by exercis- 
ing this power, the legislature will restore the peoples’ 
trust in the electoral process and resolve any remaining 
issues regarding the peoples’ right to a fair election that 
some citizens feel was compromised in the 1996 election. 


Testimony in support of this bill was submitted by: 
Let the People Decide; the National Federation of Inde- 
pendent Business; the Citizens for a Constitutional Con- 
vention; and ten private citizens. 


Testimony in opposition to this bill was submitted 
by: the Office of Hawaiian Affairs; American Civil Liber- 
ties Union of Hawaii; Hawaii Women’s Political Caucus; 
Native Hawaiian Legal Corporation; Hawaii Women 
Lawyers; Hawaii State Teachers Association; University 
of Hawaii Professional Assembly; International Long- 
shoremen’s and Warehousemen’s Union Local 142; 
United Food and Commercial Workers Union, Japanese 
American Citizens League; American Friends Service 
Committee; Marriage Project - Hawaii; Hawaii Govern- 
ment Employees Association; Hawaii State AFL-CIO; and 
fourteen private citizens. 


The Office of Elections, Sierra Club, and League of 
Women Voters of Hawaii submitted comments on this 
bill. 


234a 


Upon further consideration, your Committee has 
amended the bill by making non-substantive changes to 
conform with proper drafting style. 


As affirmed by the record of votes of the members of 
your Committee on Judiciary that is attached to this 
report, your Committee is in accord with the intent and 
purpose of H.B. No. 3130, H.D. 1, as amended herein, and 
recommends that it pass Second Reading in the form 
attached hereto as H.B. No. 3130. H.D. 1, S.D. 1, and be 
placed on the calendar for Third Reading. 


Respectfully submitted on 
behalf of the members of the 
Committee on Judiciary,

HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES 
NINETEENTH LEGISLATURE, 1998 
STATE OF HAWAII 


A BILL FOR AN ACT 
RELATING TO A CONSTITUTIONAL CONVENTION 


BE IT ENACTED BY THE LEGISLATURE OF THE 
STATE OF HAWAII: 


SECTION 1. Article XVII, section 2, of the Constitu- 
tion of the State of Hawaii provides that the Legislature 
may submit to the electorate at any general or special 
election the question, “Shall there be a convention to 
propose a revision of or amendments to the Constitu- 
tion?” The purpose of this Act is to provide for the 
submission of such question to be placed on the ballot at 
the general election of 1998. 


SECTION 2. The Legislature hereby submits the 
question to be placed on the ballot as follows: 


“Shall there be a convention to propose a revi- 
sion of or amendments to the Constitution?” 


SECTION 3. This Act shall take effect upon its 
approval.

{MATERIAL DELETED IN PRINTING] 
The Honolulu Advertiser, Sunday, June 28, 1998 


Voters to decide on convention 
Associated Press 


Gov. Ben Cayetano has signed a measure into law 
under which voters will decide in November whether a 
state constitutional convention should be held. 


“While arguments are good on both sides, this issue 
should be decided at the polls by the voters,” Cayetano 
said last week. 


A 1996 vote ended in a legal dispute. More than 
45,000 blank ballots were counted as “no” votes, and the 
issues was defeated. 


A federal judge later ordered a new vote but the 9th 
U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals eventually upheld the 
State's original election results. 


State lawmakers decided this year that a second vote 
would clear up any confusion over where voters stand on 
the issue. 


Earlier this month, a new community group called 
Protect Our Constitution began campaigning against a 
constitutional convention. 


24la 


EXHIBIT 9 
Wednesday, April 29, 1998 Star-Bulletin, 


Con Con question one step away from ballot 


Star-Bulletin staff 


You will get another chance to decide whether the 
state should have a constitutional convention if the gov- 
ernor signs the bill. 


The House yesterday passed the measure to put the 
question on the November ballot when it voted to agree 
with the Senate’s version of the bill. 


There were slightly more yes votes than no votes to 
have a Con Con when the question was on the 1996 
ballot. 


But the state Supreme Court ruled that blank and 


spoiled ballots would count as no votes and the measure 
failed. 


The governor has said he is opposed to a Con Con 
but has also said he would not veto the bill. 


The Legislature has the power under the state Consti- 
tution to call for the question, but it is sending the bill for 
the governor’s signature because the Constitution is not 
clear on whether the governor’s approval is needed.

SUMMARY 


Bennett's cumbersomely-titled and convoluted 
motion should be denied entirely and without further 
ado for three lucid, straightforward reasons.! First, this 
court has issued its mandate, and nothing in Bennett's 


' Defendants-appellants are the Governor, Lieutenant 
Governor, Chief Election Officer, and Office of Elections of 
Hawaii. Plaintiffs-appeliees are Hawaii voters and election- 
issue Organizations. Consistent with this court’s opinion on the 
merits filed March 27 and amended June 23, 1998 (“the 
Opinion”), the parties may be referred to as, respectively, 
“Yoshina” and “Bennett,” and the decision of the Hawaii 
Supreme Court in Hawaii State AFL-CIO v. Yoshina, 935 P.2d 89 
(Haw. 1997) may be referred to as “ Yoshina I.” 


244a 


motion either overcomes the strong policy of ending liti- 
gation or supports the drastic step of recalling the man- 
date. Second, the claim for attorneys’ fees falls far outside 
all known boundaries of the catalyst theory of fee recov- 
ery. Third, the request to vacate the opinion is meritless 
because (a) the case is not moot because the 1998 legisla- 
tion (Act 131) did not completely and irrevocably eradi- 
cate the effects of the alleged violation of due process in 
1996, and (b) Bennett acquiesced in the alleged mooting 
event, the passage of Act 131 in 1998. 


BACKGROUND 


The November 1996 general election in Mawaii 
included a ballot question on whether a constitutional 
convention should be convened. The question was 
included pursuant to the second sentence of Article XVIL 
Section 2 of the Hawaii Constitution (Ex. A), providing 
that the convention question must be submitted to the 
voters no less frequently than every ten years at a general 
election. 


The 1996 vote on the convention question was suffi- 
ciently close that, unprecedently, the blank ballots con- 
trolled the outcome. The Attorney General issued an 
opinion two weeks after the election that the blank ballots 
were not included in the vote count and that the constitu- 
tional convention question had been approved. Four 
months after the election, the Hawaii Supreme Court in 
an original adversary proceeding held that the blank 
ballots were included in the vote count and ordered 
Yoshina to certify that the constitutional convention ques- 
tion had failed (Yoshina I). Yoshina so certified. 


Perm 


245a 


Plaintiff filed suit in federal court in April 1997. The 
primary theory of the complaint was that Yoshina I was 
such an unforeseeably unfair interpretation of Hawaii's 
election laws as to effect a denial of due process. The 
complaint sought the relief of eradicating the result of the 
1996 election and the holding of Yoshina I as applied to 
the 1996 election, either by certifying (precisely contrary 
to the certification effected by Yoshina I) that the constitu- 
tional convention question had been approved in Novem- 
ber 1996 or by requiring an immediate special election to 
re-vote (and substitute for) the November 1996 vote on 
the constitutional convention question. 


Yoshina defended primarily on the ground that there 
was no due process violation and no basis tor federal 
intervention in the State of Hawaii's election affairs. 


On cross Motions for summary judgment, the district 
court ruled in July 1997 in favor of Bennett and ordered 
Yoshina to run a special election - later ordered for 
December 6, 1997 — to re-vote the convention question. 
Yoshina appealed (No. 97-16408). and Bennett cross 
appealed (No. 97-16543). The theory of the cross appeal 
was that the district court, rather than ordering a new 
election, should have 6rdered the convention question 
certified as approved at the 1996 general election. 


This court stayed the district court’s order (Order 
Sept. 10, 1997) and expedited the appeals. The court 
heard argument on January 16, 1998 and issued its opin- 
ion an March 27, 1998. The Opinion entirely reversed the 
summary judgment in favor of Bennett, remanded with 
instructions to enter tudgment in favor of Yoshina, and 
awarded costs to Yoshina. 


246a 


Plaintiffs sought rehearing and rehearing en banc. 
This court denied such relief on June 23, 1998. The judg- 
ment is dated July 1, 1998> 


The Hawaii Legislature convened on January 21, 
1998. The first sentence of Article XVII, Section 2 of the 
Hawaii Constitution provides that, “the legislature may 
submit to the electorate at any general or special election the 
question, ‘shall there be a convention to propose a revi- 
sion of or amendments to the Constitution?’ ” (emphasis 
added) (Ex. A). Acting pursuant to this sentence, the 
Legislature passed H.B. 3130, H.D. 1, S.D. 1 (H.B. 3130) to 
submit the constitutional convention question on the 
November 1998 general election ballot. 


The Governor approved the bill —- now Act 131 — on 
June 22, 1298 (Exs. B, C). The date the Governor approves 
a bill is of course a matter of public record.2
