May 1 H.B. 3130 transmitted to Gover- 
nor (Ex. C) 

June 22 Governor approves H.B. 3130 
(Act 131) (Exs. B, C) 

June 23 Ninth Circuit non-substan- 


tively amends Opinion and 
denies rehearing 


THERE IS NO BASIS FOR RECALLING THE MANDATE 


The judgment and mandate of this court have issued, 
including the ruling that, “Yoshina shall recover his costs 


249a 


on appeal from Bennett.” (Slip Op. at 6305). Certiorari lies 
to the Supreme Court. 


The relief Bennett seeks in his present motion is all 
premised on the theory that this court should exercise its 
inherent authority to recall the mandate. Mandates are 
recalled rarely and only for the most compelling reasons: 


Recall of the mandate “is an extraordinary rem- 
edy which is only granted in cases where it is 
necessary to serve the interest of jus- 


tice.” . . . The decision whether to grant this 
remedy rests entirely within this court’s discre- 
tion. ... We use our power to grant this remedy 


sparingly, in recognition of the rule that “[t]here 
must be special reason, ‘exceptional circum- 
stances,’ in order to override the strong policy 
of repose, that there be an end to litigation.” 


United States v. Feldman, 830 F.2d 134, 135 (9th Cir. 1987) 
(citations omitted). 


Here, there is no reason to recall the mandate. No 
injustice has occurred, much less an injustice requiring 
the exceedingly rare and extraordinary step of recalling 
the mandate. Rather, as set forth below, Bennett is not the 
prevailing party and the case is not moot. 


BENNETT IS NOT THE PREVAILING PARTY 


A. Introduction 


Bennett lost everything that he could conceivably 
lose on appeal to Yoshina. Bennett lost Yoshina’s motion 
for stay pending appeal, thereby losing the December 
1997 special election ordered by the district court. Bennett 
lost Yoshina’s motion to strike the cross appeal “reply” 


250a 


brief. Bennett lost on the merits of Yoshina’s appeal (and 
so lost that the court did not need even to address the 
cross appeal). Finally, Bennett lost on his requests for 
rehearing and rehearing en banc. 


Bennett’s defeat on appeal was absolute, uninter- 
rupted, and total. Now he asks this court for attorneys’ 
fees for appeal on the theory that his unsuccessful lawsuit 
was a catalyst for Act 131, and that therefore he qualifies 
as a prevailing party under 42 U.S.C. § 1988(b).3 


> Bennett also fleetingly attempts to arrogate unto himself 
prevailing party status with the assertion that Act 131 “sharply 
and materially” altered the legal relationship of the Parties 
(Mem. at 2). The Jaw is that a material alteration of the legal 
relationship between Parties occurs only when the plaintiff 
becomes entitled to enforce a judgment, consent decree, or 
settlement against the defendant. Kilgour v. City of Pasadena, 53 
F.3d 1007, 1011 (9th Cir. 1995) (quoting Farrar v. Hobby, 50€ U.S. 
103, 112 (1992). Bennett obtained none of these 


Moreover, the notion that Act 131, if not complied with, 
confers enforcement rights on Bennett makes no sense. His 
enforcement rights would be no greater than those of any other 
citizen, and this would be true not only as to Act 131 but as to 
any other enactment requiring compelled compliance. 


The Ninth Circuit does not interpret Farrar as precluding a 
catalyst theory of fee recovery in appropriate circumstances 
Kilgour, 53 F.3d at 1010. As reported as Kilgour, there is a split 
between the Fourth and the Ninth (and numerous other) circuits 
on this issue. 53 F.3d at 1010. 


Because the Supreme Court seems not to have addressed 
the issue, and because of Bennett's repeated and not-so-veiled 
threat that, if he does not get his way on this fee motion, he will 
seek Supreme Court review (Mot. at 3; Mem, at 3, 13, 18, 20, 44, 
46), Yoshina interposes here the argument that the catalyst 
theory of fee recovery did not survive Farrar. 


251a 


The catalyst concept cannot apply to a fee request in 
the face of a totally adverse litigation outcome and when 
the relief was obtained from non-defendants not acting 
under any legal compulsion and was obtained by pursu- 
ing a separate path of relief that was independently avail- 
able both before and after the lawsuit. . 


And, because the prevailing party question may be 
denied without resolution of factual issues, it is not nec- 
essary either to recall the mandate or to transfer the case 
to the district court for consideration of the fee issue. As 
this court stated in California Ass'n of the Physically Hand- 
icapped, Inc. v. Federal Communications Comm'n, 721 F.2d 
667, 671 (9th Cir. 1983) (CAPH), cert. denied, 469 U.S. 832 
(1984), “Although we would ordinarily remand the case 
to the District Court for a determination of the attorneys’ 
fees issue, we need not remand where resolution of fac- 
tual issues is unnecessary.” (citation. omitted). In CAPH, 
the court determined that the remand was “unnecessary” 
because CAPH could not legally qualify as a prevailing 
party. Id. That reasoning controls here. There is no need 
to set sail on a “second major litigation” about attorneys’ 
fees contrary to the teaching of Hensley v. Eckerhart, 461 
U.S. 424, 437 (1983). : 


B. Act 131 Was Gratuitous And Not Required By Law 


To qualify as a catalytic prevailing party, Bennett 
must prove both that: (1) there is a clear, causal relation- 
ship between the litigation brought and the allegedly 
_ favorable action taken by the litigation defendant; and (2) 
the allegedly favorable action on which the catalyst claim 


252a 


is premised was required by law. See e.g. Sablan v. Director 
of Finance, 856 F.2d 1317, 1324-25 (9th Cir. 1988). 


It is efficient to start the discussion of why Bennett 
fails both prongs of the inquiry with the shorter discus- 
sion of the second prong failure. 


The second prong of this court's catalyst analysis 
traces back to Nadeau v. Helgemoe; 581 F.2d 275, 281 (1st 
Cir. 1978): 


if it has been judicially determined that defen- 
_ dants’ conduct, however beneficial it may be to 
plaintiffs’ interest, is not required by law, then 
defendants must be held to have acted gratu- 
itously and plaintiffs have not prevailed in a 

- legal sense. 


(emphasis added) (cited in CAPH, 721 F2d at 672 and 
Sablan, 856 F.2d at 1327). 


As Sablan elaborated, 


Stated otherwise, “the defendant’s conduct in 
response to the lawsuit must be required by 
law,” . . . to ensure that the government did not 
act “gratuitously in response to a frivolous or 
legally insignificant claim.” 


Sablan, 856 F.2d at 1325 (citations omitted). See also 
CAPH, 721 F.2d at 672 (a plaintiff cannot be a prevailing 
party where a defendant’s action is only gratuitous.) 


Here, Act 131 was gratuitous because, both when it 
Passed the Legislature and when it was approved by the 
Governor, this court had already judicially determined 
that no re-submission of the constitutional convention 
question was legally necessary (at least until 2006, the 


253a 


next ten-year election). The Opinion was the law. Act 131 
was not required by law, because this court had already 
ruled tha: no election was required. 


Neither the fact that Bennett’s longshot rehearing 
petition was pending when the Legislature passed and 
the Governor approved H.B. 3130 nor the fact of Ben- 
nett’s repeated threat to seek Supreme Court review can 
alter the import of the Opinion as a judicial determination 
that submitting the constitutional convention question at 
the November 1998 general election, however beneficial 
that may be to Bennett’s interest, was not required by law.* 


And, because Bennett has failed the second prong of 
the catalyst inquiry, there is no need for the court to 
consider the first prong, which Bennett likewise fails.° 


4 These matters reflect only that Bennett continues to 
litigate after his action “clearly became” groundless. Nadeau v. 
Helgemoe, 581 F.2d at 281 (quoting the discussion in 
Christianburg Garment Co. v. E.E.0.C., 434 U.S. 412, 422 (1978) on 
when attorney fees may be assessed against a plaintiff). 


5 Given the gratuitous nature of the enactment of Act 131, 
this court need not decide whether Bennett’s case was frivolous, 
unreasonable, or groundless when it was filed (although 
Bennett’s core due process argument was rejected as “beyond 
belief”). (Slip Op. at 6303)). 

Bennett’s contention (Mem. at 23) that his case was 
substantial because this court granted extended argument time 
and issued a “lengthy” opinion *s preposterous. The appeal time 
was-“extended” 10 minutes to accommodate the related 
intervention appeals. The time for the merits argument 
remained at the normal 20 minutes (Order Dec. 23, 1997). 
Bennett’s present motion, by itself, disproves any correlation 
between length and meritoriousness. 


254a 


C. Bennett’s Unsuccessful Lawsuit Was Not The Cata- 
lyst For Act 131 


Returning - we believe unnecessarily — to the first 
prong of the catalyst theory, this court has explained; 


However, even in the absence of a favorable 
judgment, a litigant may be a prevailing party 
for purposes of an attorney fee award if his 
action was a “catalyst” which motivated the 
defendant to provide the relief originally sought 
through litigation. 


Beach v. Smith, 743 F.2d 1303, 1306 (9th Cir. 1984) 
(emphasis added) (citing American Constitutional Party v. 
Munro, 650 F.2d 184, 187 (9th Cir. 1981)). 


Thus, to prove the required causal link between his 
lawsuit and Act 133, Bennett must prove that his lawsuit 


(1) motivated; (2) lawsuit defendant to provide; (3) the 
relief originally sought through litigation. Bennett has 
proved none of these three factors. 


1. Clear Causal Relationship 


The pebbles of “proof” that Bennett adduces in sup- 
port of his catalyst claim lack probative value. (1) Bennett 
contends that Act 131 was the result of litigation “pres- 
sure” from his lawsuit (Mem. at 1, 20). But the fact is that 
this court’s Opinion issued March 27, 1998.6 This was 
before H.B. 3130 was either passed by the Legislature on 
April 29 or approved by the Governor on June 22, 1998. 


® Bennett’s five-page “historical accounting” (Hem. at 18) 
contains one oblique reference (at 17) to this court’s Opinion. 


255a 


In a typical catalyst situation, the litigation defendant 
confers all or a portion of the requested relief (even 
without a formal judgment, consent decree, or settle- 
ment), based upon a reasoned assessment of the applica- 
ble law and of the probable litigation outcome and 
damages, including exposure to, liability for attorneys 
fees. When this court issued its Opinion in favor of 
Yoshina, any prior litigation pressure evaporated. At that 
point, the possibilities of rehearing by the unanimous 
panel or en banc or of Supreme Court review and reversal 
could reasonably be assessed as remote indeed.” A favor- 
able ruling from a federal court of appeals is a significant 
legal event, and the party receiving the favorable ruling 
would surely be less motivated after the ruling than 
before to accede to any litigation demands of an oppo- 
nent. 


(2) Yoshina I, requiring the 1996 submission of the con- 
stitutional convention question to be certified as rejected, 
and following an opinion of the Attorney General that the 
question should be certified as approved, did create con- 
fusion and controversy. The Hawaii Legislature disagreed 
with the decision of the Hawaii Supreme Court in Yoshina 
I and reacted against it at the first opportunity.® 


? There is no reason that the Governor, in approving Act 
131, would even be aware of such an ordinary, commonplace 
event in the department of attorney gener=! as an unsuccessful 
litigant seeking rehearing. 


8 The last day for introducing bills in the 1997 Hawaii 
Legislature was January 24, 1997 (Ex. D). Yoshina I was decided 
two months later, when the 1997 Legislature was on the 
homestretch to its May 1 adjournment. 


- 256a 


~e 


Bennett now asserts that his lawsuit “generated” the 
controversy and confusion that the Legislature acted to 
resolve. (Mem. at 15; see also Mem, at 3, 16, 18, 19, 21, 23). 
The House Report contains one reference to the federal 
lawsuit (Pls. Ex. 3 at 1). That single reference does not in 
any way suggest that the lawsuit created the controversy 
and confusion. The Senate Report (Pls. Ex. 5) dated after — 
this court’s Opinion issued does not say one word about 
the lawsuit. 


Contrary to Bennett's bald assertion, his federal law- 
suit did not generate controversy and confusion. The 
controversy and confusion pre-existed the lawsuit and 
arose from the unlikely 1996 election result, with its 
unprecedented controlling bloc of blank ballots, and from 
the Hawaii Supreme Court's decision in Yoshina I. The 
confusion and controversy generated Bennett's lawsuit, 
not the other way around. 


(3) Bennett claims that the unsuccessful lawsuit allowed 
him to present to the Legislature testimony that would 
not otherwise have been available. (Mem. at 14). To sup- 
port this claim, Bennett quotes at length his own testi- 
mony to the House about the alleged unfairness of voters 
not knowing the election ground rules, and about the Fact 
Sheet, internet posting, and alleged opinions: of Chief 
Election Officer Yoshina. (Mem. at 14-15).° There is no

showing that the federal lawsuit was necessary to Ben-

to the Senate following this court’s Opinion. The key 
point he cites from the Opinion — that “Hawaii used to 
interpret voter indifference neither for nor against 
amending the state constitution, but now interprets voter

convention” (Slip. Op. at 6303) — was a fact based on the

(4). Bennett erroneously informs this court that Dwayne 
Yoshina, as Chief Election Officer and representative of 
the Office of Elections, testified in favor of H.B. 3130, “the 
curative legislation.” (Mem. at 3). Yoshina did no such

House and Senate on H.B. 3130.%° 


(5) There is no support for or consequence to Bennett's 
contention that no one in state government took any 
action to remedy the “unfairness” of Yoshina I until after 
Bennett filed his federal lawsuit. (Mem. at 7, 13-14, 21).

First, this court has rejected the due process claim of 
“unfairness.” 


Second, as Previously discussed, Yoshina I was 
decided too late in the 1997 legislative session for respon- 
sive action. Thus, absent a special session, the Legislative 
could not act on Yoshina I until the 1998 legislative ses-

suit. 


Third, there is no basis for the criticisms that the 
Attorney General should have sought reconsideration of 
the unanimous decision of the five-member Hawaii 
Supreme Court in Yoshina I or should have sought review 
by the United States Supreme Court Of an interpretation 
of the Hawaii Constitution by the highest court in Hawaii 
in a case not addressing a federal constitutional question 
(Mem. at 14). These “should-have’s” reflect the mindset 
that every lawsuit must be taken to its most prolonged, 
expensive, and bitter ending. 


Finally, even if Lennett were correct in his hindsight 
about all that the Attorney General failed to accomplish, 
that would not advance his claim about what his lawsuit 
actually did accomplish. 


(6) Bennett’s suggestion that the Governor signed H.B. 
3130 into law to “stop the clock” on further fee liability is 
pure speculation. If the Governor were motivated to 
“stop the clock,” he would have signed on May 1, when 
the bill was transmitted to him (Ex. C), rather than seven 
weeks later on June 22, 1998. And certainly the Governor 
should be allowed to not wait until the last day before 
signing without having his motives impugned. 


259a 


(7) Bennett argues that the submission of the constitu- 
tional convention question in November 1998 under Act 
131 “comes no later” than would have occurred if Bennett 
had prevailed in this court (Mem. at 13). This “what if” 
speculation adds nothing to Bennett’s catalyst claim. 
Indeed, Bennett’s speculation misses the point: the legis- 
lative relief comes no earlier than if Bennett had never 
filed his lawsuit. 


(8) Bennett’s lengthly attempt to distinguish American 
Constitutional Party v. Munro, 650 F.2d 184 (9th Cir. 1981) 
{ACP) is unavailing. Bennett contends that because plain- 
tiffs in ACP lost in the federal district court, “there was 
never the sort of litigation pressure that attended this 
case, where Defendants, as a result of the Plaintiffs’ vic- 
tory in the District Court, bore the risks of proceeding as © 
appellants in the federal judicial system.” (Mem. at 20). 
As long as the suit initiated by ACP was not frivolous, it 
would necessarily impose litigation risks. Yoshina bore 
the same risks of proceeding as a party in the federal 
appellate system, whether as appellant or appellee. Los- 
ing on appeal in either capacity would mean additional 
fee and cost liability. 


What the catalyst claim comes down to, then, is that 
the House Report refers one time to Bennett’s lawsuit in 
the sentence, “Lawsuits were filed in both state and fed- 
eral courts on this issue.” This one vague statement does 
not show a clear causal relationship between the lawsuit 
and the passage of H.B. 3130. 


Finally, while it may be possible to achieve catalytic 
prevailing party status “even in the absence of a favor- 
able judgment,” Beach v. Smith, 743 F.2d at 1306, no court 


260a 


has held that a litigant may be a prevailing party in the 
presence of a totally adverse litigation outcome. To those 
Hawaii citizens under the impression that the 1996 vote 
on the constitutional convention question was unfair or 
that Yoshina I was a maverick decision, H.B. 3130 would 
have been unnecessary if Bennett had succeeded in his 
federal action. H.B. 3130 was “necessary” only because of 
- Bennett's total litigation failure. Bennett is illogically say- 
ing that he is entitled to attorneys’ fees without regard to 
the failure of his case, under the ruse of “heads I win, 
tails you lose.” 


2. “Relief” From Non-defendants 


A lawsuit is a catalyst if it motivates the defendant to 
provide the relief originally sought through litigation. 
Beach, 743 F.2d at 1306 (emphasis added). The focus is on 
“the defendant's conduct in response to the lawsuit... . ” 
and the lawsuit plaintiff must show that the lawsuit 
“prompted defendants to take action,” Sablan, 856 F.2d at 
1325 (9th-Cir. 1988) (emphases added) (citations omitted), 


~ 
a 


Here, there is no showing that Act 131 resulted from 
action by the four litigation defendants — Governor Cay- 
etano, Lieutenant Governor Hirono, Chief Election Offi- 
cer Yoshina, and the Office of Elections. 


The Legislature — not a lawsuit defendant — passed 
H.B. 3130. There is no evidence that any of the lawsuit 
defendants contributed to. the Legislature’s action. As 
noted, Yoshina did not testify in favor of H.B. 3130. No 


26la 


one from the executive branch took any position on H.B. 
3130, much less supported it." 


Governor Cayetano approved H.B. 3130. He was a 
lawsuit defendant only in his official capacity as “the 
official whose duty it is to call for a new election if so 
ordered by the court” (CR 7 (Am. Compl.) at 3 ¥ 8. This 
language of the complaint is taken from Hawaii Revised 
Statutes § 11-174.5 (Ex. A), dealing with original proceed- 
ing election contests in the Hawaii Supreme Court. The 
Governor was not a lawsuit defendant in his capacity as 
the chief executive whose duty it is to approve or veto 
bills under Article III, Section 16 of the Hawaii Constitu- 
tion (Ex. A). 


11 Bennett's lawsuit was vigorously defended because of the 
lack of any legal basis for federal court overthrow of a state

resolve the dispute.

In short, the legislative relief was neither provided 
by the lawsuit defendants nor motivated by the lawsuit, 
which had been decided both when the Legislature 
passed and the Governor approved H.B. 3130. 


3. Different Relief 


The catalyst theory requires the litigation plaintiff to 
prove that defendant was motivated by the lawsuit “to 
provide the relief originally sought through the litiga- 
tion.” Beach, 743 F.2d at 1306 (citing American Constitu- 
tional Party v. Munro, 650 F.2d 184, 187 (9th Cir. 1981). 


Article XVII, Section 2 of the Hawaii Constitution 
(Ex. A) provides two different paths for submitting the 
constitutional convention question to the electorate: 


Section 2. The legislature may submit to the 
electorate at any general or special election the 
question, “Shall there be a convention to pro- 
pose a revision of or amendments to the Consti- 
tution?” If any nine-year period shall elapse 
during which the question shall not have been 
submitted, the lieutenant governor shall certify 
the question, to be voted on at the first general 
election following the expiration of such period. 


Under the first path the Legislature may submit the 
question as often as it pleases, even annually or more 
frequently, by special election. This provision, by itself, 
negates Bennett's claim that Act 131 confers on Hawaii 
citizens an all new “right to vote upon whether to hold a 


263a 


constitutional convention only two years after the preced- 
ing vote on that issue.” (Mem. at 1). The Hawaii Constitu- 
tion conferred that right forty years ago. Bennett’s 
claimed accomplishment is wholly illusory. 


Under the second path, the constitutional convention 
question must be automatically submitted to the electo- 
rate no less frequently than every ten years at a general 
election. sie 2 


The election at issue in Yoshina I and in this federal 
lawsuit was the second path, automatic ten-year election. 
Bennett of course did not need a federal lawsuit to ask 
the Legislature_to submit the question under the first 
path. Yoshina so noted a year ago in the motion for stay 


pending appeal: 


{I]f this court grants a stay pending appeal of 
the district court order requiring a re-vote on 
the constitutional convention question, the stay 
will not prevent plaintiffs and all others favor- 
ing a constitutional convention from lobbying 
the Hawaii Legislature to submit the question to 
the voters in an immediate special election, 
without regard to the outcome of this litigation. 


(Stay Mot. [Aug. 15, 1997] at 32 n. 18). 


The optional legislative trip in 1998 down the first 
path does not entitle Bennett to attorneys’ fees for unsuc- 
cessfully litigating the necessity of relief under the sec- 
ond path. See Rock Creek Ltd. Partnership v. State Water 
Resources Control Bd., 972 F.2d 274, 279-80 (9th Cir. 1992), 
cert. denied, 508 U.S. 949 (1993) (approach to FERC was 
independent avenue and not a condition precedent to 


264a 


partially successful federal court action; fees allowable, if 
at all, only for litigation success). 


4 


The “relief originally sought” in this lawsuit — a 
federal court ruling that the 1996 second. path election 
violated federal constitutional rights and should be 
erased from the record books by re-certification or by 
replacement election — was not the first path “relief 
obtained” and was not a condition precedent to obtaining 
first path relief. 


THERE IS NO MOOTNESS 


“The burden of demonstrating mootness ‘is a heavy 
one.’ ” County of Los Angeles v. Davis, 440 U.S. 625, 631 
(1979), quoting United States v. W.T. Grant Co., 345 U.S. 
629, 632-33 (1953). The heavy burden extends to two 
separate components of mootness, one of which is that 
“interim relief or events have completely and irrevocably 
eradicated the effects of the alleged violations.” Davis, 
440 U.S. at 631 (citations omitted).13 see also In re Printlar 
Corp., 124 F.3d 1310, 1312 (9th Cir. 1997): 


A case is moot only if interim events have “com- 
pletely and irrevocably eradicated the effects of” 
an allegedly improper ruling. . . . The party 


1S Because Yoshina I resolved the question of how blank 
ballots are counted in the vote on whether to convene a 
constitutional convention there is no reasonable expectation 
that the alleged violation - the retroactive springing of a 
unknown vote counting rule on the unsuspecting citizenry — 
will recur. - | : 


| 


265a 


seeking mootness has a heavy burden to estab- — 
lish that there is no effective relief remaining for 
a court to provide. ... 


(citations omitted). 


Here, Bennett cannot meet his heavy burden of show- 
ing that Act 131 has completely and irrevocably eradi- 
cated the alleged violation and that there is no effective 
relief remaining for a court to provide. 


The complaint targeted the 1996 election — the second 
path, automatic ten-year election mandated by the 
Hawaii Constitution — as unconstitutional under the First 
and Fourteenth Amendments (Slip Op. at 6299-6305). 
Bennett sought relief from the alleged constitutional vio- 
lation affecting the 1996 election in the form of either: 


(1) A certification that the constitutional con- 
vention question had been approved. This 
would have required undoing the certification 
issued in response to the Yoshina I the that the 
convention question had been rejected; 


or 


(2) A new election, the results of which would 
supplant and substitute for the certified results 
of the 1996 election. 


Because Bennett failed to prove any constitutional 
violation, he of course obtained no judicial relief against 
the 1996 election. This court’s opinion preserves the elec- 
tion certification mandated by Yoshina I that the conven- 
tion proposal had been rejected. 


The “relief” obtained from the Legislature — an 
optional, first path election in 1998 — does not and cannot 


266a 


eradicate the results of the 1996 election. The 1996 elec- 
tion has not been recertified contrary to Yoshina I and 
does not bear the stigmatic result of “by federal court 
order, result undetermined until a new, fair election is 
held.” The 1996 election result that Bennett challenges as 
unfair will remain in full force and effect, whatever the 
outcome of the 1998 election.14 


The 1998 optional election is not the final consumma- 
tion of the 1996 election. Rather, the 1998 election is a 
different, separate, and additional election. If, for exam- 
ple, the University of Southern California defeats UCLA 
this fall in football, it will not be able, even if it had 
unproven allegations of past recruiting violations, 
thereby to erase the record of last season’s loss. 


Further, it is still possible for a court to provide 
effective relief to Bennett against the alleged unfairness of 
applying the “blank votes do count” rule of Yoshina I to 
the 1996 election. At this juncture, that court is the United 
States Supreme Court. It may provide effective relief 
against the 1996 election first by holding that the election 
result mandated by Yoshina I violated federal constitu- 
tional rights and then by either ordering that the constitu- 
tional convention proposal be re-certified as approved in 


4 Even if the 1998 election results in approving the 
constitutional convention proposal, that would not render 
meaningless the voiding of the certification of the 1996 result. 
An election result can never be meaningless; it has an 
independent legal significance. That is, whether the 1996 result 
is, “approved,” “rejected,” or “by federal court order, result 
undetermined until a new, fair election is held” is a legally 
significant event that would not be rendered meaningless by 
any result of the 1998 vote. 


267a 


1996 or ordering an immediate election on the constitu- 
tional convention question, the vote totals and outcome 
of which will replace the 1996 vote totals and outcome 
certified under mandate of Yoshina I. 


The short of the matter, then, is that: (1) the election 
required by Act 131 cannot completely and irrevocably 
eradicate the effect of the allegedly unfair 1996 election; 
and (2) the Supreme Court may still provide effective 
relief against the allegedly unfair 1996 election by hold- 
ing that a federal constitutional violation has occurred 
and ordering either remedy sought.’ 


A related but distinct reason Act 131 does not even 
partially moot this case is, as previously explained, that 
the election at issue in Yoshina I and in this federal law- 
suit was the second path, automatic ten-year election. The 
1998 election scheduled by Act 131 involves the very 
different first path, legislative option provision. While the 
ballot question is the same either way, the two elections 
are constitutionally different creatures and necessarily 
cannot substitute for each other. A new election ordered 
by a federal court to replace a constitutionally deficient 
1996 vote would be the vote that is mandated by the 
Hawaii Constitution and its requirement that a presuma- 
bly fair vote be held at prescribed intervals. The legisla- 
tive optional election is of a totally different character. It 
is an additional vote, but it is not mandatory, and it does 


i 


5 Bennett’s motion does not address the potential 
effectiveness of a judicial remedy concerning the 1996 
mandatory election if the 1998 optional election results in 
another rejection of the constitutional convention proposal. 


268a 


not serve the purpose of the ten-year submission required 
by the Hawaii Constitution. 


VACATUR IS UNWARRANTED 


Because the case is not moot, there is no basis for the 
court even to consider the extraordinary post-mootness 
step of vacating its judgment. 


in addition, the proper application of the equitable 
doctrine of vacatur would in any event require denial of 
Bennett's request. The touchstone of vacatur is equity. 
Dilley v. Gunn, 64 F.3d 1365, 1370 (9th Cir. 1995) (applying 
U.S. Bancorp Mortgage Co. v. Bonner Mall Partnership, 513 
U.S. 18 (1994). 


A judgment is most likely to be vacated when the 
mootness results from circumstances either unattributa- 
ble to any of the parties (happenstance) or attributable to 
unilateral action of the party who prevailed. U.S. Bancorp, 
513 U.S. at 25. A judgment is least likely to be vacated 
when the party seeking relief from the judgment caused 
the mootness by voluntary action. Id. at 24; See also Dilley 
v. Gunn, 64 F.3d at 1371 (pivotal inquiry under Bancorp is 
whether appellant caused the mootness by voluntary 
action). 


The present situation lies at the voluntary action end 
of the equitable spectrum and is far removed from the 
happenstance end. While Bennett’s unsuccessful lawsuit 
was not the catalyst for H.B. 3130, it is clear from the 
documents submitted with Bennett's motion that he — the 
individual as distinguished from the lawsuit — strongly 
lobbied for passage of H.B. 3130. It is irrelevant whether 


269a 


or not Bennett intended to moot the case. Difley v. Gunn, 
64 F.3d at 1371-72. Under the circumstances it is fair to 
deny the equitable remedy of vacatur. Bennett's ability to 
obtain review of the adverse and allegedly moot Ninth 
Circuit judgment was frustrated not by the “vagaries of 
circumstance,” but by circumstances that he actively 
courted and in which he acquiesced. The equitable con- 
siderations that support the drastic step of vacating a 
judgment are plainly absent here. 


CONCLUSION 


For the reasons set forth in this opposition, Yoshina 
asks the court to deny the claim for attorneys’ fees prem- 
ised an the catalyst theory of prevailing party status and 
to deny the request to vacate the Opinion and judg- 
ment. !© 


Respectfully submitted, 


/s/ Dorothy Sellers 
DOROTHY SELLERS 
Deputy Attorney General

response is necessary.

STATE OF HAWAII 


SSeieienneaeeeeeee

A BILL FOR AN ACT 
RELATING TO A CONSTITUTIONAL CONVENTION. 


BE IT ENACTED BY THE LEGISLATURE OF THE 
STATE OF HAWAII: 


SECTION 1. Article XVII, section 2, of the Constitu- 
tion of the State of Hawaii provides that the Legislature 
may submit to the electorate at any general or special 
election the question, “Shall there be a convention to 
propose a revision of or amendments to the Constitu- 
tion?” The purpose of this Act is to provide for the 
submission of such question to be placed on the ballot at 
the general election of 1998. z 


SECTION 2. The legislature hereby submits the. 
question to be placed on the ballot as follows: 


“Shall there be a convention to propose a revi- — 
sion of or amendments to the Constitution?” ; 


SECTION 3. This Act shall take effect upon its 
approval. 


Approved By The 
Governor on 
Jun 22 1998

802 LEHUA AVENUE

DWAYNE D. YOSHINA 
CHIEF ELECTION OFFICER 


TESTIMONY OF THE 

CHIEF ELECTION OFFICER, OFFICE OF ELECTIONS 

TO THE SENATE COMMITTEE ON JUDICIARY 

ON HOUSE BILL NO. 3130 HD 1 
RELATING TO A CONSTITUTIONAL CONVENTION 
April 9, 1998 

Chair Chumbley, Chair Matsunaga, and Senate Com- 

mittee on Judiciary, thank you for this opportunity to 


testify on House Bill No. 3130 HD 1, on behalf of the 
Office of Elections. 


House Bill No. 3130 HD 1 will impact the Office of 
Elections by increasing our ballot costs by approximately 
$142,709. If the Constitutional Convention question is 
voted upon during the 1998 General Election, we will 
need to print an additional ballot that has not been bud- 
geted for. 


Thank you for this opportunity to testify.

APPENDIX R 
HOUSE JOURNAL - 21st DAY 
THIRD READING 


H.B. No. 3130, HD1: 


Representative Okamura moved that H.B. No. 3130, 
HD1, pass Third Reading, seconded by Representative M. 
Oshiro. 


Representative Kawananakoa offered the following 
amendment to H.B. No. 3130, HD1:- 


“SECTION 1. House Bill 3130 H.D.1 is amended by 
amending line 12 on page 1 to read: 


‘SECTION 3. [This Act shall take effect upon its 
approval] In connection with said election, the office of Elec- 
tions shall provide voters with a description stating, in the 
words of the Hawaii Supreme Court, that “ ‘ballots cast’ 
within the meaning of Article XVII, section 2 of the Hawaii 
Constitution, includes blank ballots and over votes: in other 
words, blank ballots and over votes shall be treated as no votes 
on the question of whether a constitutional convention should 
be convened.” 


‘Section 4. This Act shall take effect upon its approval.” 


Representative Kawananakoa moved that the amend- 
ment be adopted, seconded by Representative Pendleton. 


Representative Kawananakoa rose to speak in sup- 
port of the amendment, stating: 


“Mr. Speaker, the purpose of this amendment is to 
ensure that we don’t end up in the same mess that we are 
in right now. It is very clear. — 


281a 


“As you know, Mr. Speaker, last election a majority of 
the votes cast as ‘yes’ outnumbered the votes cast as ‘no’ 
for the question on calling for a constitutional conven- 
tion. 


“Therefore, my estimation is that the people of 
Hawaii believe that they have spoken and have clearly 
said that they wanted a constitutional convention. 


“The powerful special interest groups that don’t 
want any changes to government that is benefitting them, 
petitioned the Hawaii Supreme Court to overrule the 
people’s mandate by arguing that the blank votes and the 
spoiled votes should be cast and counted as ‘no’ votes, 
Mr. Speaker. The court agreed on technical grounds and 
the people’s will was thwarted. 


“This decision was appealed to the Federal Court and 
Judge Ezra ruled that under the Hawaii State Supreme 
Court's ruling and their decition [sic], the vote was unfair 
~ greatly unfair — because the people didn’t know that the 
blank and spoiled votes would be considered as ‘no’ 
votes. 


“So here we are today, passing this measure calling 
for a constitutional convention, which I am in complete 
support of, but I am concerned because we want to set 
the record straight so we don’t end up in the same mess 
again. 


“This amendment simply calls for a clarification so 
that our Elections Officer will include or otherwise 
inform the public and ensure that they know that when 
they don’t vote, if they give a blank vote, or they over | 


282a 


vote, or they spoil their ballots, that these will be consid- 
ered ‘no’ votes, and with that the people would be able.to 
clearly vote their conscience and understand the ramifica- 
tions of being silent. You notice this in Hawaii: If you're 
not sure, you don’t want to talk ‘stink’, you just don’t Say 
anything. So a lot of people may have just remained 
silent, because they didn’t know it was going to be coun- 
ted as a ‘no’ vote. So we are going to pass this measure 
and many of our constituents, many of the people of 
Hawaii, will still be in the dark. They still won’t know in 
their mind that their silence will be counted as a ‘no’ vote 
on whether or not to have a constitutional convention. 


“I think it is incumbent upon us to set a fair question 
before the people of Hawaii on such an important matter. 
I would encourage ali of my colleagues to do the demo- 
cratic thing: vote ‘yes’ for this amendment. Give the 
people a fair shake, a fair understanding, of what they are 
voting on. 


“Thank you, Mr. Speaker.”
